Table 1
Inclusive AI Adoption and Human-AI Collaboration
1. Q: What is the chapter's central argument about successful AI adoption?
A: Show answer
Successful AI adoption is a people-centered transformation, not merely a technology deployment or automation project. Leaders should involve employees affected by AI throughout the design, implementation, use, and improvement process. Participation helps workers understand the system, express concerns, influence workflow decisions, develop competence, and see how their contributions remain valuable. Excluding employees can undermine trust, create resistance, and reduce the quality of implementation. The chapter argues that human-AI collaboration is more promising than treating employees as obstacles or replacing them indiscriminately. The objective is to create organizational value while respecting employee autonomy, learning, engagement, and well-being.

Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between a technology-led rollout and an employee-inclusive transformation, using a workplace example.

Key point:
- AI adoption succeeds through people, participation, and organizational integration.
- Employees are contributors to transformation, not merely recipients of change.
2. Q: What does it mean for a manager to become AI-savvy without becoming an AI engineer?
A: Show answer
AI-savvy leadership means understanding enough about AI capabilities, limitations, risks, and workflow implications to make informed decisions and guide collaboration. A manager need not write code or train models, but should recognize suitable opportunities, ask meaningful questions, understand how outputs are produced at a high level, and know when human review is needed. The leader should connect AI initiatives to organizational purpose, stakeholder needs, and measurable outcomes. Technical expertise remains necessary within specialist teams; leadership literacy enables managers to bridge those specialists with business users and ensure that technology choices are operationally relevant and responsibly implemented.

Study instruction:
- List five questions a nontechnical manager should be able to ask before approving an AI use case.

Key point:
- AI literacy for leaders is practical judgment, not programming proficiency.
- Managers must understand capability, limitation, risk, and purpose.
3. Q: Why does the chapter caution against assuming AI can think like a human?
A: Show answer
AI can identify patterns, generate outputs, and perform tasks that resemble intellectual work, but the chapter cautions that this does not make it equivalent to human understanding. The systems discussed rely on patterns learned from data and can miss meaning, cultural nuance, context, contradictions, or implied intentions. Humans can interpret social signals and connect ideas through lived experience and contextual reasoning in ways that current AI may not reliably reproduce. Managers who treat an AI response as a complete substitute for human judgment risk misunderstanding customers, employees, or complex decisions. AI output should be evaluated within the context of the task and the consequences of error.

Study instruction:
- Create an example in which a literal AI interpretation could miss the speaker's implied meaning, then identify the human judgment needed.

Key point:
- Pattern generation is not the same as human contextual understanding.
- AI outputs require judgment appropriate to their consequences.
4. Q: How should leaders distinguish AI efficiency goals from the broader objective of organizational transformation?
A: Show answer
Efficiency goals often emphasize speed, cost reduction, or automating as many steps as possible. Transformation is broader: it examines how work, responsibilities, decisions, skills, relationships, controls, and value creation should change. The chapter warns that pursuing hyperautomation without employee feedback can alienate the people whose work is being redesigned. A process may become faster yet less safe, less understandable, or less customer-oriented if human needs are ignored. Leaders should assess both operational gains and effects on autonomy, quality, workload, service, learning, and employee engagement, then redesign the process rather than simply remove human involvement wherever technically possible.

Study instruction:
- Compare a hyperautomation target with a transformation plan that includes employee experience and process quality measures.

Key point:
- Efficiency is one outcome, not the whole transformation objective.
- Process redesign should consider people, quality, risk, and value.
5. Q: What does the software-security example in the chapter illustrate about fully automated work?
A: Show answer
The chapter cites research in which Copilot produced 1,692 software programs without human coder input, and 40% reportedly contained critical security flaws. The example illustrates that automated output can be productive while still introducing serious weaknesses. It does not establish that every AI-generated program is insecure or that the result generalizes to every tool and task; it shows why human review, testing, security analysis, and accountability cannot be removed merely because generation is automated. Leaders should define review standards, risk-based testing, and responsibility for approving AI-assisted work before deployment, particularly where failures could harm users or expose sensitive data.

Study instruction:
- Design a review workflow for AI-generated code that includes testing, security checks, ownership, and approval before release.

Key point:
- Automation can create quality and security risks alongside productivity gains.
- Human verification remains essential for consequential outputs.
6. Q: How can AI adoption reduce employee autonomy and create cynicism?
A: Show answer
Employees may feel that decisions are being made by an opaque system while they are expected to comply without understanding or influence. When a worker cannot explain an algorithmic decision to a customer, challenge an error, or exercise professional judgment, the role may feel diminished. The chapter's credit-card example shows how an employee's helplessness in explaining an automated rejection can produce poor service and disengagement. Cynicism can grow when management treats AI as unquestionable or presents automation as a purely technical improvement while disregarding employee experience. Leaders should provide transparency, escalation options, participation, training, and defined human authority where appropriate.

Study instruction:
- Identify a workflow where an employee could lose discretion and propose a mechanism to restore meaningful judgment or escalation.

Key point:
- Opaque automation can weaken autonomy and professional confidence.
- Explainability, support, and discretion can improve employee experience.
7. Q: Why can automating routine tasks while leaving difficult human tasks behind negatively affect employee well-being?
A: Show answer
Automation may remove repetitive or relatively manageable activities while leaving people with the most complex, emotionally demanding, or exception-heavy work. If the remaining workload becomes more intense, workers may experience greater strain even when productivity rises. The chapter cites a study suggesting that increasing workplace automation was associated with worse employee health and job satisfaction in the studied context. That finding should be interpreted as evidence of a possible effect, not a universal causal rule. Managers should examine workload distribution, task difficulty, pace, break opportunities, staffing, and support rather than assuming automation automatically improves the quality of human work.

Study instruction:
- Map which tasks are automated and which remain, then assess workload, emotional demand, and capacity of the remaining human role.

Key point:
- Automation can concentrate difficult work on people rather than simply reduce workload.
- Employee well-being must be measured after process redesign.
8. Q: Why do employees sometimes resist AI even when a system appears technically effective?
A: Show answer
Resistance may reflect uncertainty, fear of job loss, loss of control, inadequate understanding, distrust, or a preference for human interaction rather than irrational opposition. Employees may also have legitimate concerns about fairness, errors, workload, privacy, safety, or how performance will be evaluated. The chapter advises leaders to understand the emotional and organizational reasons for resistance instead of dismissing it. Listening to objections can reveal design problems and improve implementation. Leaders should communicate purpose and limits, provide training, invite feedback, and demonstrate how employees can influence the process and receive help when the system fails.

Study instruction:
- Categorize employee concerns into skill, job security, control, fairness, privacy, workload, and reliability, and plan a response to each.

Key point:
- Resistance often signals an unresolved concern or poor implementation design.
- Listening and support are part of adoption, not obstacles to it.
9. Q: What role should employee training and support play in an inclusive AI rollout?
A: Show answer
Training should help employees understand what the system can do, what it cannot reliably do, how to use it in their actual work, how to recognize errors, and where to seek assistance. Support must be available beyond an initial workshop because confidence develops through practice, feedback, and changing use cases. The chapter emphasizes mediation and facilitation of human-AI interaction, including access to a human when automated interactions go wrong. A training plan should therefore include realistic examples, protected learning time, guidance, escalation routes, and opportunities to suggest improvements. Attendance alone is insufficient; leaders should assess practical competence and confidence.

Study instruction:
- Build a training plan with practice tasks, error-recognition exercises, support contacts, and a follow-up competence check.

Key point:
- Training and human support enable meaningful, safer use of AI.
- Learning must continue as the workflow and system evolve.
10. Q: What lessons does the Amazon algorithmic-supervision example offer for AI-enabled management?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes workers whose productivity was monitored by algorithms, with demanding targets, limited breaks, and potentially indiscriminate dismissal when targets were missed. The example warns that algorithmic management can create safety, dignity, and fairness problems when metrics dominate and workers have little control or recourse. Performance measurement must not be designed solely around speed or output counts. Leaders should test targets for realism, consider human variation, protect break and safety requirements, provide appeal mechanisms, and monitor injury, stress, and turnover. A system that increases measured productivity while degrading people or quality may be an organizational failure rather than a success.

Study instruction:
- Review an algorithmic performance system and identify safeguards for safety, fairness, breaks, appeal, and metric manipulation.

Key point:
- Algorithmic supervision needs human safeguards and accountable decisions.
- Productivity metrics should not override safety, fairness, or dignity.
11. Q: How can AI adoption create organizational silos between technical and business teams?
A: Show answer
AI expertise may be concentrated in technology departments while operational, HR, marketing, finance, or program teams lack the knowledge or access needed to apply it. This separation can lead to technical solutions that do not fit real workflows and business teams that cannot influence system design. The chapter argues that AI adoption should cross disciplinary boundaries. Technical staff need to understand business objectives and operational realities, while business users need enough literacy to communicate requirements and evaluate outcomes. Collaboration should be built into project governance, staffing, meetings, decision-making, and feedback loops rather than left to informal cooperation.

Study instruction:
- Identify the roles needed for a proposed AI project and map how each role contributes from discovery through evaluation.

Key point:
- AI projects require sustained collaboration between technical and business expertise.
- Silos reduce the ability to implement and scale useful solutions.
12. Q: Why can data ownership and access become a source of conflict during AI adoption?
A: Show answer
AI relies on data, but departments may own separate repositories, have different policies, or be reluctant to share information. Data sharing can also be constrained by privacy, security, legal obligations, quality concerns, or legitimate stewardship responsibilities. The chapter identifies data ownership and access as potential sources of silo behavior, while emphasizing organizational solutions and collaboration. Leaders should establish common governance, clarify permitted access, define stewardship and accountability, and create secure ways to share relevant information. Centralization or cloud tools may help in some situations, but they must be evaluated against confidentiality, security, regulatory, and operational requirements rather than adopted automatically.

Study instruction:
- Create a data-sharing decision checklist covering purpose, owner, quality, access rights, privacy, security, and retention.

Key point:
- Data collaboration requires governance and trust, not simply technical access.
- Sharing decisions must respect protection and stewardship obligations.
13. Q: Why might AI's effects differ across departments and create territorial behavior?
A: Show answer
AI may benefit some teams by improving their work while threatening or changing tasks in others. Differences in perceived benefit, workload, job security, control, expertise, or visibility can lead departments to protect their own resources and information. The chapter notes that uneven effects can encourage siloed behavior and discourage collaboration. Leaders should openly assess how roles and incentives change across teams, involve affected groups in decisions, and avoid presenting a single uniform benefit story. Shared goals, transparent criteria, and cross-functional governance can reduce competition over data, expertise, or credit and make the transformation more equitable and coordinated.

Study instruction:
- Compare the likely impacts of one AI initiative on three departments and identify the differing concerns and benefits.

Key point:
- Uneven AI impacts can create internal competition and resistance.
- Leaders should address department-specific concerns transparently.
14. Q: What operational problems can result when departments develop AI solutions independently?
A: Show answer
Independent development can duplicate tools and effort, produce inconsistent data, create incompatible workflows, underutilize resources, and prevent organization-wide learning. Teams may collect and manage data separately, reducing the completeness and reliability of information available for broader analysis. The chapter argues that siloed AI adoption limits scale and interdisciplinary problem-solving. A practical response is to establish shared architecture and governance where appropriate, reusable components, common standards, cross-team reviews, and a mechanism for teams to exchange lessons. Central coordination should still allow local experimentation when it is controlled and aligned with organizational objectives.

Study instruction:
- Identify duplicated AI capabilities or datasets in an organization and propose a coordination mechanism that avoids unnecessary central bureaucracy.

Key point:
- Siloed AI development creates duplication and weakens data and learning quality.
- Shared standards and collaboration can improve scalability.
15. Q: What are the four practices the chapter recommends for a more effective and inclusive AI model?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends creating space and time for social connection, making technology and nontechnology teams collaborate, constantly developing leadership skills, and rewarding workers for being human. Together, these practices address isolation, siloed expertise, leadership capability, and perceived loss of value. They are not isolated activities: social connection supports feedback, cross-functional collaboration improves design, leadership humility sustains trust, and recognition reinforces the contribution of people in human-AI workflows. An organization should translate the four practices into responsibilities, routines, resources, and measurable behaviors rather than treat them as slogans or a one-time culture campaign.

Study instruction:
- Design one practical initiative for each of the four practices and assign an owner and review indicator.

Key point:
- Inclusion depends on social connection, collaboration, leadership learning, and recognition.
- The four practices reinforce one another.
16. Q: Why should organizations deliberately create social connection for employees working with AI?
A: Show answer
AI-intensive work can increase time spent interacting with screens and automated systems, reducing opportunities for human contact. The chapter discusses concern about isolation and recommends events, communities, meetings, and regular human interaction. Social connection helps employees share experiences, raise questions, learn from others, and maintain relationships that support cooperation. It can also provide a forum for discussing system problems and work redesign. Social activities should not be superficial substitutes for meaningful communication; the aim is to create practical opportunities for connection, collaboration, and support within the work itself and across the organization.

Study instruction:
- Plan recurring human interaction opportunities for a team using AI heavily, specifying how they will support learning and problem-solving.

Key point:
- AI-enabled work should not unintentionally isolate employees.
- Human connection supports learning, feedback, and engagement.
17. Q: What can the Land O'Lakes example teach leaders about employee-inclusive AI transformation?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Land O'Lakes' AI transformation in commodity forecasting and propensity modeling, where leaders communicated with employees about challenges and possibilities, coordinated across departments, and conducted weekly people-to-people check-ins with business units. Employees could voice concerns, question tactics, and discuss issues. The example illustrates that transformation can be supported by regular dialogue, shared understanding, and visible management attention rather than a rollout announcement alone. Leaders should adapt the practice to their context by scheduling recurring listening sessions, tracking concerns, providing feedback on what changed, and ensuring that participation is meaningful rather than ceremonial.

Study instruction:
- Adapt a weekly check-in model for an AI project and define how feedback will be logged, acted on, and communicated back.

Key point:
- Regular dialogue helps employees understand and influence AI transformation.
- Participation is stronger when feedback receives a visible response.
18. Q: How should technology and nontechnology teams work together during AI adoption?
A: Show answer
Cross-functional teams should combine business knowledge, technical expertise, HR support, operational experience, and relevant risk or governance roles. Business experts clarify objectives and constraints; technical specialists assess systems and implementation; HR helps address skills, roles, and training; operational staff identify workflow integration and real-world consequences. The chapter emphasizes that successful human-AI collaboration crosses disciplines. The team should jointly define use cases, test assumptions, evaluate results, and design the workflow. Collaboration should continue after launch so that technical performance and business impact are assessed together and changes are informed by users' experience.

Study instruction:
- Build a project responsibility matrix for an AI use case and identify how each discipline participates in decisions and testing.

Key point:
- Cross-disciplinary design aligns technical capabilities with real business work.
- Collaboration should continue through implementation and optimization.
19. Q: Why is a common language important when technical and business teams collaborate on AI?
A: Show answer
Different teams may use terms, assumptions, and success criteria that others do not understand. Without a common language, business leaders may struggle to communicate needs and technical teams may withdraw into specialist discussions, creating mistrust and poor alignment. The chapter recommends establishing shared understanding of how teams approach challenges, recognize patterns, break problems into smaller tasks, and work together. A common language does not mean removing technical precision; it means explaining concepts in terms that support joint decisions. Teams should define key terms, translate objectives into testable requirements, and check that everyone understands trade-offs and responsibilities.

Study instruction:
- Create a glossary for one AI project containing technical terms, plain-language definitions, business implications, and decision points.

Key point:
- Shared vocabulary enables collaboration without erasing specialist expertise.
- Miscommunication can weaken AI project alignment.
20. Q: What does the CEO and CTO anecdote illustrate about leadership communication during AI transformation?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's anecdote, a CEO interrupted a CTO's technology strategy presentation and demanded a simplified explanation, embarrassing the CTO and contributing to a retreat by the technical team. The story illustrates how dismissive communication and a lack of AI literacy can damage credibility and collaboration. Leaders should ask clarifying questions respectfully, learn enough to engage with technical ideas, and help translate between specialist and business audiences. Simplification is useful when it improves understanding, but it should not humiliate experts or suppress important complexity. Communication must preserve respect and encourage constructive exchange across disciplines.

Study instruction:
- Rewrite a dismissive executive response into questions that clarify technical implications while preserving the expert's dignity.

Key point:
- Leaders must translate technical information without devaluing expertise.
- Respectful curiosity strengthens collaboration and credibility.
21. Q: What does the CNH Industrial example demonstrate about cross-functional AI adoption?
A: Show answer
CNH Industrial's initiative involved commercial vehicle units, industry-specific vehicle units, IT, and operations, along with digital advisers and a digital team supporting strategy, implementation, and execution. Cross-disciplinary participation allowed the organization to adopt or retire experiments quickly, lower barriers between developers and business owners, and provide real-time feedback. The example shows how AI transformation can be organized around collaboration and iterative learning rather than a narrow technology department. Leaders can apply the lesson by creating teams with operational authority, feedback access, and clear decision processes so experiments are evaluated against actual business needs.

Study instruction:
- Identify which functions would need to collaborate in an AI initiative in your organization and how they would make decisions together.

Key point:
- Cross-functional structure and feedback can accelerate learning and implementation.
- AI experiments should be evaluated with business and operational participation.
22. Q: Why should leaders continuously develop their own leadership skills during AI adoption?
A: Show answer
AI transformation creates uncertainty, new work arrangements, and concerns that leaders may not have encountered before. The chapter emphasizes listening, humility, openness, and the ability to guide employees through discomfort. Leadership development should therefore be ongoing, including learning about AI, communicating its limits, receiving feedback, managing change, and facilitating collaboration. A leader who assumes that past success or authority is sufficient may resist new ideas or discourage questions. Continuous learning helps leaders adapt their behavior and make the transformation more responsive to employees and organizational realities.

Study instruction:
- Set a leadership learning plan that combines AI literacy, employee feedback, communication practice, and reflection on decisions.

Key point:
- AI transformation requires leaders to learn and adapt continuously.
- Leadership behavior can either support or undermine inclusion.
23. Q: How does humility in leadership influence employee trust in AI adoption?
A: Show answer
Humility signals that leaders are willing to learn, acknowledge uncertainty, and receive feedback rather than treating their position as proof that they are correct. The chapter links openness and humble leadership to employees' willingness to trust and engage with AI. When leaders admit what they do not know and invite different perspectives, employees may feel safer raising concerns and sharing practical knowledge. Humility does not mean indecision or abandoning accountability; it means making decisions with evidence and learning from others. Leaders should demonstrate it through listening, asking genuine questions, acknowledging mistakes, and explaining how feedback influences decisions.

Study instruction:
- Identify three observable behaviors that demonstrate humble leadership during a technology change and three behaviors that undermine it.

Key point:
- Humility and openness can support employee trust and participation.
- Listening must be accompanied by accountable decisions.
24. Q: Why do employees need a mental model of both their own capabilities and those of AI systems?
A: Show answer
Human-AI collaboration requires understanding where human judgment, contextual knowledge, empathy, and accountability complement machine capabilities, as well as where AI may be unreliable. Employees who lack a mental model may either overtrust AI or reject it without evaluating its usefulness. The chapter argues that people need frameworks for thinking about how to work with smart machines and should understand their own strengths and weaknesses alongside those of the tools. This understanding supports appropriate reliance, error recognition, task allocation, and escalation. Training should therefore explain capability boundaries and practice decisions about when to use, verify, or disregard AI output.

Study instruction:
- Create a human-versus-AI task map for a workflow, showing tasks, strengths, weaknesses, review points, and accountability.

Key point:
- Effective collaboration depends on understanding complementary strengths and limits.
- Mental models help users calibrate trust and reliance.
25. Q: What does the aviation-autopilot example teach about training for human-AI collaboration?
A: Show answer
The chapter cites the need for pilots to understand both aircraft systems and flight automation so they can manage situations that may become dangerous. The lesson is that automation does not eliminate the need for expertise; it can make expertise more important when systems behave unexpectedly or require intervention. Employees need a mental model of the AI system, its limits, and the human responsibilities remaining in the workflow. Training should include realistic failure and handoff scenarios, not only routine use. The analogy should be applied carefully across industries, but it reinforces the importance of competent oversight and preparedness for exceptions.

Study instruction:
- Design a simulation in which AI fails or produces an unexpected result and users must recognize, intervene, and escalate appropriately.

Key point:
- Automation requires trained human oversight and contingency skills.
- Users should practice handling failures, not only normal operation.
26. Q: How can leaders reward employees for their uniquely human contributions in AI-enabled work?
A: Show answer
Leaders should make clear how human judgment, creativity, context, relationship management, problem-solving, review, and responsibility contribute to the value created with AI. Recognition can include formal rewards, career development, performance criteria, opportunities to lead improvement, and visible acknowledgment. The chapter warns that employees may feel their contributions are downplayed when success is attributed primarily to AI. Reward systems should therefore reflect collaboration and the quality of human contribution, not just output volume. Recognition must be fair and evidence-based, and should avoid rewarding unsafe shortcuts, excessive reliance on AI, or inflated claims about individual impact.

Study instruction:
- Propose performance criteria that recognize human-AI collaboration, quality, judgment, learning, and responsible use.

Key point:
- Recognition reinforces the value of human contribution to AI outcomes.
- Reward design should support quality and responsibility, not only speed.
27. Q: Why is it important to clarify who receives credit for outcomes produced through human-AI collaboration?
A: Show answer
When contributions are unclear, employees may believe that AI is receiving recognition for work they performed, or managers may overlook the judgment and review that made an output reliable. The chapter suggests establishing guidelines for credit and celebrating human contributions. Clarification supports motivation, accountability, and fairness. It should also distinguish the contributions of employees, tools, teams, and external vendors where appropriate. Recognition should be linked to transparent criteria, evidence of contribution, and responsibility for quality. This reduces ambiguity and helps employees understand how their role is valued in the new workflow.

Study instruction:
- Draft a recognition and attribution policy for AI-assisted work that specifies evidence, team contribution, review, and responsibility.

Key point:
- Fair attribution supports motivation and accountability.
- Human contributions should be visible in AI-enabled outcomes.
28. Q: How should employee participation be built into the AI project lifecycle rather than limited to consultation?
A: Show answer
Participation should influence the definition of the problem, selection of use cases, design of workflow, testing, implementation, training, monitoring, and improvement. Consultation that occurs only after a decision is made may be perceived as symbolic. The chapter's examples show leaders involving workers in the transformation, communicating regularly, and using feedback to adjust implementation. A lifecycle approach gives employees clear opportunities to contribute, with decisions and feedback documented and returned to participants. Leaders should also clarify which decisions are open to influence, which constraints are fixed, and how unresolved concerns are escalated.

Study instruction:
- Create an employee participation plan for each AI project phase, including feedback channels and decision rights.

Key point:
- Meaningful participation influences decisions throughout the lifecycle.
- Feedback should be acted on or answered transparently.
29. Q: What governance arrangements can make employee-inclusive AI adoption practical and accountable?
A: Show answer
Governance should define project ownership, decision rights, employee representation, technical and operational review, risk assessment, feedback channels, escalation, and monitoring. It should also clarify how privacy, security, safety, quality, job impacts, and policy constraints are assessed. The chapter does not prescribe a formal governance template, but its inclusion principles imply that participation and collaboration must be operationalized. Leaders can establish a cross-functional steering group, named workflow owners, regular check-ins, issue logs, and approval criteria. Governance should be proportionate to the use case and avoid turning participation into a bureaucratic obstacle or a token activity.

Study instruction:
- Draft a governance charter with owners, decision rights, employee input, escalation, and review cadence for an AI use case.

Key point:
- Inclusion becomes actionable when roles, processes, and accountability are defined.
- Governance should be proportionate and responsive to risks.
30. Q: How should leaders measure whether AI adoption is creating value without neglecting employee experience?
A: Show answer
Measures should include process performance and human outcomes. Operational indicators may cover quality, cycle time, cost, error rates, customer outcomes, and use-case effectiveness, while employee measures may include confidence, autonomy, workload, training effectiveness, engagement, safety, and ability to raise concerns. The chapter emphasizes employee inclusion, well-being, and collaboration alongside organizational value. Measurement should establish a baseline, monitor trends, and interpret results in context because productivity improvements can coexist with deteriorating work conditions. Leaders should use metrics to identify issues and adjust the workflow rather than to punish individuals or claim success from a single indicator.

Study instruction:
- Build a balanced AI adoption scorecard with operational and employee indicators, baseline, owner, and review date.

Key point:
- AI success should be evaluated across organizational and human outcomes.
- Balanced measurement helps reveal hidden costs or unintended effects.
31. Q: How can leaders build trust without asking employees to accept AI uncritically?
A: Show answer
Trust should be earned through transparent communication, evidence of reliability, appropriate safeguards, employee participation, and the ability to challenge or escalate decisions. The chapter encourages leaders to support employee confidence and understanding, but trust should not become unquestioning acceptance. Employees need to know what the system is used for, its limitations, how outputs are checked, and what happens when errors occur. Leaders should invite scrutiny, explain decisions, and respond to concerns. Trust is better calibrated when users understand the system and retain appropriate control than when management relies on persuasion or promises alone.

Study instruction:
- Design a trust-building communication plan that explains use, limitations, review, escalation, and employee feedback.

Key point:
- Trust should be informed, calibrated, and supported by safeguards.
- Employees need the right to question and verify AI outputs.
32. Q: How should an AI-enabled customer service workflow handle a decision that the employee cannot explain?
A: Show answer
The employee should not be expected to defend an opaque decision without access to information or authority to escalate. The workflow should provide a clear explanation where feasible, identify the decision owner, allow human review, and offer a way to correct errors or provide additional information. The chapter's credit-card anecdote shows how lack of explanation can undermine service and employee motivation. Leaders should design customer-facing processes with escalation, communication guidance, and training so staff can acknowledge uncertainty honestly and obtain help. The organization should also review recurring unexplained decisions as a potential system or policy issue.

Study instruction:
- Create a customer-service escalation script and workflow for AI decisions that cannot be explained by frontline staff.

Key point:
- Frontline employees need explanation, authority, and escalation support.
- Unexplained automated decisions should trigger review, not helplessness.
33. Q: How can leaders respond when employees feel AI is being introduced mainly to replace them?
A: Show answer
Leaders should acknowledge the concern rather than dismiss it, explain the purpose and expected changes honestly, and involve employees in identifying how tasks and roles may evolve. They should clarify what is known, what remains uncertain, how decisions will be made, and what support or learning opportunities are available. The chapter argues that inclusion and human-AI collaboration are more promising than excluding workers. Leaders should avoid assurances that cannot be substantiated and instead provide transparent updates, practical training, role discussions, and channels for employees to influence implementation. Where employment impacts are real, they should be addressed through appropriate organizational processes and commitments.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a change communication plan that distinguishes confirmed facts, uncertainties, employee participation, and support measures.

Key point:
- Job-related concerns require honesty, participation, and tangible support.
- Avoid false reassurance or communication that minimizes legitimate uncertainty.
34. Q: What is the difference between inclusion and merely asking employees for feedback during an AI rollout?
A: Show answer
Feedback collection is one activity; inclusion means employees have meaningful opportunities to influence decisions and understand how their input is considered. A survey or meeting that produces no response may increase cynicism. Inclusive practice involves accessible channels, psychological safety, timely feedback, clear decision boundaries, and visible action or explanation. The chapter's examples emphasize participation, check-ins, cross-team collaboration, and openness. Leaders should track concerns, assign owners, communicate outcomes, and revisit unresolved issues. Inclusion also requires considering employees who may be affected but are less able to participate because of workload, location, language, status, or accessibility barriers.

Study instruction:
- Evaluate a feedback process and identify evidence that employee input changed, clarified, or informed decisions.

Key point:
- Inclusion is demonstrated by influence and follow-through, not feedback collection alone.
- Participation must be accessible and meaningful.
35. Q: How should an organization balance centralized data or AI infrastructure with local team autonomy?
A: Show answer
Central coordination can reduce duplication, improve consistency, and support secure sharing, while local teams may understand their workflows, constraints, and users better. The chapter mentions centralized data and cloud tools as possible solutions to siloed data, but the broader lesson is to remove barriers to collaboration. Leaders should evaluate whether centralization improves quality, access, governance, and reuse without creating excessive dependency or ignoring local needs. Shared standards, stewardship, access controls, and federated decision arrangements may be preferable to a single rigid model. The appropriate design depends on organizational scale, risk, capability, and data requirements.

Study instruction:
- Compare centralized, federated, and locally managed arrangements against collaboration, control, responsiveness, and risk.

Key point:
- Coordination should reduce silos while preserving legitimate local knowledge.
- Infrastructure choices must fit governance and operational needs.
36. Q: How can AI transformation leaders prevent the technology team from becoming isolated from organizational decision-makers?
A: Show answer
Leaders should create recurring communication and decision forums where technical experts can explain capabilities, limitations, dependencies, and risks in terms relevant to business objectives. They should invite operational and business representatives to contribute requirements and feedback, and ensure that technical teams are treated respectfully. The chapter's CEO-CTO anecdote illustrates the damage caused when a leader dismisses or fails to understand technical expertise. Translation should be mutual: specialists explain technical concepts, while business teams explain purpose and constraints. Shared ownership and decision processes help prevent retreat into separate domains.

Study instruction:
- Design a monthly technical-business review that includes a plain-language decision summary, technical risks, business needs, and open questions.

Key point:
- Leadership must bridge technical and business communication.
- Respectful dialogue prevents expert isolation and weak alignment.
37. Q: What risks arise when AI adoption is evaluated only by the speed or cost of automated output?
A: Show answer
A narrow speed or cost measure can conceal errors, safety issues, customer dissatisfaction, reduced autonomy, overload, disengagement, data problems, or the need for expensive human correction. The chapter's examples show that automation may generate insecure code, produce unexplained decisions, intensify work, or create resistance when employees are excluded. Evaluation should include quality, reliability, human workload, service, safeguards, and long-term organizational capability. Leaders should also examine whether the system shifts difficult work to people or creates new dependencies. The apparent efficiency of a process should be tested against its full costs and outcomes.

Study instruction:
- Identify hidden costs that a simple automation-time metric would miss and design indicators to detect them.

Key point:
- Speed and cost are incomplete measures of AI value.
- Quality, safety, people, and long-term effects must also be evaluated.
38. Q: How can an organization identify and address AI-related employee isolation after implementation?
A: Show answer
Leaders can examine the frequency and quality of human interaction, access to colleagues and supervisors, opportunities to discuss AI-related difficulties, and whether teams have shared learning forums. The chapter suggests social events, online communities, weekly meetings, and people-to-people check-ins. If employees are isolated, leaders should redesign work routines so interaction is part of the process rather than an optional activity. They should consider remote and frontline workers, shift patterns, and the accessibility of support. Measures should focus on meaningful connection and collaboration, not simply attendance at social events.

Study instruction:
- Assess a team's communication network and propose recurring peer, manager, and cross-functional interaction opportunities.

Key point:
- Social connection should be intentionally designed into AI-enabled work.
- Support routines help employees learn and share problems.
39. Q: What should leaders do when AI adoption creates conflicting interests between employees, technical teams, and management?
A: Show answer
Leaders should identify the interests, constraints, and evidence behind each position, then create a forum for discussing trade-offs and shared objectives. Employees may prioritize safety, autonomy, or job impact; technical teams may prioritize reliability and feasibility; management may prioritize performance or cost. These interests are not necessarily incompatible, but they need explicit consideration. The chapter recommends cross-disciplinary collaboration and an inclusive mindset. A structured decision process should document concerns, alternatives, safeguards, decision criteria, responsibilities, and unresolved issues. Where policy or legal obligations constrain choices, those limits should be explained clearly and respected.

Study instruction:
- Facilitate a case discussion among three stakeholder groups and produce a decision record that reflects their concerns and the agreed trade-offs.

Key point:
- AI transformation involves legitimate competing interests that need transparent decisions.
- Cross-functional dialogue can align trade-offs with organizational purpose.
40. Q: How should leaders pilot an AI workflow while keeping employees involved and protected?
A: Show answer
A pilot should define its purpose, scope, success criteria, affected roles, risks, safeguards, training, feedback channels, and review schedule before deployment. Employees should help identify realistic use cases and test the workflow. The pilot should include monitoring for quality, safety, workload, customer impact, errors, and unintended effects, with a clear process for pausing or changing the system. Leaders should communicate what the pilot can and cannot establish, and avoid treating early results as proof of universal success. The chapter supports iterative collaboration and real-time feedback, which can help organizations adopt or retire experimental approaches responsibly.

Study instruction:
- Draft a pilot plan with baseline measures, employee involvement, testing, review gates, stop criteria, and feedback responsibilities.

Key point:
- Controlled pilots support learning when participation and safeguards are built in.
- Early experimentation should inform decisions rather than guarantee rollout.
41. Q: How can leaders ensure AI adoption respects human dignity while still pursuing organizational performance?
A: Show answer
Respecting dignity means considering employees as people with expertise, needs, rights, relationships, and responsibilities rather than treating them as interchangeable inputs. The chapter emphasizes autonomy, social connection, participation, recognition, and well-being while also discussing organizational value. Leaders should establish fair processes, avoid dehumanizing surveillance, provide support and meaningful roles, and ensure that AI does not remove accountability or recourse. Performance goals should be balanced with safety, quality, and human outcomes. Dignity is not a substitute for performance management; it is a condition that helps make sustainable performance and responsible transformation possible.

Study instruction:
- Review an AI-enabled workplace practice against performance objectives and human dignity criteria, and identify changes needed.

Key point:
- Sustainable AI adoption must value people as contributors and stakeholders.
- Performance and human dignity should be designed together.
42. Q: What practical sequence can a leader follow to start an employee-inclusive AI transformation?
A: Show answer
Begin by developing foundational AI literacy and explaining the organizational purpose. Identify affected workflows and employees, then convene a cross-functional team to define needs, constraints, risks, and possible use cases. Involve workers in design and testing, provide training and support, and establish feedback and escalation channels. Pilot the workflow with balanced measures for quality, performance, and employee experience. Review results, address concerns, recognize human contributions, and decide whether to refine, scale, or retire the initiative. Maintain communication and leadership learning throughout rather than treating adoption as a one-time event.

Study instruction:
- Turn this sequence into a project roadmap with deliverables, owners, employee participation, and decision gates.

Key point:
- AI transformation is iterative and people-centered from discovery through scale.
- Purpose, collaboration, training, evaluation, and recognition should remain connected.
43. Q: How should leaders interpret employee feedback that an AI tool is technically useful but makes work less satisfying?
A: Show answer
The feedback should be treated as evidence of a trade-off that needs investigation rather than dismissed because the tool performs well technically. Leaders should examine whether the tool changes workload, autonomy, interaction, task complexity, stress, recognition, or responsibility. The chapter warns that AI can alienate employees and recommends inclusive practices to address those effects. Managers should ask for specific examples, compare outcomes before and after implementation, and redesign the workflow or support arrangements where feasible. The objective is not to maximize satisfaction at the expense of all performance goals, but to find sustainable ways to deliver value without avoidable harm or disengagement.

Study instruction:
- Conduct a structured review of employee feedback and map each concern to a workflow change, support action, or decision explanation.

Key point:
- Technical usefulness does not guarantee a positive employee experience.
- Feedback should inform workflow redesign and support.
44. Q: What common mistakes should leaders avoid when introducing AI to employees?
A: Show answer
Leaders should avoid pursuing automation without employee feedback, presenting AI as infallible, neglecting training, ignoring job or workload concerns, isolating technical and business teams, failing to communicate purpose, and attributing outcomes solely to the technology. They should also avoid using performance metrics that encourage unsafe behavior, promising benefits that are not established, or collecting feedback without responding. The chapter's examples demonstrate how exclusion, poor communication, siloing, and dehumanizing practices can undermine adoption. A disciplined approach requires participation, transparent limits, human support, cross-functional collaboration, and fair recognition of contribution.

Study instruction:
- Review a proposed rollout and identify at least five risks caused by exclusion or poor leadership communication, with mitigation actions.

Key point:
- Exclusion, overconfidence, and siloed implementation undermine AI adoption.
- Avoidable mistakes can be mitigated through participation and accountable leadership.
45. Q: What are the chapter's most important lessons for sustainable human-AI collaboration?
A: Show answer
Sustainable collaboration requires leaders to understand AI well enough to guide it, recognize its limitations, and connect technology to organizational purpose. Employees must be included, trained, supported, and able to raise concerns. Social connection and cross-functional teamwork prevent isolation and siloed adoption. Humble leadership encourages feedback and learning, while recognition makes human contributions visible. Organizations should evaluate both operational outcomes and employee experience, adapt workflows, and maintain human accountability for consequential decisions. The chapter's core message is that successful AI transformation depends on constant human-to-human connection, collaborative work, and valuing employees for the uniquely human contribution they make alongside technology.

Study instruction:
- Summarize the four inclusive practices and develop a personal or organizational action plan that applies them to a real AI use case.

Key point:
- AI adoption is a human and organizational transformation as much as a technical one.
- Inclusion, collaboration, leadership learning, and recognition are central to sustainable success.

Table 2
Embedding Human Values in AI Products and Services
1. Q: What is the central argument of “Bring Human Values to AI”?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that successful AI products must be designed and managed around human values, not evaluated only by technical performance. Accuracy, speed, scale, and cost matter, but they do not establish whether a system is safe, fair, respectful, trustworthy, or appropriate for the people affected by it. Values must therefore influence the whole innovation lifecycle: identifying whose values matter, translating principles into system behavior, assessing trade-offs, selecting and governing partners, collecting human feedback, and responding to unexpected behavior after launch. This makes alignment an organizational and product-development responsibility rather than a final compliance check or a feature added after engineering is complete. The chapter organizes the challenge into six connected areas: define values, write them into the program, assess trade-offs, align partners, ensure human feedback, and prepare for surprises. Each area requires explicit ownership, evidence, review, and adaptation as the product and its environment change.

Study instruction:
- Explain the six areas in the chapter and show how each one contributes to value alignment across an AI product’s lifecycle.

Key point:
- AI quality includes human and societal consequences, not only technical performance.
- Values alignment must be built into development, deployment, monitoring, and improvement.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
2. Q: Why are conventional AI performance metrics insufficient for determining whether an AI product is acceptable?
A: Show answer
Conventional metrics such as accuracy, latency, throughput, cost, and benchmark scores describe selected technical properties, but they do not fully describe the effects of a product on people. A highly accurate system can still expose private information, produce discriminatory outcomes, manipulate users, undermine autonomy, or behave unsafely in a context that was not represented in its tests. A recommendation system can maximize engagement while amplifying harmful material; a decision-support tool can be efficient while making its reasoning difficult to challenge. Performance measures are therefore necessary but incomplete. Product teams need complementary measures for safety, privacy, fairness, robustness, transparency, human control, accessibility, and the quality of remedies when something goes wrong. The relevant measures depend on the use case and affected groups. They should be linked to explicit requirements and tested in realistic operating conditions rather than treated as generic scores that automatically prove a system is trustworthy.

Study instruction:
- Select an AI product and list three technical performance metrics and three human-impact measures that would be needed to evaluate it responsibly.

Key point:
- Technical capability does not by itself establish social acceptability or trustworthiness.
- Evaluation must include context-specific effects on users and other affected people.

Source links:
- OECD: AI Principles
3. Q: What is the difference between a value, a principle, a policy, a requirement, and a technical guardrail in AI development?
A: Show answer
A value expresses what an organization or society considers important, such as dignity, privacy, fairness, safety, or helpfulness. A principle translates a value into a general direction for conduct, such as minimizing unnecessary data collection or ensuring that consequential decisions can be reviewed. A policy assigns organizational expectations, authority, and responsibilities. A requirement makes the expectation testable for a particular product or process, such as restricting access to a defined class of personal data. A technical guardrail is an implemented mechanism intended to enforce or support that requirement, such as an access-control rule, refusal behavior, output filter, rate limit, or human-approval gate. These levels should connect, but they are not interchangeable. A statement that a product “respects privacy” is not sufficient unless teams specify data purposes, access, retention, security, user rights, and verification tests. Technical guardrails also cannot resolve every ethical or legal question; governance and human judgment remain necessary.

Study instruction:
- Take one value, such as privacy, and write a linked principle, policy, product requirement, technical control, and test of effectiveness.

Key point:
- Values become operational only when translated into accountable and testable practices.
- Technical controls support, but do not replace, organizational governance and judgment.

Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework
4. Q: Whose values should be considered when a company designs an AI-enabled product?
A: Show answer
The relevant group is broader than the paying customer or the product team. It may include direct users, people whose data is processed, people affected by a system’s recommendations or decisions, employees who operate or are monitored by it, suppliers, business partners, regulators, civil-society organizations, advocacy groups, and communities affected indirectly. For example, a recruitment model affects applicants who may never interact with its interface; a school tool affects students, teachers, parents, and administrators; a security system can affect people who are observed without choosing to use it. The chapter emphasizes widening stakeholder consideration because AI products can influence society beyond the immediate transaction. Stakeholder selection should be based on actual impact pathways, not simply on who is easiest to consult. Organizations should document who was included, who was not, why the selection was made, and how concerns from less powerful groups can be heard and addressed.

Study instruction:
- Draw a stakeholder map for one AI use case and identify direct users, data subjects, indirectly affected groups, and parties with oversight responsibilities.

Key point:
- People affected by an AI system may not be its customers or intended users.
- Stakeholder mapping should follow the system’s real impact pathways.

Source links:
- OECD: AI Principles
5. Q: How can a product team identify stakeholders who may be overlooked during AI value definition?
A: Show answer
Start by describing the system’s intended purpose, data sources, outputs, users, deployment settings, and foreseeable secondary uses. Trace who supplies data, who is classified or ranked, who relies on the output, who can contest it, and who bears the cost of an error. Include people with limited digital access, language minorities, children, older adults, people with disabilities, workers subject to automated monitoring, and communities whose data may be underrepresented. Consult operational staff who handle exceptions as well as people who experience the system’s effects. Use interviews, workshops, impact assessments, complaints, field research, and independent expertise where appropriate. A stakeholder map should be revisited when the product expands into new markets, adds features, changes its model, or is used for a different purpose. Consultation is not meaningful if it is only symbolic; teams should record what they heard, what changed as a result, and why some suggestions were not adopted.

Study instruction:
- Review a stakeholder map and identify at least three groups whose interests might be missed by a customer-only consultation process.

Key point:
- Stakeholder discovery should include people exposed to indirect and downstream effects.
- Consultation needs a traceable connection to product decisions.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
6. Q: How should a company handle disagreement among stakeholders about which AI values should take priority?
A: Show answer
The company should not assume that a single consultation will produce universal agreement. Stakeholders may reasonably disagree about privacy, security, openness, safety, convenience, autonomy, or the distribution of benefits and risks. The team should identify the specific conflict, the people affected, the legal and human-rights constraints, the severity and reversibility of possible harms, and the evidence available. It should distinguish non-negotiable legal or safety requirements from preferences that can be balanced through product choices. Deliberation should include transparent reasons, representation of affected groups, and an escalation route for unresolved high-impact conflicts. Where a trade-off cannot be justified or controlled, limiting the feature, changing the use case, or not deploying may be the responsible decision. Recording dissent and uncertainty is more useful than presenting a false consensus. Values governance is a continuing process as context and stakeholder expectations change.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a decision record for a hypothetical conflict between user convenience and privacy, including affected groups, constraints, options, and reasons for the selected approach.

Key point:
- Value conflicts require transparent reasoning and accountable decisions, not an assumed universal preference.
- Some constraints should operate as hard limits rather than negotiable product preferences.

Source links:
- United Nations: Universal Declaration of Human Rights
7. Q: How can established moral frameworks and international principles help companies define values for AI products?
A: Show answer
Established frameworks provide a starting point for reasoning about dignity, rights, welfare, fairness, harm, and responsibility. A company may examine human-rights instruments, democratic principles, professional codes, sector standards, and ethical theories rather than inventing a value vocabulary from scratch. These sources can help teams identify interests that commercial product metrics may overlook. However, a framework does not automatically answer every design question. Principles must be interpreted for the product’s purpose, affected populations, local context, and applicable law. A company should explain why it selected particular principles, how it handles conflicts among them, and how the principles become requirements and controls. The chapter notes approaches that draw on established moral systems or international principles, while warning in practice that broad declarations need to be translated into concrete product decisions and tested behavior.

Study instruction:
- Choose an established ethical or human-rights framework and show how two of its principles could become product requirements for a specific AI system.

Key point:
- Established frameworks offer a defensible foundation for value definition.
- They must be adapted into context-specific and testable product practices.

Source links:
- OECD: AI Principles
- United Nations: Universal Declaration of Human Rights
8. Q: What do the OECD AI Principles contribute to defining trustworthy AI?
A: Show answer
The OECD AI Principles provide an intergovernmental foundation for developing and using AI that is innovative and trustworthy while respecting human rights and democratic values. Their values-based principles address areas including inclusive growth and well-being, human rights and fairness, transparency and explainability, robustness and safety, and accountability. The 2024 update reflects developments such as general-purpose and generative AI, privacy, intellectual property, safety, and information integrity. For a company, the principles can guide policy, stakeholder engagement, impact assessment, transparency, risk management, and accountability. They are not a substitute for binding national or regional law, nor do they provide a ready-made test for every product. Teams must translate the principles into use-case requirements, identify responsible owners, and choose evidence that can show whether the product behaves consistently with the stated objectives.

Study instruction:
- Select three OECD AI Principles and write one measurable product or governance control for each.

Key point:
- OECD principles provide an internationally recognized values-based reference for trustworthy AI.
- Organizations still need context-specific controls and must comply with applicable law.

9. Q: How can the Universal Declaration of Human Rights inform AI product design without being treated as a technical specification?
A: Show answer
The Universal Declaration of Human Rights expresses foundational rights and principles such as equal dignity, non-discrimination, life and security, privacy-related protections, freedom of thought and expression, and access to education. It can help teams recognize that AI systems may affect interests beyond immediate user satisfaction. For example, a biometric system may raise concerns about privacy and equal treatment, while an educational AI tool may affect access to learning and the treatment of children. The Declaration is not a software standard, certification scheme, or detailed algorithmic test. A company should use it as a normative reference, then identify applicable treaties and laws, assess the system’s impact on rights, and translate relevant obligations into design and governance controls. It should also avoid claiming that a product is “human-rights compliant” merely because a team has cited the Declaration.

Study instruction:
- Identify two rights potentially affected by an AI use case and describe the impact assessment and product controls needed to address them.

Key point:
- Human-rights principles help identify important impacts that technical metrics may miss.
- A declaration informs analysis but does not replace applicable law or detailed controls.

- United Nations: Universal Declaration of Human Rights
10. Q: When should a company articulate its own AI values rather than rely only on external principles?
A: Show answer
External principles provide a common foundation, but companies may need additional values that reflect their mission, products, users, risk appetite, and responsibilities. A company developing educational tools may place particular emphasis on learner agency, developmental appropriateness, and teacher oversight. A health technology company may need explicit commitments concerning clinical safety, confidentiality, and escalation to qualified professionals. The chapter describes multidisciplinary internal processes involving technologists, ethicists, human-rights specialists, legal experts, product teams, and employees across functions. A company-specific statement is credible only when leadership supports it, affected stakeholders can influence it, and product teams can apply it to actual decisions. It should identify prohibited conduct, decision rights, measures, and review arrangements. It must not be used to dilute legal duties or to claim that an organization’s preferences override the rights of people affected by its systems.

Study instruction:
- Draft a short value statement for an AI product and identify the roles that should review, approve, and operationalize it.

Key point:
- Company-specific values should complement, not displace, external rights and standards.
- Credibility depends on implementation, accountability, and evidence of use in decisions.

Source links:
- OECD: AI Principles
11. Q: What is the “veil of ignorance” approach to eliciting AI values, and why might it reduce self-interested bias?
A: Show answer
The veil of ignorance is a thought experiment associated with philosopher John Rawls. Participants consider rules for a community without knowing what position they themselves will occupy within it. Applied to AI value elicitation, people are asked to consider system rules without knowing whether they will be a powerful customer, a vulnerable user, a person subject to a decision, or someone disadvantaged by the technology. This can encourage attention to fairness and the interests of people who might otherwise have little influence. The chapter describes a related approach used by a research team to elicit values from customers, employees, and others. The method does not eliminate bias or guarantee agreement. Facilitation, participant selection, framing, power dynamics, and the interpretation of results still matter. It should be combined with evidence from affected communities and explicit analysis of rights and risks.

Study instruction:
- Run a short veil-of-ignorance exercise for an AI hiring or education system and record which safeguards participants prioritize when they do not know their future role.

Key point:
- Imagining oneself in an unknown position can expose self-serving assumptions.
- The exercise supports deliberation but does not replace representative consultation or impact assessment.

Source links:
- United Nations: Universal Declaration of Human Rights
12. Q: How should a company validate that its stated AI values reflect the experiences of affected people?
A: Show answer
Validation should test whether the values are understandable, relevant, and credible to the people who may experience the system’s effects. Teams can use interviews, participatory workshops, user research, scenario testing, complaints analysis, independent review, and consultation with civil-society or domain experts. They should include people with different languages, abilities, levels of digital access, and exposure to risk. The company should ask participants to evaluate concrete situations rather than only endorse abstract words such as “fairness” or “safety.” For example, ask what a person should be told when an AI system denies an application, what evidence should be available to challenge an error, and who should be able to intervene. Record disagreement and changes made as a result. If a stakeholder group is excluded, explain why and consider alternative ways to understand its interests.

Study instruction:
- Design a consultation plan for a high-impact AI product, specifying participants, questions, feedback channels, and how decisions will be documented.

Key point:
- Values should be tested against concrete experiences and foreseeable impacts.
- Meaningful consultation includes feedback, response, and transparent documentation.

- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
13. Q: How can abstract human values be translated into measurable product requirements?
A: Show answer
Start by defining the value in the context of the intended use, then identify the harm or failure the value is meant to prevent and the behavior expected from the product. For privacy, a requirement might specify that personal data is collected only for a documented purpose, retained for a defined period, and accessible only to authorized roles. For fairness, requirements might define relevant groups, test conditions, acceptable error analysis, and a process for investigating disparities. For user autonomy, a product might provide meaningful choices, explanations, and a way to decline optional personalization. Requirements should identify an owner, test method, acceptance criteria, monitoring signal, and response if the control fails. A metric is useful only when it measures the intended outcome rather than a convenient proxy. Where values are difficult to quantify, use qualitative review, case analysis, and documented expert judgment instead of pretending that a single score captures them.

Study instruction:
- Convert the values of privacy, safety, and autonomy into one requirement and one verification method for each.

Key point:
- Operational requirements connect values to observable product behavior and accountability.
- Not every important value can be reduced to one numerical score.

- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework
14. Q: What does it mean to write values into an AI program rather than leave them in a policy document?
A: Show answer
Writing values into a program means embedding the intended constraints and behaviors into the product’s design, training, deployment, and operating controls. A policy may state that a system must not expose confidential information, but the product must also enforce access restrictions, minimize data, test for leakage, log relevant events, and provide an incident response process. A safety commitment may require prohibited-use rules, testing for dangerous outputs, monitoring for misuse, and escalation to a human reviewer. The chapter emphasizes that values, red lines, guardrails, and regulatory requirements should be reflected in the program itself and updated when requirements change. This does not mean that every ethical question can be solved by code. Human oversight, governance, user communication, and remedies remain necessary, particularly where the system’s behavior is uncertain or consequences are significant.

Study instruction:
- Choose one policy commitment and identify the technical, operational, and human controls needed to make it effective in a deployed AI system.

Key point:
- A written value must be connected to system behavior and operating controls.
- Code is one part of a broader governance and accountability system.
