- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
15. Q: How does privacy by design support human values in AI products?
A: Show answer
Privacy by design means considering privacy throughout the system lifecycle rather than treating it as a notice or security feature added at the end. Teams should identify the purposes for processing, determine what data is necessary, limit collection and retention, restrict access, protect data in transit and at rest, and provide appropriate information and rights to individuals. They should also examine whether model training, logging, analytics, vendor access, or personalization creates secondary uses that were not expected by people. Privacy design includes governance choices as well as technical mechanisms; encryption alone does not make excessive collection appropriate. In jurisdictions governed by the GDPR, the organization must assess the law’s scope and applicable obligations, including principles such as purpose limitation and data minimization. Product teams should document the rationale, test the controls, and revisit them when the model or use case changes.

Study instruction:
- Map the personal-data lifecycle of an AI application and identify where purpose limitation, minimization, access control, retention, and user rights must be addressed.

Key point:
- Privacy needs to shape data and product decisions from the beginning.
- Security controls do not justify collecting or retaining data without a valid purpose.

Source links:
- European Union: General Data Protection Regulation
16. Q: What does safety by design require from an AI product team?
A: Show answer
Safety by design requires the team to anticipate foreseeable hazards and build prevention, detection, mitigation, and response into the product lifecycle. The work begins with a clear statement of intended use and foreseeable misuse, then identifies affected people, severity of possible harm, exposure conditions, and available safeguards. Controls may include limits on capability, safe defaults, human approval for consequential actions, refusal behavior, rate limits, monitoring, fail-safe modes, and incident escalation. Testing should cover normal use, edge cases, adversarial prompts, environmental changes, and interactions with other systems. Safety is not proven by one pre-launch test because models, users, and contexts evolve. Teams need post-deployment monitoring and the authority to suspend or roll back a feature when evidence indicates unacceptable risk. Sector-specific safety obligations must also be checked where products affect health, transport, finance, or other regulated domains.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a safety case for an AI feature showing hazards, affected parties, preventive controls, detection methods, and response actions.

Key point:
- Safety requires lifecycle risk management, not only pre-release testing.
- Teams need a practical mechanism to restrict, suspend, or change unsafe behavior.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
17. Q: What are AI guardrails and red lines, and how should they be distinguished?
A: Show answer
Red lines define conduct or outcomes that a product or organization will not permit, such as facilitating specified harmful activity or exposing protected information. Guardrails are the technical and operational measures used to prevent, detect, or respond to behavior that approaches or crosses those boundaries. Examples include input restrictions, output policies, access controls, human review, monitoring, and refusal or escalation mechanisms. A red line should be specific enough to guide decisions and should identify who has authority to interpret exceptions. Guardrails need testing because they can fail, be bypassed, or create false positives that block legitimate use. A refusal mechanism should also explain appropriate alternatives where possible. The chapter notes that generative AI systems need formal constraints, but no single filter can guarantee that all prohibited outputs will be prevented. Governance must therefore include layered controls, testing, monitoring, and incident handling.

Study instruction:
- Draft two red lines for a customer-facing AI assistant and list at least three controls that would help enforce each one.

Key point:
- Red lines define unacceptable outcomes; guardrails are mechanisms intended to enforce boundaries.
- Guardrails require testing, monitoring, and escalation because they are not infallible.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
18. Q: How should AI product teams respond when laws and regulatory expectations change after a product has been launched?
A: Show answer
Teams need a process for monitoring relevant legal developments, determining which products and markets are affected, interpreting the obligations with qualified legal and compliance personnel, and translating changes into system requirements. This may involve updating data practices, documentation, transparency, testing, risk controls, user notices, human oversight, or deployment restrictions. The process should identify affected model components and dependencies so that a regulatory change can be traced to the parts of the product that need review. It should include version control, approval, testing, release management, and evidence that changes were implemented. Requirements differ by jurisdiction, product category, provider or deployer role, and date of application. The EU AI Act, for example, has phased application and specific provisions; a team should consult the current consolidated legal text rather than rely on an old summary or assume one deadline applies to every obligation.

Study instruction:
- Create a regulatory-change workflow showing monitoring, applicability assessment, control mapping, implementation, testing, approval, and retained evidence.

Key point:
- Regulatory compliance is a continuing product-maintenance responsibility.
- Applicability depends on the law, role, system category, jurisdiction, and relevant date.

Source links:
- European Union: Artificial Intelligence Act
- European Union: General Data Protection Regulation
19. Q: How can a company measure whether an AI product is behaving consistently with its stated values?
A: Show answer
The company should define indicators that reflect the intended value and the product’s actual context. Safety indicators may include incident severity, rates of unsafe outputs, successful escalation, and time to mitigation. Privacy indicators may include unauthorized access, retention exceptions, and data-subject request handling. Fairness analysis may compare error patterns or outcomes across relevant groups, while recognizing that the choice of groups, measures, and legal constraints needs careful review. Transparency measures may examine whether users receive understandable information and whether explanations are useful in practice. Qualitative review, user research, complaints, red-team findings, and independent assessments can complement quantitative measures. Every indicator should have a defined owner, data source, frequency, threshold or trigger, and response. A dashboard without an action process creates visibility but not control. Metrics should be reviewed when the product, population, or risk profile changes.

Study instruction:
- Build a small values scorecard for an AI service with an indicator, data source, owner, review frequency, and escalation trigger for each value.

Key point:
- Values measures need owners and response processes, not just dashboards.
- Quantitative and qualitative evidence should be interpreted together.

Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework
20. Q: What is the danger of using a convenient proxy metric as proof that an AI system respects human values?
A: Show answer
A proxy metric measures something related to a desired outcome but may not capture the outcome itself. For example, time spent on a platform may indicate engagement but does not necessarily show that users are safe, informed, or satisfied. A low complaint count may mean few problems, but it may also mean that reporting is difficult or users do not trust the process. A model’s average accuracy may conceal serious errors for a smaller group. If a proxy becomes a target, teams may optimize it in ways that undermine the value it was intended to represent. Organizations should therefore test whether the measure tracks the underlying concept, examine subgroup and edge-case results, and combine it with independent evidence. Metrics should be periodically challenged for unintended incentives and revised when product behavior or user expectations change.

Study instruction:
- Identify one proxy metric used by an AI product and explain at least two ways it could improve while the underlying human value worsens.

Key point:
- A proxy is evidence about a value, not proof that the value has been achieved.
- Measurement systems can create incentives that conflict with the original purpose.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
21. Q: How should managers assess trade-offs between competing human values in an AI product?
A: Show answer
Managers should identify the values in conflict, the people affected, the benefits and harms under each option, the legal constraints, the uncertainty of the evidence, and the possibility of less harmful alternatives. They should distinguish a genuine trade-off from a design weakness that can be reduced through better architecture, access controls, user choice, or human review. The decision should consider the severity, likelihood, distribution, reversibility, and duration of potential harms, not only aggregate business benefits. Some rights or safety obligations may establish limits that cannot be overridden by a commercial preference. For unresolved high-impact issues, decision-makers should document the rationale, dissent, residual risk, and accountable approver. Trade-offs should be revisited after deployment because new evidence may change the balance. A defensible decision is transparent about uncertainty and does not present a difficult choice as a purely technical optimization problem.

Study instruction:
- Compare two design options for an AI service and prepare a trade-off record covering benefits, harms, affected groups, legal limits, uncertainty, and residual risk.

Key point:
- Trade-offs require explicit reasoning about distribution and severity of impact.
- Legal and safety constraints may rule out options regardless of commercial benefit.

Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework
22. Q: Why can privacy and security conflict in the design of an AI-enabled service?
A: Show answer
Security measures may require monitoring, logging, identity checks, or data access to detect misuse and protect systems. Those measures can create privacy risks if they collect excessive information, retain it too long, or make it available to too many people. Conversely, strict data minimization without a carefully designed security approach may leave insufficient evidence to investigate incidents or detect abuse. The goal is not to choose one value and ignore the other. Teams should define a specific security purpose, collect only the information necessary for that purpose, restrict access, protect logs, set retention periods, and review whether monitoring is proportionate. They should also examine less intrusive alternatives and explain relevant practices to users where required. The chapter uses privacy and security as examples of values that can pull in different directions and require context-sensitive design rather than slogans.

Study instruction:
- Review a proposed monitoring feature and assess its security benefit, privacy impact, data minimization, access rules, and retention controls.

Key point:
- Security monitoring should be purpose-limited and proportionate to the risk addressed.
- Privacy and security should be designed together rather than treated as mutually exclusive.

Source links:
- European Union: General Data Protection Regulation
23. Q: How can an AI product balance helpfulness with user autonomy and dignity?
A: Show answer
A system can be helpful while still undermining autonomy if it takes over decisions, manipulates choices, or encourages dependence. The appropriate level of assistance depends on the user, task, stakes, and consequences of error. An AI assistant for an older adult may support daily activities while preserving the person’s ability to choose and perform tasks. An educational tool may offer hints and feedback without removing the learner’s opportunity to think. Teams should identify where assistance is beneficial, where human choice should remain central, and when the system should explain limitations or defer to a qualified person. User preferences, accessibility, informed consent, and the ability to opt out may be important design considerations. The chapter highlights that safety and dignity or agency can require nuanced decisions, especially for older people and children. Helpful behavior should not be equated with doing everything automatically.

Study instruction:
- Design an AI support feature for an older adult or student and identify where it should assist, ask permission, provide choices, or defer to a person.

Key point:
- Helpful automation should strengthen rather than unnecessarily replace human agency.
- Dignity and autonomy depend on the user’s context and the consequences of assistance.

Source links:
- United Nations: Universal Declaration of Human Rights
24. Q: How should a company balance rapid time-to-market with the risk that an AI product is not adequately aligned with its values?
A: Show answer
A company should treat release speed as one consideration within a risk-based launch decision, not as a reason to bypass essential safeguards. It should define minimum release criteria for the product’s intended use, including safety testing, privacy review, security assessment, evaluation of foreseeable misuse, user information, incident response, and accountable approval. The required evidence should increase with the severity and scale of potential harm. A staged rollout, restricted feature set, limited user group, or supervised pilot may allow learning while limiting exposure. If critical risks remain unmitigated, delaying or cancelling the launch may be appropriate. Public launch errors can damage trust and create legal, financial, and human consequences that outweigh a short-term first-mover advantage. The chapter’s examples illustrate that speed can create reputational costs when guardrails are weak, but a single public incident should not be treated as a universal measure of product risk.

Study instruction:
- Create risk-based launch gates for a generative AI feature and specify which unresolved issues would block release.

Key point:
- Release timing should follow evidence and risk tolerance, not competitive pressure alone.
- Staged deployment can support learning without exposing everyone to untested risks.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
25. Q: When might market segmentation help a company respond to different stakeholder values, and what are its limits?
A: Show answer
Market segmentation may help a company serve groups with different preferences, such as customers who prioritize privacy over extensive personalization. A product can offer meaningful settings or distinct service models when the differences are legitimate and the choices are understandable. The chapter uses a privacy-focused search engine as an example of positioning a product around a particular value preference. However, segmentation is not a way to avoid legal obligations or shift serious harms onto less powerful groups. A company should not offer a lower level of safety, fairness, or rights protection merely because a segment is less vocal or has fewer alternatives. It should also avoid deceptive choice architecture, where users cannot realistically understand or refuse the consequences. Market preferences can inform product strategy, but minimum legal, safety, and human-rights safeguards remain applicable regardless of customer segment.

Study instruction:
- Evaluate a proposed AI product tier and distinguish legitimate preference-based features from safeguards that should apply to all users.

Key point:
- Segmentation can reflect different preferences when choices are meaningful and transparent.
- It cannot justify weaker baseline protection or discriminatory treatment.

Source links:
- OECD: AI Principles
26. Q: What role can an independent oversight board or AI policy committee play in values governance?
A: Show answer
An oversight body can provide structured review of difficult cases, examine whether product decisions reflect declared values, challenge management assumptions, and bring perspectives that may be missing from a development team. The chapter describes the use of an independent board in content moderation and notes that companies may establish watchdog boards or internal AI policy teams. An effective body needs a clear mandate, access to relevant evidence, appropriate expertise and diversity, independence sufficient to challenge commercial pressure, and a defined relationship with management and legal accountability. It should not become a symbolic panel that approves decisions without understanding the system. Its authority may include recommendations, escalation, review of appeals, policy updates, or approval of specified high-risk changes. The organization should publish or internally document how recommendations are considered and how conflicts of interest are managed.

Study instruction:
- Draft terms of reference for an AI oversight committee, including membership, decision rights, evidence access, conflicts of interest, and escalation routes.

Key point:
- Oversight bodies can improve challenge and accountability when they have real access and a clear mandate.
- A committee does not transfer the organization’s legal or operational responsibility.

Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework
27. Q: Why should companies establish communication channels with stakeholders before and after an AI product is launched?
A: Show answer
Early communication helps teams understand needs, expectations, potential harms, and disagreement before design choices become difficult to change. After launch, communication allows users and affected people to report errors, explain unexpected impacts, request review, and provide information that testing may not have revealed. Channels can include in-product reporting, customer support, accessible complaint processes, professional or community advisory groups, and formal escalation routes for serious incidents. The organization should explain what information is collected, how reports are assessed, what response users can expect, and when a matter will be referred to a human reviewer. A feedback channel is not effective if reports disappear into an unmonitored inbox. Teams need triage criteria, service levels, ownership, documentation, and a process for feeding lessons into product updates and policy changes.

Study instruction:
- Map the feedback route for an AI product from user report through triage, investigation, decision, response, and product improvement.

Key point:
- Stakeholder communication is a continuous input to product governance.
- Reporting channels need owners, response standards, and traceable follow-through.

Source links:
- European Commission: Digital Services Act Transparency
28. Q: Why is aligning the values of external AI partners a separate challenge from defining a company’s own values?
A: Show answer
An AI product often depends on components and services supplied by other organizations, including foundation models, cloud providers, training-data vendors, evaluation services, and fine-tuning partners. Those partners may use different data, safety assumptions, policies, incentives, or definitions of acceptable behavior. A company can therefore have a well-written internal value statement but still deliver a product whose underlying model or data pipeline conflicts with it. The chapter emphasizes that downstream developers may have limited visibility into how a third-party model was trained and limited control over its original capabilities. Partner alignment requires due diligence before selection and ongoing review after deployment. It also requires clear allocation of responsibilities for testing, incident reporting, model changes, data rights, security, and remediation. The company remains responsible for understanding the risks of the product it offers, even when important components are externally supplied.

Study instruction:
- Draw an AI supply-chain map and identify the values, risks, evidence, and responsibilities associated with each external partner.

Key point:
- A product inherits risks and assumptions from its suppliers and partners.
- Outsourcing model development does not eliminate downstream governance responsibilities.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
29. Q: What should a company examine when conducting due diligence on a foundation-model provider?
A: Show answer
Due diligence should examine the provider’s intended-use restrictions, safety practices, evaluation evidence, incident history, update process, security controls, privacy and data-handling terms, intellectual-property position, transparency, and ability to support investigations. The company should ask what is known about training data and its limitations, how model versions are identified, how changes are communicated, what testing has been performed, and what mechanisms exist to report vulnerabilities or harmful behavior. It should also assess service continuity, subcontractors, data location, audit rights, and contractual remedies where relevant. The depth of review should be proportionate to the use case and potential harm. A provider’s marketing statement that a model is “safe” or “aligned” is not sufficient evidence. Where information is unavailable, the buyer should record the uncertainty, consider compensating controls, restrict the use, or choose another supplier.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a due-diligence questionnaire for a foundation-model vendor and identify which answers require documentary evidence or independent testing.

Key point:
- Vendor claims should be supported by evidence about the model, data, controls, and update practices.
- Unresolved supplier uncertainty should influence deployment limits and risk acceptance.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
30. Q: How can bias in third-party training data undermine the values of a downstream AI product?
A: Show answer
Training data may underrepresent groups, reproduce historical discrimination, contain harmful stereotypes, reflect uneven quality, or omit important languages and contexts. A downstream company may fine-tune a model for a specific product but still inherit patterns learned from upstream data. If those patterns affect recommendations, classification, generation, or ranking, the product may conflict with its stated commitments to fairness, safety, or inclusion. Due diligence should therefore examine available information about data provenance, composition, collection rights, filtering, annotation, and known limitations. Product-specific evaluation should test performance and impact on the populations and settings where the system will be used. Where the provider cannot disclose sensitive details, the company can request meaningful assurance, conduct independent tests, limit the use, and monitor outcomes. A lack of visibility should be treated as a risk, not as proof that the data is unbiased.

Study instruction:
- Identify potential data gaps for a multilingual AI service and design tests to detect unequal performance or harmful output patterns.

Key point:
- Downstream fine-tuning does not automatically remove bias inherited from upstream data.
- Data provenance and population-specific testing are important parts of partner due diligence.

Source links:
- OECD: AI Principles
31. Q: Why can fine-tuning a third-party foundation model create new values-alignment risks?
A: Show answer
Fine-tuning changes how a model responds in a particular product or context, but it does not give the downstream company complete control over the model’s underlying training history or all of its behavior. New data, objectives, prompts, retrieval sources, and product interfaces can introduce different risks or weaken safeguards. Customization may also create many variants that behave differently across users or markets. A downstream developer should document the base model and version, the fine-tuning data and method, intended use, evaluation results, known limitations, and controls that remain active. It should retest relevant values after every material change and ensure that the product does not assume the provider’s original alignment work covers the customized system. The chapter’s concern is not that fine-tuning is inherently wrong, but that responsibility and testing must follow the complete product configuration.

Study instruction:
- Design a change-control checklist for a fine-tuned model, including version identification, dataset review, safety evaluation, approval, and rollback criteria.

Key point:
- Fine-tuning can change model behavior and must trigger renewed evaluation.
- Alignment evidence for a base model does not automatically validate every downstream variant.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
32. Q: How should contracts and technical controls allocate responsibility between an AI company and its external partners?
A: Show answer
Contracts should clarify the intended use, permitted data handling, security obligations, model and service changes, incident notification, cooperation with investigations, intellectual-property rights, audit or assurance evidence, subcontracting, service continuity, and remedies for material failures. Technical controls should complement those terms by limiting data access, separating environments, validating model versions, monitoring outputs, and preserving relevant logs. Responsibilities should be allocated according to who controls each activity and who can realistically prevent or correct the risk. A contract cannot make an unsafe model safe, and a technical control cannot resolve every legal or commercial dispute. The company should also maintain an exit or substitution plan where a partner changes its service or fails to meet essential requirements. Legal review is necessary because contractual enforceability and regulatory duties vary by jurisdiction and relationship.

Study instruction:
- Review a sample AI supplier agreement and map each major risk to a contractual obligation, technical control, owner, and evidence record.

Key point:
- Supplier governance requires both enforceable responsibilities and operational safeguards.
- Contractual allocation does not remove the need for downstream testing and oversight.

Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework
33. Q: What is reinforcement learning from human feedback, and how can it contribute to AI value alignment?
A: Show answer
Reinforcement learning from human feedback, or RLHF, is a family of methods that uses human judgments about model outputs to help train or refine a system. In a common language-model workflow, people review example responses or compare alternatives; those preferences are used to train a reward model or otherwise guide optimization of the system’s behavior. The aim is to make outputs more consistent with specified human preferences, such as helpfulness or avoidance of harmful content. The chapter describes RLHF as one way to use human input to reduce undesirable behavior. However, feedback represents the judgments of particular people under particular instructions and conditions. It is not a direct measurement of universal human values or a guarantee that the model will behave safely in every context. RLHF should be combined with policy design, technical testing, monitoring, and human accountability.

Study instruction:
- Explain the basic feedback loop in an RLHF process and identify two limitations that remain even after the model has been optimized using human preferences.

Key point:
- Human preferences can guide model behavior but do not define every ethical or legal boundary.
- RLHF is one alignment technique within a broader risk-management system.

Source links:
- Ouyang et al.: Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback
34. Q: At which stages of the AI lifecycle should human feedback be collected?
A: Show answer
Human feedback can contribute before launch, during deployment, and after material updates. During development, engineers and domain specialists can review outputs, identify failure patterns, and test whether the system follows intended constraints. Red teams can deliberately probe for weaknesses. During a pilot, representative users can evaluate usability, misunderstandings, and context-specific problems. After launch, users and affected people can report harmful outputs, incorrect decisions, or unexpected behavior. Content moderators and support teams can provide structured evidence about recurring issues. Feedback should be connected to a process for triage, investigation, policy revision, retraining, or product change. The chapter emphasizes that values alignment is not achieved by one training event. Because the system and its environment evolve, the feedback process must continue and should be linked to version control and risk review.

Study instruction:
- Build a lifecycle feedback map that identifies who provides feedback before launch, during a pilot, in production, and after a model update.

Key point:
- Feedback is needed throughout development and operation, not only during initial training.
- Each feedback stage should connect to an accountable decision and improvement process.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
35. Q: How should a company decide who is qualified to provide human feedback on AI behavior?
A: Show answer
The selection depends on the type of judgment required. General usability feedback may come from representative users, while clinical, legal, financial, safety, or other specialized judgments may require qualified domain professionals. Moderation decisions may require trained reviewers who understand the applicable policy and cultural context. A single group should not be assumed to represent all affected people. The company should consider language, geography, lived experience, professional expertise, accessibility, and the risk of conflicts of interest. It should provide clear instructions, examples, escalation routes, and a method for resolving disagreements. Feedback providers should understand how their judgments will be used and what limitations apply. Diversity does not guarantee correctness, but a narrow or poorly trained group can systematically miss important harms and produce an incomplete picture of acceptable behavior.

Study instruction:
- Specify the reviewer profiles needed for a healthcare assistant, a social-media moderation system, and an educational AI tool, explaining why each requires different expertise.

Key point:
- Reviewer competence and representativeness should match the judgment and affected population.
- Clear guidance and escalation are needed when feedback is ambiguous or contested.

Source links:
- OECD: AI Principles
36. Q: What is red teaming, and how does it help test whether an AI system follows its intended values?
A: Show answer
Red teaming is a structured adversarial exercise in which testers deliberately attempt to make a system fail, behave unsafely, disclose protected information, or violate specified policies. In AI development, red teams may test prompt injection, harmful requests, misleading inputs, biased scenarios, misuse pathways, and interactions that were not covered by ordinary evaluation. The purpose is to discover weaknesses before or after deployment so that teams can improve controls. Red teaming should have a defined scope, rules of engagement, documentation standards, escalation path, and process for verifying fixes. It is not proof that all risks have been found. A test set can be incomplete, a model may change, and real users may discover new combinations of behavior. External testing can broaden perspectives, but it requires safe disclosure arrangements and appropriate protection for testers and users.

Study instruction:
- Write a red-team test plan for an AI assistant, including target values, attack scenarios, evidence capture, severity ranking, and retest requirements.

Key point:
- Red teaming actively searches for failure modes that ordinary testing may miss.
- A successful test round reduces uncertainty but cannot prove the absence of future failures.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
37. Q: What risks arise when human feedback is inconsistent, biased, or poorly explained?
A: Show answer
Reviewers may disagree because instructions are unclear, the task is ambiguous, cultural expectations differ, or the evidence is incomplete. Individual biases can influence labels, and inconsistent judgments can cause a model to learn unstable or contradictory preferences. If the feedback dataset overrepresents one language, group, or viewpoint, the resulting system may perform poorly for others. Organizations should develop clear annotation guidelines, train reviewers, use calibration exercises, measure agreement where appropriate, review difficult cases, and document how disagreements are resolved. They should also monitor whether feedback incentives encourage speed at the expense of careful judgment. A high agreement score does not necessarily prove that the standard is fair or correct; reviewers can agree while applying a flawed rule. Quality assurance must therefore examine both consistency and the substantive validity of the criteria being used.

Study instruction:
- Design an annotation quality process that includes reviewer training, calibration, disagreement escalation, bias checks, and periodic policy review.

Key point:
- Feedback quality depends on both consistent application and sound underlying criteria.
- Reviewer agreement alone does not prove that a judgment standard is appropriate.

Source links:
- Ouyang et al.: Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback
38. Q: Why should users understand how their feedback and moderation decisions are used to improve an AI system?
A: Show answer
Transparency helps users understand what happens after they report an issue, how a decision is made, and whether their information may be used for model improvement. Without clear explanations, people may assume that reports are ignored, that automated moderation is infallible, or that their data is used for purposes they did not expect. Organizations should explain relevant reporting processes, review criteria, appeal options, data practices, and the limits of automated decisions in language suited to the audience. For online platforms subject to the EU Digital Services Act, specific transparency and statement-of-reasons duties may apply, and the applicable obligations depend on the service and provider category. Transparency should not expose confidential security details or personal information unnecessarily. It should provide enough information for users and oversight bodies to understand decisions and seek appropriate review.

Study instruction:
- Draft a plain-language explanation of an AI moderation decision, including the reason, applicable rule, review option, and relevant data-use information.

Key point:
- Transparency makes feedback and moderation processes more understandable and contestable.
- Legal reporting duties vary by service and must be checked against the applicable rules.

Source links:
- European Commission: How the Digital Services Act Enhances Transparency
- European Commission: Harmonised DSA Transparency Reporting Rules
39. Q: What responsibilities does a company have toward human reviewers who evaluate disturbing or harmful AI content?
A: Show answer
Human reviewers may be exposed to graphic, abusive, violent, or otherwise distressing material while assessing model outputs or user reports. A values-aligned feedback system must consider the welfare of the people who perform this work, not only the quality and quantity of labels they produce. Employers and contractors should assess exposure risks, provide suitable training, limit unnecessary exposure, use appropriate rotation and workload controls, offer confidential support, and establish escalation procedures for particularly distressing cases. They should also consider the psychological effects of repetitive exposure and ensure that productivity targets do not discourage reviewers from taking necessary breaks or seeking help. The chapter cites a legal settlement as an example of the consequences of inadequate protection, but the broader lesson is that human feedback has human costs that must be governed and monitored.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a reviewer-welfare risk assessment covering content exposure, workload, support, escalation, privacy, and contractor oversight.

Key point:
- Human feedback processes must protect the people who perform review work.
- Reviewer welfare is part of responsible product governance, not an optional operational benefit.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
40. Q: Why should companies prepare for unexpected behavior even after extensive AI testing?
A: Show answer
AI systems can behave in ways that were not anticipated because of complex interactions among data, model architecture, prompts, users, tools, deployment environments, and updates. A system may encounter inputs outside its training distribution, be used for an unintended purpose, or produce a combination of outputs that reveals a new risk. Testing can reduce uncertainty but cannot enumerate every possible interaction in an open environment. The chapter gives examples of surprising behavior in games, chatbots, and simulations to show why organizations should not assume that a system will remain within expected patterns simply because it passed pre-launch tests. Companies need monitoring, user reporting, incident response, change control, and the ability to restrict or withdraw a feature. Preparedness means expecting uncertainty and building a system that can detect, communicate, and respond to it.

Study instruction:
- Identify three plausible unexpected behaviors for an AI product and define how each would be detected, escalated, and mitigated after launch.

Key point:
- Pre-deployment testing cannot guarantee predictable behavior in every real-world context.
- Preparedness requires monitoring and the ability to intervene after release.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
41. Q: How can extreme customization and hyper-personalization increase the difficulty of maintaining values alignment?
A: Show answer
When users or organizations fine-tune, configure, or personalize an AI model, the number of operating variants can grow rapidly. Different prompts, retrieval sources, settings, data, and integrations may produce behavior that differs from the tested base product. A safeguard that works in one configuration may fail in another, and a change intended for one market may create new risks elsewhere. The company should maintain an inventory of material variants, identify who can create or modify them, define allowed customization boundaries, and test high-risk configurations. It should also monitor model and configuration changes, preserve version identifiers, and provide a way to roll back or disable a problematic variant. Personalization should not be treated as harmless simply because it is requested by a user or customer; downstream effects and applicable restrictions still need assessment.

Study instruction:
- Create a version inventory for a customized AI service and specify which configuration changes require renewed safety and values testing.

Key point:
- More variants create more opportunities for behavior to diverge from the tested baseline.
- Version control and risk-based retesting are essential for customized systems.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
42. Q: What should post-launch monitoring cover for an AI product intended to respect human values?
A: Show answer
Monitoring should cover technical performance and the human-impact indicators associated with the product’s stated values. Relevant signals may include harmful or inaccurate outputs, privacy and security events, subgroup performance changes, unusual user behavior, complaints, appeal outcomes, model drift, and incidents involving downstream integrations. Teams should monitor both aggregate patterns and severe individual cases because a low average error rate can conceal a serious harm. Monitoring should be proportionate to risk and should respect privacy and data-protection obligations. The organization needs defined thresholds, named owners, review frequency, incident severity levels, escalation paths, and authority to restrict or suspend a feature. Results should feed into policy updates, model changes, training, user communication, and supplier review. Monitoring is effective only when signals lead to decisions and documented action.

Study instruction:
- Build a post-launch monitoring plan with at least five value-related indicators, their data sources, owners, thresholds, and response actions.

Key point:
- Monitoring should detect both systemic patterns and serious individual incidents.
- Every material signal needs an owner and a defined response route.

Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework
43. Q: How should a company establish an AI incident-reporting and learning process?
A: Show answer
The process should define what counts as an incident or hazard, how reports can be submitted, what information is needed, how severity is assessed, who investigates, and how corrective action is approved and verified. Reports may come from users, employees, suppliers, researchers, regulators, or people affected indirectly. An incident record should preserve relevant context such as model and configuration version, date, use case, observed behavior, impact, affected parties, response, and lessons learned, while limiting unnecessary personal data. Similar events should be grouped so that recurring patterns can be identified. The organization should communicate outcomes appropriately and share information with relevant parties when required. External repositories such as the OECD AI Incidents and Hazards Monitor or the AI Incident Database can help practitioners learn from documented events, but they do not replace an organization’s own reporting and investigation obligations.

Study instruction:
- Draft an AI incident form and severity process, including minimum evidence, escalation criteria, remediation owner, and closure verification.

Key point:
- Incident reporting turns unexpected behavior into evidence for correction and prevention.
- External databases support shared learning but do not replace internal governance.

Source links:
- OECD: AI Incidents and Hazards Monitor Methodology
- AI Incident Database
44. Q: What can organizations learn from the pharmacovigilance model when monitoring AI products after release?
A: Show answer
Pharmacovigilance recognizes that pre-market clinical testing cannot identify every adverse effect that may emerge when a medicine is used by a wider population over time. Healthcare professionals and patients can report suspected side effects, which are assessed and used to identify patterns and determine whether warnings, restrictions, or withdrawal are needed. The chapter uses this as an analogy for AI: a product may pass extensive testing and still produce unexpected harms in real settings. The transferable lessons are to provide accessible reporting channels, standardize incident information, analyze patterns, communicate risks, and have authority to change or withdraw a product. The analogy has limits because AI systems differ from medicines in their update cycles, contexts, and types of impact. It is a model for continuous post-market vigilance, not a claim that AI regulation should copy pharmaceutical procedures exactly.

Study instruction:
- Compare pharmacovigilance with AI incident management and identify which elements transfer directly and which require adaptation.

Key point:
- Real-world monitoring is needed because pre-release testing cannot reveal every adverse effect.
- Incident learning must lead to warnings, controls, changes, or withdrawal when justified.

Source links:
- OECD: AI Incidents and Hazards Monitor Methodology
45. Q: How can adversarial learning or continuous model-to-model testing strengthen AI monitoring?
A: Show answer
Adversarial learning can use one system or testing agent to challenge another with inputs designed to expose weaknesses, unsafe outputs, or unexpected responses. It can help scale repeated testing across model versions and deployment conditions, particularly where manual red teaming is too slow to cover every update. A testing model may generate unusual prompts, probe policy boundaries, or compare responses against expected constraints. However, automated adversarial testing has limitations: the testing model may share blind spots with the target, miss contextual harms, or produce false alarms. Human experts should review important findings, and test results should be connected to severity assessment and remediation. Continuous testing complements, rather than replaces, representative evaluation, independent red teaming, user feedback, and operational monitoring.

Study instruction:
- Design a continuous adversarial test cycle and identify which results require human review before a product release or policy change.

Key point:
- Automated adversarial testing can expand coverage across versions and scenarios.
- It remains limited by the tester’s own assumptions and needs human interpretation.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
46. Q: What is out-of-distribution detection, and why should an AI system be able to abstain when it encounters unfamiliar conditions?
A: Show answer
Out-of-distribution detection attempts to identify inputs or circumstances that differ materially from those represented in the system’s training or evaluation data. An autonomous vehicle may encounter an unfamiliar object or unusual road condition; a medical model may receive an image unlike those in its validated population; a language model may be asked to make a high-stakes judgment outside its intended scope. A system that recognizes uncertainty can abstain, request more information, or refer the case to a qualified human rather than produce an unsupported answer or action. Detection is imperfect and should not be treated as a guarantee that all unfamiliar cases will be recognized. Teams need to test detection performance, define safe fallback behavior, and monitor cases where the system expresses unjustified confidence. Abstention is valuable only when the fallback is practical and does not create a different unmanaged risk.

Study instruction:
- Identify unfamiliar conditions for an AI use case and define when the system should answer, ask for more information, abstain, or escalate to a human.

Key point:
- Recognizing uncertainty can prevent a system from acting beyond its validated conditions.
- Abstention requires a safe and usable fallback process.

Source links:
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
47. Q: How can natural-language feedback channels help users communicate unexpected AI behavior?
A: Show answer
A natural-language feedback channel allows users to describe what happened, what they expected, why the result was harmful or confusing, and what outcome they need without requiring technical knowledge. This can reveal context that a simple thumbs-up or thumbs-down rating would miss. For example, a user may explain that a recommendation is culturally inappropriate, that an answer ignored an accessibility need, or that a system’s refusal prevented a legitimate task. The organization can classify reports, request clarification where appropriate, and connect them to policy review, testing, or product changes. The AI itself should not be the sole judge of complaints about its own behavior; human review and independent escalation are important for consequential issues. Feedback forms should be accessible, protect sensitive information, and explain how reports will be used.

Study instruction:
- Draft a user feedback prompt that captures the observed behavior, expected behavior, impact, context, and preferred resolution without requesting unnecessary personal data.

Key point:
- Natural-language reports can reveal context that simple ratings fail to capture.
- Important complaints need human review and a route to corrective action.

Source links:
- European Commission: Digital Services Act Transparency
48. Q: How can the NIST AI Risk Management Framework help operationalize the chapter’s six challenges?
A: Show answer
The NIST AI Risk Management Framework provides a voluntary structure for incorporating trustworthiness into the design, development, use, and evaluation of AI systems. Its core functions—Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage—can organize the work described in the chapter. Govern establishes policies, roles, accountability, and a risk-management culture. Map clarifies the system’s purpose, context, stakeholders, and potential impacts. Measure evaluates risks through testing, analysis, and monitoring. Manage prioritizes risks and selects responses such as mitigation, avoidance, transfer, or acceptance where appropriate. The functions are iterative rather than a one-time sequence. The framework does not decide every value conflict or replace law, but it can help organizations make responsibilities and evidence more systematic. NIST’s Generative AI Profile adds considerations relevant to generative systems and their distinctive risks.

Study instruction:
- Map each of the chapter’s six challenge areas to one or more NIST AI RMF functions and identify evidence that would demonstrate implementation.

Key point:
- Governance, context mapping, measurement, and risk response can structure values work across the lifecycle.
- The framework supports decisions but does not replace legal analysis or stakeholder judgment.

Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework
- NIST: Generative AI Risk Management Profile
49. Q: How should a company assess cross-border regulatory obligations for an AI product that uses data and models in several jurisdictions?
A: Show answer
The company should map where the provider, deployer, users, affected people, data, and processing activities are located, then assess which laws apply to each role and activity. Relevant obligations may concern personal data, automated decision-making, consumer protection, safety, discrimination, cybersecurity, transparency, content moderation, and sector-specific regulation. A product offered in multiple markets may face different requirements and different implementation dates. The EU AI Act and GDPR are examples of legal instruments with defined scopes and obligations, but their applicability depends on facts such as territorial reach, system classification, organizational role, and processing context. Teams should maintain a jurisdictional register, obtain qualified legal advice, map obligations to product controls, and track updates. They should not assume that compliance in one market proves compliance elsewhere or that a single global policy automatically satisfies every local requirement.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a jurisdiction map for an AI service operating in the European Union and at least one other market, listing the legal questions that require specialist review.

Key point:
- Regulatory obligations depend on jurisdiction, role, system type, and processing activity.
- A legal register must be linked to product controls and maintained as rules change.

Source links:
- European Union: Artificial Intelligence Act
- European Union: General Data Protection Regulation
50. Q: What practical roadmap can an organization use to embed human values into an AI product from concept through post-launch operation?
A: Show answer
A practical roadmap begins by defining the product’s purpose, intended users, affected groups, and foreseeable misuse. Next, identify relevant human values, legal duties, and stakeholder concerns, then translate them into policies, requirements, measurable tests, and red lines. Assess the trade-offs and document who approves residual risk. Conduct due diligence on models, data, and service partners, and assign responsibilities for updates and incidents. Build privacy, safety, security, transparency, and human-oversight controls into the system. Use qualified and representative human feedback, structured red teaming, and independent review before launch. Release in stages where appropriate, monitor technical and human-impact signals, maintain accessible reporting channels, and investigate incidents. Feed lessons into model changes, policy revisions, supplier reviews, and user communication. Maintain version records and evidence so that decisions can be audited. The roadmap should be iterative: material changes in users, data, model behavior, regulation, or deployment context should trigger renewed assessment.

Study instruction:
- Create an end-to-end implementation plan for one AI product, assigning an owner, evidence artifact, and review trigger to each lifecycle stage.

Key point:
- Values alignment is a continuous management discipline spanning design, partners, feedback, monitoring, and response.
- Evidence, accountability, and willingness to change or withdraw a feature make the commitments operational.

Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework
- OECD: AI Principles

Table 3
From Prediction to Transformation: AI, Decision Systems, and Organizational Design
1. Q: What is the central argument of “From Prediction to Transformation”?
A: Show answer
Ajay Agrawal, Joshua Gans and Avi Goldfarb argue that artificial intelligence creates business value primarily when it improves decisions, not merely when it produces predictions or insights. A prediction has limited practical value unless someone or something uses it to choose an action. Because decisions are interdependent across departments and supply chains, introducing AI can change more than one task: it can alter who decides, what information they need, how authority is distributed, and how the wider operating system is coordinated. The article therefore shifts attention from installing an AI model to redesigning the decision system in which the model operates.

Study instruction:
- Summarize the article’s thesis and distinguish an AI prediction from the organizational decision it is intended to improve.

Key point:
- AI creates value when prediction changes a decision and its outcome.
- The relevant unit of change may be the whole decision system, not a single task.

Source links:
- HBR: From Prediction to Transformation
- NBER: Exploring the Impact of Artificial Intelligence — Prediction versus Judgment
2. Q: Why do the authors consider “insights” an inadequate description of AI’s business value?
A: Show answer
The word “insights” can conceal the missing link between analysis and action. A model may identify a likely outcome, classify a transaction or forecast demand, but the organization still has to decide what to do with that information. Unless the prediction changes an operational choice, resource allocation, customer response or risk treatment, the organization may have improved its knowledge without improving performance. The authors argue that entrepreneurs should explain which decisions become better, faster or newly feasible because of the AI. This requires specifying the decision-maker, the action, the expected benefit and the consequences for connected processes.

Study instruction:
- Take an AI proposal described as delivering insights and rewrite it as a decision-improvement proposition with a named decision, user and outcome.

Key point:
- An insight is not an outcome; the decision changed by the insight is the value mechanism.
- AI business cases should identify actions and measurable consequences.

Source links:
- HBR: From Prediction to Transformation
3. Q: What is the difference between prediction and judgment in a business decision?
A: Show answer
Prediction estimates what is likely to happen under a set of conditions. Judgment evaluates what the outcome is worth, which objectives matter, what trade-offs are acceptable and which action should be taken. A route-planning system may predict travel times under different routes, but a person or organization must judge whether speed, cost, safety, accessibility or customer preference should dominate. The distinction matters because AI can improve the prediction component without automatically supplying values, objectives or contextual judgment. Some judgment can be structured or supported by data, but organizations must identify which parts of a decision remain normative, contextual or accountable.

Study instruction:
- Choose a decision and separate its predictive questions from the judgments about objectives, acceptable trade-offs and final action.

Key point:
- Prediction estimates outcomes; judgment evaluates consequences and priorities.
- A better forecast does not determine which outcome an organization should prefer.

Source links:
- NBER: Prediction, Judgment and Complexity
- HBR: From Prediction to Transformation
4. Q: Why does AI’s ability to predict more accurately not automatically make decisions better?
A: Show answer
Decision quality depends on more than predictive accuracy. The prediction must be relevant to the decision, available at the right time, trusted appropriately and connected to a workable action. The decision-maker must also understand the objective, constraints, consequences and uncertainty that remain. A highly accurate demand forecast can still produce waste if procurement rules, supplier lead times or menu decisions do not respond to it. A fraud score can be accurate but harmful if the organization has no proportionate review process for false positives. The authors’ wider point is that prediction improvements create value only when the surrounding decision system is redesigned to use them responsibly.

Study instruction:
- List the additional conditions, beyond model accuracy, required to turn a prediction into a better decision.

Key point:
- Prediction accuracy is necessary for some applications but not sufficient for decision quality.
- Workflow, authority, timing, objectives and safeguards determine whether predictions are useful.

Source links:
- HBR: From Prediction to Transformation
- NBER: Exploring the Impact of Artificial Intelligence — Prediction versus Judgment
5. Q: What does the article mean by a system of decision-making?
A: Show answer
A decision system is the connected set of people, rules, information, processes, technologies and authority arrangements through which an organization makes choices. It includes formal approvals, informal handoffs, data flows, incentives and the way decisions in one function affect decisions in another. A business may use AI for forecasting, but its value depends on whether purchasing, inventory, staffing, pricing and customer service can adapt to the forecast. The article argues that AI often changes the relationship among these components. Therefore, a successful implementation examines the entire path from prediction to action and the interactions among affected decision-makers.

Study instruction:
- Map one organizational decision from input data to final action, showing roles, systems, approvals and downstream decisions.

Key point:
- AI operates inside an organizational decision system, not in isolation.
- Connected workflows should be reviewed when a prediction process changes.

Source links:
- HBR: From Prediction to Transformation
6. Q: What lesson does the Emirates Team New Zealand America’s Cup example illustrate?
A: Show answer
The team used an AI sailing bot to run large numbers of simulator trials much faster than human sailors could. The bot learned maneuvers that initially seemed counterintuitive to experienced sailors but proved effective when tested on the water. The innovation did not simply replace the crew or deliver a report of insights. It changed the process linking boat design, simulation, tactical experimentation and sailor learning. Better design choices and better sailing maneuvers could now be explored together, accelerating the overall learning cycle. The example shows how AI can transform a system by expanding the range and speed of experimentation while humans remain responsible for applying the learning in the real world.

Study instruction:
- Explain how the AI changed the relationship among simulation, boat design, tactics and human sailor learning.

Key point:
- The transformation occurred at the system level, not merely through automated prediction.
- AI accelerated experimentation and improved the human team’s capabilities.

Source links:
- HBR: From Prediction to Transformation
- McKinsey: How AI Learned to Sail
7. Q: Why was the sailing bot more than a replacement for human sailors in the simulator?
A: Show answer
The bot could conduct many experiments continuously and compare alternative designs or maneuvers under consistent conditions. This expanded the number of learning cycles and allowed the team to discover options that human experience might not have suggested. Human sailors then tested and learned from the AI’s recommendations, transferring the discoveries into real sailing. The result was a new division of work: the AI searched the design and tactic space at machine speed, while human expertise interpreted, validated and applied the findings. This is an example of complementarity rather than simple substitution. The model’s value came from reorganizing how the human team learned and improved.

Study instruction:
- Identify which activities were accelerated by the bot and which remained dependent on human interpretation and real-world validation.

Key point:
- AI can extend human capability by exploring options at scale.
- The best design may combine machine experimentation with human application.

Source links:
- HBR: From Prediction to Transformation
- McKinsey: How AI Learned to Sail
8. Q: What is the difference between automating a task and transforming a decision system?
A: Show answer
Task automation replaces or speeds up a discrete activity while leaving most surrounding roles and processes intact. System transformation changes how connected decisions are made, who has authority, how information flows and how activities coordinate. Replacing a human image classifier with an AI model may be a narrow automation if the same diagnostic workflow remains unchanged. By contrast, using AI to redesign how clinicians, imaging specialists and care teams allocate responsibility can reshape the decision system. Task automation can be valuable, but the article warns that its largest opportunities may appear when organizations rethink the operating model rather than simply inserting a model into an existing task.

Study instruction:
- Classify three AI applications as task automation or system transformation and justify the classification using changes in authority and workflow.

Key point:
- Automation changes a task; transformation changes connected decisions and coordination.
- A narrow deployment can be useful without capturing the full opportunity.

Source links:
- HBR: From Prediction to Transformation
9. Q: Why do major technologies often have a delayed productivity impact?
A: Show answer
Organizations initially apply new technologies to familiar tasks because those uses are easier to understand and implement. Larger gains may require changes to buildings, production methods, roles, processes, skills and complementary investments. The article uses electricity and computing to illustrate this delay: replacing one power source or using a computer as a faster calculator does not automatically redesign a factory or business. The productivity effects become more visible when organizations restructure work around the technology. AI may follow the same pattern. Early prediction tools can create local efficiencies, while broader transformation requires time, experimentation and changes across multiple functions.

Study instruction:
- Explain why a new technology can be widely adopted before its full effect appears in organizational productivity.

Key point:
- General-purpose technologies often require complementary organizational redesign.
- Early task-level savings may understate long-term system-level potential.

Source links:
- HBR: From Prediction to Transformation
10. Q: What does the article’s comparison with electricity teach about AI implementation?
A: Show answer
Electricity initially replaced steam power in some existing applications, but its more transformative effect came when factories were redesigned around electric motors, new layouts and more flexible production. The lesson is that a technology’s greatest value may arise from new ways of organizing work rather than from directly substituting for an existing method. Similarly, organizations should not limit AI to predicting the same outputs previously estimated by employees. They should ask whether more reliable and cheaper prediction enables new business models, faster feedback, different decision rights or redesigned value chains. Such redesign requires experimentation and complementary investments rather than only software procurement.

Study instruction:
- Use the electricity analogy to identify one redesign opportunity that would be missed by deploying AI only as a direct task substitute.

Key point:
- Technology value depends on complementary changes in processes and organization.
- Ask what becomes newly possible, not only what existing task becomes cheaper.

11. Q: What does the article’s comparison with computerization and the productivity paradox imply?
A: Show answer
The article recalls the observation that computers could be seen everywhere while productivity statistics initially showed little improvement. The implication is that the presence of technology does not prove that organizations have reorganized work effectively. Benefits may be delayed, difficult to measure or offset by transition costs, duplicated processes and poor integration. For AI, counting models, licenses or automated tasks is therefore an inadequate measure of transformation. Organizations should track whether decisions improve, resources are used more effectively, service outcomes change and coordination costs are managed. Productivity measurement should also account for the time and investment needed to redesign workflows.

Study instruction:
- Design a productivity evaluation that distinguishes technology adoption, workflow change and measurable business outcomes.

Key point:
- Technology deployment is not equivalent to realized productivity.
- Measure decision and system outcomes, including transition costs and delayed benefits.

12. Q: Why do the authors describe AI as an advance in prediction technology?
A: Show answer
The authors frame many AI advances as reducing the cost of estimating uncertain outcomes, often by using more data and more complex statistical methods. Examples include predicting language translations, classifying medical images, identifying likely fraud and forecasting demand. The framing focuses attention on what AI can estimate rather than treating it as an all-purpose substitute for human intelligence. Prediction feeds into decisions, but it does not automatically specify goals or acceptable trade-offs. This economic perspective helps organizations ask where better forecasts would change choices, how much a prediction is worth and what human judgment or organizational design remains necessary.

Machine translation predicts a likely translation based on patterns in prior human translations. Medical-image classification predicts how an expert may interpret an image. In both cases, AI can deliver predictions faster or more cheaply than relying entirely on a human prediction specialist. The business impact depends on the next decision: whether a translated message is fit for use, whether a diagnosis needs confirmation, or whether a clinician should change a treatment plan. The article uses these cases to illustrate AI’s ability to substitute for particular predictive tasks while leaving important judgment, review and accountability questions in the wider workflow.

14. Q: What does the Verafin fraud-detection example illustrate about substitution?
A: Show answer
The article describes Verafin’s AI-driven fraud-detection technology being used by financial institutions as a substitute for some of the predictive work previously performed by security teams. The example illustrates how a model can replace a defined prediction activity and create substantial commercial value. It does not mean that all fraud prevention, investigations, policy decisions or customer remediation can be delegated to the model. Institutions still need to decide what actions follow a risk signal, how false positives are reviewed, how customers are treated and how controls are monitored. The operational question is which part of the workflow is being substituted and which responsibilities remain.

15. Q: How can AI shift decision rights inside an organization?
A: Show answer
When prediction becomes cheaper, more accurate or more widely available, the person who previously supplied the prediction may no longer control the decision. Authority may move to the person who still determines objectives, chooses trade-offs or acts on the prediction. In other cases, central AI systems may concentrate authority by defining the criteria embedded in automated workflows. In medical settings, for example, automating an imaging prediction can make the treating physician’s judgment more central while changing the role of the imaging specialist. Organizations should therefore map who generates information, who interprets it, who approves action and who is accountable after AI is introduced.

Study instruction:
- Before and after an AI deployment, map the decision rights of data specialists, professional users, managers and system owners.

Key point:
- AI can redistribute authority as well as reduce labor for prediction.
- Decision rights and accountability should be deliberately redesigned and documented.

16. Q: What are the three broad patterns of decision-right changes described in the article?
A: Show answer
The article illustrates several patterns. AI may concentrate a large number of similar decisions in fewer hands, as in an HR process where one executive sets screening criteria that replace many small screening decisions. It may radically centralize a decision, as in payment authorization governed by common risk parameters. Or it may make the judgment of existing decentralized professionals more important, as when a clinician uses AI-supported image analysis to make treatment decisions. These patterns are not mutually exclusive across a whole organization. A deployment should be assessed by identifying which decisions become centralized, which become decentralized and where professional judgment gains or loses importance.

17. Q: How does the ride-sharing example show that AI can decentralize one decision while centralizing another?
A: Show answer
Ride-sharing platforms make route prediction and matching available through a shared system. More people can participate in providing rides because they no longer need the same specialized local navigation knowledge as traditional taxi drivers. At the same time, the platform controls important elements such as matching passengers and drivers, routing and transaction infrastructure. Drivers gain access to the market but lose discretion over some decisions that a traditional taxi driver controlled. This illustrates that a technology can broaden participation in one activity while concentrating platform control over another. An organizational analysis should therefore examine both who can participate and who controls the rules and infrastructure.

Study instruction:

- AI platforms can lower entry barriers while shifting control to infrastructure owners.

The platform also matches supply and demand, provides a booking interface, supports payments, transmits trip information and coordinates drivers and passengers. Those complementary capabilities allow people without detailed city knowledge or a traditional dispatch network to provide transport services. The system therefore transforms market participation and industry organization rather than simply giving drivers a better map. The lesson for AI projects is to inspect the complementary services and coordination mechanisms required to turn prediction into a functioning business process.

The process may use fewer people while concentrating influence over who advances. This creates a governance issue: the criteria, data, thresholds and exception rules become highly consequential. A centralized screening model requires documented job relevance, testing for adverse effects, human review routes and clear accountability for the criteria chosen.

20. Q: What governance risks arise when AI centralizes hiring-screening criteria?
A: Show answer
A small group may determine which candidates are visible to the organization, making the chosen criteria and model behavior powerful gatekeepers. If the training data or screening rules reflect past inequities, automation can reproduce or scale them. A system may also hide the reason an applicant was filtered out unless explanations and audit records are designed into the process. Governance should therefore cover job-related validation, input-data quality, subgroup outcome analysis where lawful, human reconsideration, accessibility, vendor oversight and periodic review. The organization remains responsible for its employment decisions even when a vendor provides the model.

Study instruction:
- Develop a control checklist for automated résumé screening, including validation, monitoring, exception handling and documentation.

Key point:
- Centralized AI screening can scale both efficiency and error.
- The employer must govern criteria, outcomes and accountability rather than delegate responsibility to the model.

23. Q: What is the difference between prediction accuracy and clinical responsibility?
A: Show answer
Prediction accuracy describes how well a system estimates or classifies an image under specified conditions. Clinical responsibility includes deciding how to interpret that result for a particular patient, explaining options, weighing risks and benefits, and ensuring that care is appropriate. Even a highly accurate model can be wrong for an individual case or perform differently across populations and settings. A treating professional must understand the model’s limitations and know when further review is needed. The distinction generalizes beyond medicine: a model can supply an estimate, but the accountable decision-maker must consider context and consequences.

Study instruction:
- Separate model performance measures from the professional responsibilities that remain after an AI prediction is delivered.

Key point:
- Model accuracy does not transfer all professional accountability to the model.
- Human oversight should be substantive and matched to the consequences of the decision.

24. Q: What does the restaurant avocado example demonstrate about AI and supply-chain uncertainty?
A: Show answer
A restaurant uses AI to forecast customer demand more accurately and therefore varies its avocado orders rather than buying a fixed weekly amount. The restaurant may reduce waste and avoid lost sales. However, its supplier had planned around a relatively stable order quantity and now faces much more volatile demand. The supplier may pass that variability upstream to growers, who must make production decisions months or a year in advance. The restaurant has improved its own forecast while shifting uncertainty to other participants. The example demonstrates that local prediction improvements can create system-level coordination problems when connected organizations rely on one another’s plans.

Study instruction:
- Trace the avocado example from customer demand through restaurant orders, supplier purchases and grower decisions.

Key point:
- A local forecast improvement can increase uncertainty for upstream partners.
- Value-chain outcomes depend on how information and variability are shared.

25. Q: Why does better forecasting not automatically solve the supply-and-demand problem?
A: Show answer
A better forecast helps one decision-maker plan, but it does not guarantee that all other participants receive the same information or can adapt their decisions at the same speed. The underlying supply-and-demand alignment problem remains. If every participant changes orders based on more responsive local predictions, their combined behavior may produce larger fluctuations in quantities and timing. A supplier may need to buy, store or grow inventory long before final demand is known. Better local predictions can therefore reduce uncertainty for one actor while shifting it to another. The system needs mechanisms to coordinate plans, share information and absorb variability.

Study instruction:
- Identify where uncertainty is reduced and where it reappears in a supply chain using a simple flow diagram.

Key point:
- Prediction relocates uncertainty as well as reducing it.

26. Q: What are the ripple effects of introducing AI into a value chain?
A: Show answer
A change in one participant’s prediction or decision can alter the volume, timing and variability of orders, workloads and resource needs for other participants. A restaurant’s demand forecast affects its supplier; the supplier’s purchasing changes affect growers; growers must adjust planting and harvesting plans. Similar effects can occur in manufacturing, logistics, finance and healthcare networks. The article argues that AI implementation should map these interdependencies before deployment. A business case that measures only the focal organization’s savings can miss new costs, volatility or coordination burdens imposed on partners. Responsible implementation considers the end-to-end system and the distribution of benefits and risks.

27. Q: What does the article mean when it says AI shifts uncertainty rather than eliminating it?
A: Show answer
AI can make one event or demand estimate more predictable for the organization using it, but the resulting change in orders or actions may make another participant’s planning less reliable. Uncertainty is therefore redistributed across locations, organizations or time periods. The restaurant has a better view of its own demand, but its supplier and the grower may face wider fluctuations. The article’s argument is not that AI fails to reduce uncertainty; it is that the system-level consequences depend on how actors respond to one another. A robust design must coordinate the new information and decide where variability should be buffered or absorbed.
