A: Show answer
Time saved at the task level does not automatically reduce payroll, increase output or improve profit. Employees may use the time for experimentation, low-value busywork, idle time or tasks that do not contribute to strategic objectives. The organization may also incur software, review, data, integration and change-management costs. To capture value, managers need to estimate the time saved, identify how it will be redeployed and define the expected operational or financial outcome. The article emphasizes that productivity gains need deliberate management and alignment across the value chain before they appear in financial results.

Study instruction:
- Choose one AI-assisted task and create a time-savings calculation that includes review time, adoption costs and a plan for redeploying capacity.

Key point:
- Task-level efficiency is not the same as realized financial benefit.
- Saved time must be intentionally redeployed or translated into output, service or cost outcomes.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
36. Q: How should managers redeploy time saved by gen AI?
A: Show answer
Managers should agree with employees which activities will receive the capacity released by AI. Options may include higher-value customer interaction, quality improvement, analysis, service expansion, training or reducing backlogs. The redeployment plan should be realistic and measured. The article recommends treating time as a strategic resource rather than assuming that productivity gains will automatically flow to the bottom line. Managers should avoid rewarding only the number of tasks processed, because that can encourage low-value output. They should also involve employees in identifying the best use of freed capacity, since frontline workers often understand unmet needs and process constraints.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a capacity-redeployment plan that specifies hours saved, new tasks, responsible owners and outcome measures.

Key point:
- Productivity requires an intentional plan for how saved time will be used.
- Employee input can reveal higher-value work that management has not identified.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
37. Q: What is the role of incentives in turning gen-AI efficiency into business gains?
A: Show answer
Incentives influence whether employees and managers use saved time for activities that support organizational priorities. If people are rewarded only for speed or volume, they may generate more low-value outputs or use AI without adequate review. If they have no expectations for redeploying capacity, time savings may disappear into idle or fragmented work. The article suggests linking recognition and incentives to effective use of freed time. A well-designed system should also reward quality, responsible escalation, learning and collaboration. Performance measures need to avoid encouraging employees to conceal errors or overstate AI-generated productivity gains.

Study instruction:
- Design two performance indicators that reward productive redeployment without encouraging rushed work or under-reporting errors.

Key point:
- Incentives help convert saved time into outcomes that matter.
- Reward quality and responsible use, not only speed or volume.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
38. Q: What does the NBER study “Generative AI at Work” add to the chapter’s productivity discussion?
A: Show answer
The NBER working paper by Erik Brynjolfsson, Danielle Li and Lindsey Raymond studied the staggered introduction of a generative-AI conversational assistant among 5,179 customer-support agents. It reported a 14 percent average increase in issues resolved per hour, with a 34 percent improvement for novice and lower-skilled workers and minimal impact for experienced, highly skilled workers. The study also reported improvements in customer sentiment and retention, with suggestive evidence of worker learning. This evidence shows that productivity effects can differ across workers and tasks. It should not be assumed that the same percentages will apply to every workplace, tool or implementation.

Study instruction:
- Read the study abstract and identify the sample, outcome measure, overall effect and differences across worker experience levels.

Key point:
- Observed AI productivity effects can be heterogeneous across workers.
- Use research as context, not as a guaranteed forecast for another organization.

Source links:
- NBER: Generative AI at Work
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
39. Q: Why might gen AI benefit novice employees more than experienced employees in some tasks?
A: Show answer
The NBER customer-support study suggests that AI assistance can help less experienced employees by making effective practices more accessible. A conversational assistant may provide examples, prompts or guidance that help novices handle unfamiliar cases and move along the learning curve. Experienced employees may already perform those tasks efficiently, leaving less room for improvement. This pattern is not universal: results depend on the task, tool, training and employee expertise. Managers should measure effects across experience levels and avoid assuming that one average productivity figure describes everyone. AI may change both immediate output and how quickly employees develop skill.

Study instruction:
- Identify tasks in which junior employees may benefit from embedded guidance and define how learning or quality will be measured.

Key point:
- AI can distribute expert practices and support novice learning.
- Productivity effects should be analyzed by role and experience, not only by average.

Source links:
- NBER: Generative AI at Work
40. Q: How can gen AI change the design of middle-management roles?
A: Show answer
As AI automates or supports routine tasks, middle managers may spend less time supervising manual information work and more time coordinating people, software and exceptions. The article suggests that managers may work directly with software agents and that some functional roles may become more cross-functional. This changes the skills required: managers may need to set objectives, define quality standards, monitor outputs, resolve exceptions and redeploy employee capacity. It does not remove the need for management. In many cases it increases the importance of workflow design, judgment, coaching and accountability.

Study instruction:
- Compare a current manager’s task list with a possible AI-enabled future role and identify new coordination and oversight responsibilities.

Key point:
- AI can reshape management work rather than simply eliminate it.
- Managers need capabilities in workflow design, oversight and people development.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
41. Q: Why should organizations mandate broad access to gen AI tools?
A: Show answer
The authors argue that employees across the organization encounter tasks that could benefit from gen AI, not only technical specialists. Broad access can encourage bottom-up experimentation and reveal practical opportunities that central teams might miss. It also helps employees develop familiarity and share successful approaches. A broad-access strategy should still use approved tools, clear data rules, training and appropriate restrictions for sensitive work. The aim is not that everyone should use AI for every task; it is that employees should be empowered to identify where it is useful and safe within defined boundaries.

Study instruction:
- Design an employee access programme with approved tools, basic training, safe-use rules and a channel for sharing use cases.

Key point:
- Gen AI can be useful across nontechnical functions and roles.
- Broad experimentation should operate within clear security and risk controls.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
- NIST: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile
42. Q: Why is delegating all gen-AI decisions to the CTO or IT department potentially limiting?
A: Show answer
IT and security teams are essential for infrastructure, privacy, security and vendor management, but they may not understand every operational opportunity or have the capacity to approve each experiment individually. If all use cases must wait for a central technical review, adoption may become slower than the organization’s learning needs. The article advocates faster pathways for frontline experimentation while preserving controls for critical risks. A practical model distributes use-case discovery and task assessment to business teams, while IT, legal, security and data specialists provide standards, approved platforms and escalation for higher-risk applications.

Study instruction:
- Define the division of responsibilities between business owners, IT, security, legal and data teams in an AI adoption process.

Key point:
- AI opportunity discovery should not be limited to central technical teams.
- Central functions should enable safe experimentation through reusable standards and controls.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
43. Q: How can organizations avoid uncontrolled shadow-AI use while encouraging experimentation?
A: Show answer
Organizations should offer approved tools that are easy to access, communicate what data may be entered, provide practical training and create a fast process for proposing new use cases. If official pathways are too restrictive, employees may use unapproved consumer services without safeguards. A good programme combines clear boundaries for sensitive information with low-friction access for ordinary work. It also gives employees a way to report problems and share successful methods. Monitoring should focus on meaningful risks and compliance rather than punishing all experimentation. This creates a governed environment that supports innovation instead of driving it underground.

Study instruction:
- Compare current approved-tool access with employee needs and design a safe alternative to unapproved AI use.

Key point:
- Overly restrictive controls can push AI use into ungoverned channels.
- Provide accessible approved tools and clear rules for sensitive information.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
- NIST: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile
44. Q: What strategic advantage can come from proprietary organizational data?
A: Show answer
Proprietary data can give a company context, customer history, process knowledge or operational detail that general-purpose models do not possess. When properly governed, this data can make AI outputs more relevant to the organization’s products, customers and decisions. But data alone is not a moat: competitors may obtain similar information or replicate a technical integration. The article argues that advantage comes from data combined with unique processes, people and a culture that continually converts information into action. Organizations should therefore invest not only in data storage but in the routines that use the data to improve services, decisions and learning.

Study instruction:
- Identify one proprietary dataset and explain how it could improve a decision or customer outcome that competitors cannot easily replicate.

Key point:
- Proprietary data can strengthen relevance and differentiation.
- Data advantage depends on governance and organizational ability to use it repeatedly.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
45. Q: Why does proprietary data eventually become less defensible unless the organization is redesigned around it?
A: Show answer
Data can be copied, purchased, inferred or commoditized over time. If a competitor can access similar information or reproduce a technical connection, the initial advantage may fade. What is harder to copy is an organization that continuously uses data to learn, experiment and adapt its decisions. The article’s Capital One example emphasizes the combination of marketing, risk and IT teams running ongoing experiments and using results to adjust products. The advantage came from the organizational learning loop, not only from the existence of a dataset. Sustainable advantage therefore requires structure, incentives and routines built around data-informed action.

Study instruction:
- Identify a data asset and list the processes, teams and decision routines that would make it difficult for competitors to imitate the resulting capability.

Key point:
- Data ownership alone does not create a durable advantage.
- Learning processes and cross-functional execution make data strategically valuable.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
46. Q: What does the Capital One example show about data-driven organizational design?
A: Show answer
The article describes Capital One combining marketing, risk and IT teams and conducting frequent experiments using customer data. A promotional offer generated growth, while longitudinal behavioral information later helped the company recognize that newer applicants were higher risk and phase out the product. The example illustrates how data can inform both expansion and restraint. A learning organization does not use analytics only to confirm a growth strategy; it uses feedback to recognize when conditions have changed and when a successful product should be modified or stopped. The structure of teams and feedback loops made that learning possible.

Study instruction:
- Explain the sequence from experiment to data collection, customer-behavior analysis, risk recognition and product adjustment.

Key point:
- Data-driven advantage requires cross-functional experimentation and feedback.
- Good learning systems support both scaling and timely withdrawal.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
47. Q: How can an organization create a continuous learning loop around gen AI?
A: Show answer
A learning loop connects data, experimentation, review, operational changes and new measurement. Teams should identify a question, test a use case, compare outcomes with a baseline, examine errors and user feedback, then revise the model, workflow or task allocation. The results should be documented so that learning spreads beyond the original team. A continuous loop requires clear ownership and time for review; otherwise, experiments remain isolated. It should also include safeguards to stop or reverse a deployment if error rates, privacy concerns or unintended effects become unacceptable.

Study instruction:
- Design a repeatable AI learning cycle with a hypothesis, pilot, baseline, evaluation, decision gate and documentation process.

Key point:
- AI capability improves through repeated organizational learning, not deployment alone.
- Learning loops need ownership, evidence and stop-or-adjust criteria.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
- NIST: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile
48. Q: How should organizations decide whether an AI use case should be scaled, redesigned or stopped?
A: Show answer
The organization should compare the use case with its baseline and intended outcomes, assess the cost and frequency of errors, examine user adoption and determine whether the surrounding workflow can support it. It should also review impacts on customers, employees and partners, including privacy and security. If the model performs well but the workflow does not capture the benefit, the process may need redesign rather than abandonment. If risks remain high or the intended value is not realized, the system may need to be restricted or stopped. Decision gates should be documented and owned by people with authority to act.

Study instruction:
- Create decision criteria for scaling, redesigning, restricting or stopping an AI pilot, including performance and risk measures.

Key point:
- A successful pilot requires evidence of both value and operational readiness.
- Redesign the process when the model is useful but benefits are not captured.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
- NIST: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile
49. Q: How can an organization identify which tasks remain distinctly human?
A: Show answer
The organization should examine tasks that require empathy, ethical reasoning, complex contextual judgment, accountability, trust or nuanced interaction. These qualities are particularly important when error costs are high. It should also identify cases where people must reconcile conflicting goals or make decisions that cannot be reduced to a stable set of rules. AI may still assist by summarizing information, generating options or preparing communications, but a qualified person remains responsible for interpreting context and making the final decision. Task analysis should be repeated as model capabilities and organizational controls change, rather than treating task boundaries as permanent.

Study instruction:
- Identify five tasks that require distinctly human judgment and specify how AI could support each without taking over the final decision.

Key point:
- Human work remains central where context, values and accountability dominate.
- AI support should be designed to strengthen rather than displace human judgment.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
50. Q: What should managers do when AI saves time but employees do not use that time productively?
A: Show answer
Managers should first identify why the time is not translating into outcomes. Employees may lack direction, the saved time may be fragmented, review work may offset the benefit, or the organization may not have defined valuable activities to take on. Managers should agree on priorities with staff, estimate realistic savings, track how capacity is redeployed and provide training or resources for higher-value work. The article warns that AI gains can disappear into idle tinkering or low-value tasks. The response should not simply increase workload indiscriminately; it should redesign work so that capacity contributes to quality, service, innovation or development.

Study instruction:
- Create a team-level plan that identifies time saved, barriers to redeployment, new priorities and a review measure.

Key point:
- Efficiency gains require management of employee time and priorities.
- Redeployment should create meaningful value rather than simply increase activity.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
51. Q: How can AI alter an organization chart over time?
A: Show answer
AI may automate tasks concentrated in the No Regrets zone, change the skills needed for other tasks and create new coordination responsibilities. Functional employees may take on cross-functional work, managers may supervise AI-supported processes and some roles may focus on human-first tasks such as complex relationships or strategic judgment. The article emphasizes anticipating these changes because organizational redesign takes time. An organization should not assume every task in a role disappears or that current employees can move automatically into new responsibilities. Workforce planning should include task analysis, reskilling, redeployment, recruitment and clear accountability for AI-enabled work.

Study instruction:
- Redesign one department’s organization chart for an AI-enabled future and identify roles that change, combine, expand or need new skills.

Key point:
- AI changes the task mix and may alter reporting and coordination structures.
- Workforce transition requires deliberate reskilling and role design.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
52. Q: What common misconception does the article identify about AI adoption speed?
A: Show answer
The article distinguishes moving quickly from moving strategically. Speed can help an organization learn sooner, but adopting the same tools for the same tasks as competitors may create only temporary efficiency. Conversely, waiting for flawless tools can cause an organization to miss learning opportunities and new business models. The strategic choice is to experiment rapidly in suitable tasks while differentiating how the technology is used. That requires a clear task framework, risk-based controls and investment in distinctive data and organizational assets. Speed is useful when it advances learning and execution; it is not a strategy by itself.

Study instruction:
- Assess an AI initiative and explain whether its adoption speed is connected to a differentiated business objective.

Key point:
- Move quickly where the use case is suitable, but let strategy determine priorities.
- Generic adoption can be copied and may not produce sustained returns.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
53. Q: How should an organization apply the framework to an end-to-end business process?
A: Show answer
Start by decomposing the process into discrete tasks rather than labelling the whole process as suitable or unsuitable for AI. For each task, identify its inputs, required knowledge, error consequences, output recipient and downstream use. Place it in one of the four zones, then decide whether AI should perform, augment or merely support it. Map the human review and approval points, data permissions, interfaces with other functions and measures of success. Finally, pilot the redesign and examine whether it improves the full process. This approach turns the framework into an implementation method instead of a high-level strategy slogan.

Study instruction:
- Apply the framework to one end-to-end process and produce a task matrix, future workflow, control map and outcome measures.

Key point:
- Task decomposition makes the framework actionable.
- Review the full process and downstream effects, not isolated model performance.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
54. Q: How does the article connect AI strategy with competitive differentiation?
A: Show answer
The authors identify three broad sources of advantage: rapid but targeted deployment across suitable tasks, proprietary data and process improvements that prevent value from being lost to bottlenecks, and unique people, processes and culture that make AI more valuable inside one organization than another. These factors work together. A tool can be copied, but a distinctive customer-data asset, specialized workflow and learning culture may be harder to reproduce. Competitive advantage is therefore not obtained simply by purchasing the best model. It comes from how the company organizes its resources and decisions around the technology.

Study instruction:
- Identify one potential advantage in each of the three sources and explain how the sources reinforce one another.

Key point:
- Advantage comes from targeted use, proprietary data and complementary organizational assets.
- A model or vendor alone is unlikely to create a lasting moat.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
55. Q: What role should boards and senior leaders play in gen-AI strategy?
A: Show answer
Boards and senior leaders should ensure that AI adoption aligns with organizational strategy, risk appetite and stakeholder obligations. They should ask whether use cases improve meaningful tasks, whether sensitive information is protected, whether high-impact decisions retain appropriate human oversight and whether productivity gains are captured. They should also monitor workforce effects, third-party dependencies, reputation and the organization’s ability to learn. The board need not select every tool or design every workflow; it should set expectations, require accountable ownership and receive decision-useful reporting. NIST’s governance approach supports clear roles, risk assessment and ongoing monitoring across the AI lifecycle.

Study instruction:
- Prepare five board questions that test strategic value, human accountability, data governance, risk and measurable benefit.

Key point:
- Senior leadership owns strategic direction and risk accountability.
- Governance should extend across the AI lifecycle and value chain.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
- NIST: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile
56. Q: What is the difference between an AI pilot that proves technical feasibility and one that proves business value?
A: Show answer
Technical feasibility shows that a model can perform a defined task under test conditions. Business value requires evidence that the task is integrated into real work and improves an outcome after accounting for review, integration, training, risk and operating costs. A pilot may generate impressive drafts but fail to reduce cycle time because staff must rewrite them. It may improve speed but create higher error costs or downstream rework. A business-value test therefore compares the full workflow against a baseline and includes user adoption and organizational effects. The article’s strategic framework encourages leaders to judge task fit and value creation, not model demonstrations alone.

Study instruction:
- Design a pilot evaluation that separates technical metrics, workflow metrics, financial impact and risk outcomes.

Key point:
- A working model is not proof of a valuable operating process.
- Measure end-to-end results and total costs against a credible baseline.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
57. Q: What are the most important lessons from the Gen AI Playbook for Organizations?
A: Show answer
The article teaches leaders to evaluate gen AI task by task using the cost of errors and the type of knowledge required. Low-risk explicit tasks can often be automated; creative tasks can be augmented; high-stakes tacit decisions should remain human-led; and high-stakes explicit tasks need quality control and human review. It also argues that organizations should broaden access through safe pathways, treat data as a strategic asset, redesign workflows and roles, and plan how to capture time savings. Since customers, suppliers and competitors can access similar tools, durable advantage depends on distinctive processes, people, data and learning systems. The central message is strategic use, not technology adoption for its own sake.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a one-page executive summary with the four quadrants, three sources of advantage and a practical deployment sequence.

Key point:
- Use the framework to decide where AI acts, assists or remains subordinate to human judgment.
- Build advantage through data, workflow redesign, people and continuous learning.

Source links:
- HBR: The Gen AI Playbook for Organizations
- NIST: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile
- NBER: Generative AI at Work
Rows: 57
Table 5
Managing the AI Product Lifecycle: Selection, Development, Evaluation, Adoption, and Oversight
1. Q: What is the central message of “Keep Your AI Projects on Track”?
A: Show answer
Iavor Bojinov argues that AI projects need a disciplined lifecycle rather than enthusiasm for a promising model. A project must be selected for strategic value and feasibility, developed into a usable product, evaluated in real operating conditions, adopted by intended users and managed after launch. Failure can occur at any stage: a technically strong model may solve the wrong problem, fail to integrate with work, produce weak business results, be rejected by users or deteriorate over time. The article therefore treats AI as an organizational capability requiring product management, experimentation, trust, engineering and ongoing oversight—not merely a data-science exercise.

Study instruction:
- List the five lifecycle stages in order and explain one distinct failure mode that can occur in each.

Key point:
- An AI model is not a successful product until it creates sustained value in use.
- Selection, development, evaluation, adoption and management are connected stages.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
2. Q: What are the five critical stages every AI project should navigate?
A: Show answer
The five stages are selection, development, evaluation, adoption and management. Selection asks whether the problem is strategically important, measurable and feasible. Development turns data and a model into an integrated product. Evaluation tests whether the product creates value and identifies unintended effects. Adoption builds user trust and embeds the product in actual work. Management monitors performance, addresses changes and audits outcomes after deployment. The stages are iterative rather than a one-way checklist: evaluation may reveal a development problem, adoption may expose a trust issue, and ongoing monitoring may require new data or a revised use case.

Study instruction:
- Draw the five-stage lifecycle and add an exit criterion and a feedback loop for each stage.

Key point:
- Each stage has a different management problem and control requirement.
- The lifecycle continues after launch and should feed lessons back into earlier stages.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
3. Q: Why should AI project selection distinguish internal-facing and external-facing projects?
A: Show answer
Internal-facing projects improve how employees perform work, such as sales targeting, supply-chain planning or employee help services. External-facing projects directly shape products and services experienced by customers, such as search results, recommendations or matching platforms. The distinction matters because the users, risks, metrics and adoption mechanisms differ. An internal tool may depend on employee workflow integration and trust, while an external product may affect customer experience, revenue, fairness and reputation at scale. Both categories require strategic alignment, but they should not be evaluated using identical success measures or stakeholder assumptions.

Study instruction:
- Classify three candidate projects as internal-facing or external-facing and identify their primary users and outcome measures.

Key point:
- Project type shapes user needs, risk and performance measurement.
- Internal adoption and external customer impact require different evidence.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
4. Q: What does strategic alignment mean when selecting an AI project?
A: Show answer
Strategic alignment means that the project supports an important organizational objective rather than simply demonstrating an interesting technical capability. A model may be accurate and novel yet deliver little value if it addresses a low-priority problem or does not influence an important decision. Bojinov notes that technical teams can become disconnected from the company’s strategy when they work in silos. Organizations can improve alignment by embedding data scientists in business units or creating teams that mirror and collaborate closely with business functions. The project sponsor should be able to explain how the AI initiative advances a strategic priority and who will use the result.

Study instruction:
- For a proposed AI project, state the strategic objective, target business process, executive sponsor and decision that will change.

Key point:
- Start with business need rather than the technology available.
- Cross-functional collaboration helps technical teams understand strategy and context.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
5. Q: How can an organization measure the expected impact of an AI project before building it?
A: Show answer
The article recommends defining the intended business or operational outcome and estimating the size of the expected improvement. A useful structure is “if, then, by, because”: if the project is implemented, then a specified outcome or KPI will improve by a defined amount because of a stated mechanism. This forces the project team to articulate a testable hypothesis instead of relying on broad claims such as “improve productivity” or “use AI to gain insights.” The estimate need not be certain at selection, but its assumptions, baseline and measurement method should be explicit enough to compare projects and evaluate results later.

Study instruction:
- Write an if-then-by-because hypothesis for one AI proposal and identify the baseline data needed to test it.

Key point:
- A measurable hypothesis aligns teams around an observable result.
- A project’s impact should be assessed against a baseline and a plausible causal mechanism.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
6. Q: What is the “if, then, by, because” framework for AI project impact?
A: Show answer
The framework expresses a project hypothesis in four parts: if the AI project is implemented; then a specific business outcome or KPI should improve; by a defined amount; because a clear mechanism explains how the AI changes the process. For example, if an AI tool prioritizes customer leads, then sales conversion should rise by a specified percentage because sales staff will spend more time on higher-propensity prospects. The value of the statement is its falsifiability. Teams can test whether the tool changed the intended activity and whether the outcome moved as expected, rather than claiming success because the model was delivered.

Study instruction:
- Draft an if-then-by-because statement and distinguish the output metric from the business outcome metric.

Key point:
- Specify the causal path from AI use to measurable business result.
- The hypothesis provides a basis for prioritization and post-launch evaluation.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
7. Q: Why should leaders compare the impact and feasibility of candidate AI projects?
A: Show answer
Impact estimates whether the project could materially improve the organization’s results. Feasibility considers whether the organization has the problem definition, data, technology, skills and controls needed to build and operate it. A high-impact but low-feasibility project may reveal infrastructure or data gaps worth addressing. A low-impact but high-feasibility project may be suitable only as a cheap sandbox or learning exercise. Comparing both dimensions helps allocate scarce engineering, data and management resources and reduces the temptation to select projects because they use fashionable technology. It also makes portfolio trade-offs more transparent.

Study instruction:
- Place five candidate projects on an impact-feasibility matrix and explain which should be funded, investigated, piloted or declined.

Key point:
- High potential value is not enough if the project cannot be implemented reliably.
- Portfolio selection should account for both impact and feasibility.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
8. Q: What should organizations do with high-impact but low-feasibility AI projects?
A: Show answer
They should investigate the specific source of low feasibility rather than automatically abandoning the idea or approving it prematurely. The barrier may be missing data, poor data quality, insufficient infrastructure, lack of specialist skills, unclear ownership or unresolved ethical risk. Some of these issues can be addressed through investment that strengthens the organization for several future projects. The project should be re-estimated after the root cause is understood. If the gap is too expensive or cannot be resolved safely, the organization may need to defer or stop the project. The decision should be based on evidence about the constraint and the expected return from fixing it.

Study instruction:
- For a high-impact, low-feasibility project, identify the root cause, remediation cost, dependencies and a decision point for reassessment.

Key point:
- Low feasibility should trigger root-cause analysis, not wishful approval.
- Capability investments may benefit a portfolio of future projects.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
9. Q: When might a low-impact but high-feasibility AI project still be worth doing?
A: Show answer
The article suggests that a small project may be justified if it is inexpensive enough or provides a useful sandbox for testing infrastructure and technology. A limited pilot can build skills, reveal integration problems or establish a reusable data pipeline. However, a project should not be approved merely because it is easy to build. Leaders should specify what learning or reusable capability it is expected to produce and cap the cost. Otherwise, the organization may accumulate demonstrations with little business value while higher-priority problems remain unaddressed.

Study instruction:
- Assess a low-impact proposal and state what reusable learning or infrastructure benefit would justify its cost.

Key point:
- A small pilot can be useful when its learning value is explicit.
- Do not confuse technical ease with strategic importance.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
10. Q: How should a company decide whether AI should augment or replace a human task?
A: Show answer
The article recommends examining the cost of a prediction error. When an incorrect output has relatively limited consequences, automation may be appropriate. When errors could cause serious harm, financial loss or other major damage, AI should generally support rather than replace a human decision-maker. A product recommendation that is not relevant to a customer may have a low cost, while an incorrect medical diagnosis can be life-altering. The analysis should also consider whether the task requires judgment that is not captured in the data. The decision should be made at task level, not through a blanket policy for an entire job.

Study instruction:
- For five tasks, estimate the cost of an error and recommend automation, augmentation or human-only decision-making.

Key point:
- Error consequences help determine the degree of automation and oversight.
- High-impact decisions require stronger human responsibility and escalation.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
11. Q: What feasibility questions should be answered before approving an AI project?
A: Show answer
Bojinov identifies four areas: the nature of the problem, data availability, technological capability and skills, and ethical considerations. Leaders should ask whether AI is suited to the problem; whether the organization has enough relevant and fresh data; whether it can store, process and protect that data; and whether the required people and infrastructure are available. They should also consider bias, privacy and transparency before development begins. These checks prevent projects from moving into expensive model-building when a fundamental prerequisite is absent or a major risk has not been addressed.

Study instruction:
- Use the four feasibility areas to prepare a readiness assessment for a proposed project and assign an owner to each gap.

Key point:
- Feasibility includes problem fit, data, technology and ethical readiness.
- Unresolved feasibility gaps should be explicit before project approval.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
12. Q: What kinds of problems are well suited to AI, according to the article?
A: Show answer
AI is especially useful for well-formulated problems involving trend detection, pattern recognition, classification and prediction from available information. It is less suited to tasks whose essential requirements are emotional intelligence, ethical reasoning, nuanced context or moral judgment. A sales model may identify a promising prospect, for example, but closing a deal involves relationships, negotiation and situational understanding. The point is not that AI can never assist in complex work. It is that leaders should distinguish the predictive component from the human judgment and interaction required to complete the task.

Study instruction:
- Decompose a business problem into prediction, information processing, interpersonal judgment and execution, and identify which components AI can address.

Key point:
- AI needs a well-defined task and usable information.
- A prediction model does not automatically perform the contextual work around the prediction.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
13. Q: Why are data availability, quantity, freshness and quality all important to AI feasibility?
A: Show answer
An AI model learns patterns from the data available to it. If the data is incomplete, outdated, poorly defined or unrepresentative of the intended users, the model may make unreliable predictions or fail in production. Quantity alone does not solve these issues: a large dataset can still be biased, duplicated or irrelevant. The article uses lead prioritization as an example of a task that requires sufficiently comprehensive customer information. Leaders should assess the data needed for the intended decision, how it will be collected and updated, who owns it and whether its use is permitted.

Study instruction:
- Create a data-readiness checklist that covers relevance, coverage, quality, freshness, ownership, access and lawful use.

Key point:
- Model quality depends on suitable data, not merely large volumes.
- Data readiness should be assessed against the intended users and decision.

- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
14. Q: What technological capabilities are required to develop and operate an AI product?
A: Show answer
At a minimum, the organization needs reliable data storage and management, sufficient computing resources to train or run the system, integration capabilities and safeguards that protect data entering and leaving the model. The project may also require data engineering, software engineering, product design, security and deployment expertise. The exact infrastructure depends on the use case: an internal document assistant has different requirements from a real-time recommendation system or a safety-critical predictive model. Leaders should assess not only whether a model can be trained but whether it can be maintained, monitored and integrated into the business process at the required scale.

Study instruction:
- List the technical components and skills needed for one AI product from data ingestion through user interface, deployment and monitoring.

Key point:
- Feasibility includes production infrastructure and integration, not only model development.
- Operational support and security must be planned before deployment.

15. Q: Why should ethical issues be assessed during project selection rather than after development?
A: Show answer
Bias, privacy and transparency can shape the feasibility and design of an AI product. If a model is trained on unrepresentative data, it may systematically underperform for certain people. If sensitive information is collected or exposed without appropriate safeguards, the organization may create legal, financial and trust risks. If users cannot understand the system’s assumptions or limitations, they may misuse it or reject it. These issues can be expensive to retrofit after a product is developed. Early assessment allows leaders to modify the use case, obtain better data, design controls or stop a project that cannot be made acceptable.

Study instruction:
- For a proposed AI project, identify ethical risks at selection and specify what evidence or design changes are needed before approval.

Key point:
- Ethical risks affect project feasibility and should be addressed early.
- Retrofitting safeguards can be more costly than designing for them initially.

- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track

16. Q: How can bias in training data affect an AI product?
A: Show answer
Bias can arise when the data used to train a model does not represent the population or situations in which it will be used. The model may then produce less accurate or less fair outputs for some groups. Bojinov gives the example of a cancer-diagnosis model trained only on data from White men, which could perform poorly for women and people of color. This illustrates why data coverage must be assessed against the real intended population. Bias review should consider how data was collected, whose outcomes are represented, where missingness occurs and whether performance differs across relevant groups.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a data-representation review for one AI use case and identify groups or operating conditions that may be missing.

Key point:
- Training data can encode gaps that become model-performance disparities.
- Evaluate representativeness and subgroup performance before and after launch.

- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track

17. Q: What does privacy by design mean in an AI project?
A: Show answer
Privacy by design means embedding privacy protections into the system’s architecture, data practices and operational processes from the beginning rather than adding them after a problem occurs. A project should define what data is genuinely needed, how it will be collected, who may access it, how long it will be retained and how it will be protected. The article refers to privacy-by-design principles and differential privacy as possible tools. The correct safeguards depend on the data, use case and applicable law. Privacy should be treated as a design requirement and a continuing management responsibility.

Study instruction:
- Create a privacy-by-design checklist for one AI project covering data minimization, access, retention, protection and user rights.

Key point:
- Privacy controls should be designed into the project lifecycle.
- Collect and retain only what is justified for the intended use.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
18. Q: What is differential privacy, and how does it relate to AI data protection?
A: Show answer
Differential privacy is a mathematical framework for limiting what can be inferred about an individual from the output of a data analysis. It typically adds calibrated randomness so that the result does not reveal too much about whether a particular person’s data was included. It can help reduce privacy risks in aggregate analysis, but it is not a universal solution for every AI system. Its usefulness depends on the task, privacy budget, data access pattern and implementation. Organizations should assess it alongside access control, data minimization, security and legal requirements rather than treating one technique as a complete privacy programme.

Study instruction:
- Explain the purpose and limitations of differential privacy and identify a use case where aggregate analysis may benefit from it.

Key point:
- Differential privacy limits certain inferences about individuals from analytical outputs.
- It complements, but does not replace, broader privacy and security governance.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
19. Q: Why is transparency important when users rely on an AI product?
A: Show answer
Users need to understand what the system is intended to do, what data and assumptions influence its outputs, where it performs well and what limitations remain. Without this information, they may over-trust predictions, reject a useful tool or use it outside its intended context. Transparency does not necessarily mean exposing every line of code or the full mathematics of a complex model. It means providing explanations appropriate to the user’s role and decision, including the data scope, known limitations and routes for challenge or escalation. The required level of transparency should be considered during selection and design.

Study instruction:
- Draft a user-facing explanation of an AI product’s purpose, inputs, limitations, intended use and escalation route.

Key point:
- Users need enough information to interpret and challenge AI outputs responsibly.
- Transparency should be designed for the decision and the audience.

20. Q: What trade-off between explainability and predictive performance does the article discuss?
A: Show answer
The article notes that more transparent and easier-to-explain models may sometimes perform less well than more complex, less interpretable alternatives. This is not an absolute rule, and the trade-off depends on the model, task and explanation method. It does mean that leaders should decide what level of explainability is needed before development rather than discovering too late that the chosen model is difficult to justify. In high-impact contexts, explanation, contestability and auditability may be essential even if they require a different model or workflow. Model selection should consider both predictive performance and the governance needs of the decision.

Study instruction:
- For a high-impact use case, identify what users and reviewers must be able to understand and how that requirement affects model choice.

Key point:
- Explainability requirements should be defined at the beginning.
- The most accurate model is not automatically the most appropriate for every decision.

21. Q: What is an AI factory, as described in the article?
A: Show answer
An AI factory is a standardized and automated operating system for developing, evaluating and deploying AI products. It can include data pipelines, reusable tooling, experimentation platforms, model training and deployment processes, monitoring and governance. By standardizing repetitive work, an AI factory can speed development, make oversight more systematic and reduce the specialist skills required for routine stages. It depends on shared data infrastructure and clear processes. The concept is not just a software platform; it is a repeatable organizational capability that turns promising models into maintainable products.

Study instruction:
- Map the components of an AI factory and identify which stages should be standardized and which require project-specific judgment.

Key point:
- An AI factory makes AI development more repeatable and observable.
- Standardization improves speed and oversight when supported by shared data infrastructure.

22. Q: How can an AI factory improve model quality and development efficiency?
A: Show answer
An AI factory can automate data preparation, exploratory analysis, model training, evaluation and deployment tasks that would otherwise be repeated manually by individual teams. Reusable tools reduce inconsistencies and allow the organization to compare models under more standardized conditions. Bojinov reports an experiment in which providing data scientists with automated development tools improved final-model accuracy by 30 percent in that particular setting. That result should be treated as a case-specific finding, not a universal promise. The broader benefit is that automation can free specialists to focus on problem formulation, data quality, product integration and evaluation.

23. Q: Why should end users be involved throughout AI product development?
A: Show answer
Users understand the workflow, practical constraints and consequences that technical teams may not see. Their involvement helps ensure that the product solves a real problem, produces usable outputs and fits how work is performed. It also builds familiarity and trust before launch. If users are excluded, they may conclude that the developer does not understand their needs or is trying to replace their role. Involvement should begin during problem definition and continue through prototypes, testing, evaluation and rollout. User engagement is therefore both a product-quality control and an adoption strategy.

Study instruction:
- Create a user-engagement plan with representatives, feedback points and decisions that users can influence at each project stage.

Key point:
- User involvement improves relevance and reduces adoption risk.
- Feedback should influence product design, not be collected as a formality.

24. Q: What is the difference between an AI model and an AI product?
A: Show answer
An AI model generates a prediction, classification or other output. An AI product includes the model plus the software, data pipelines, user interface, integration, monitoring and operating processes needed to deliver that output in a real workflow. A model may perform well in a notebook but provide little value if users cannot access it, data cannot reach it reliably or results cannot be incorporated into decisions. The development stage therefore includes building the mechanism that transfers data to the model and routes outputs to the appropriate users or systems. Productization is where technical capability becomes usable organizational capability.

Study instruction:
- For one project, list everything required beyond the model before a user can rely on it in daily work.

Key point:
- A model is a component; a product is an integrated workflow capability.
- Integration and user experience are essential to business value.

25. Q: What is the purpose of evaluation before widespread AI adoption?
A: Show answer
Evaluation determines whether the developed product produces sufficient value under realistic conditions and whether its risks are acceptable. A model’s predictive accuracy during development does not guarantee that the product will improve user engagement, customer satisfaction, operational efficiency or financial results. Evaluation should therefore test the complete product and its interaction with existing systems and processes. It should also examine error patterns and unintended effects. Only after the evidence supports adoption should the organization invest in scaling use. If results are weak, evaluation should identify whether the problem lies in model performance, data, workflow, user experience or the chosen objective.

Study instruction:
- Define evaluation criteria for an AI product before rollout, including model metrics, business outcomes and unintended effects.

Key point:
- Evaluate the complete product in context, not just the model in isolation.
- Use evidence to decide whether to scale, revise or stop.

- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track

26. Q: What is A/B testing, and why is it useful for evaluating AI products?
A: Show answer
A/B testing compares an existing version of a product or process with an alternative version by assigning eligible users or cases to different conditions and measuring relevant outcomes. Random assignment helps distinguish the effect of the AI product from other differences between groups. The organization may measure engagement, satisfaction, conversion, errors or other outcomes tied to the project hypothesis. A/B testing is useful because it evaluates the effect of the deployed product rather than relying only on model metrics. It still requires careful experiment design, ethical safeguards, adequate sample size and monitoring for harmful effects.

Study instruction:
- Draft an A/B test plan with treatment, control, randomization unit, outcome measure, duration and stopping rules.

Key point:
- A/B testing estimates the causal impact of a product change under defined conditions.
- The experiment must measure business and user outcomes, not only model accuracy.

- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NBER: Generative AI at Work
27. Q: Why can an AI model with excellent development accuracy still fail after deployment?
A: Show answer
Development data may not represent real users, operating conditions or interactions with other products. A model may also create new feedback loops, change user behavior or alter incentives in ways that were not visible during offline testing. The article identifies four causes: interactions with the rest of the organization, unrepresentative training data, negative feedback loops and failure to adapt to changing real-world conditions. Deployment is therefore a new test environment. Evaluation should include operational pilots, ongoing monitoring and mechanisms for users to report problems before the system is scaled widely.

Study instruction:
- For a model with strong offline accuracy, identify four production risks that could reduce business value after deployment.

Key point:
- Offline performance is not proof of production effectiveness.
- Real-world interactions and user behavior can change the result.

28. Q: How can an AI product create conflicts with other products or business processes?
A: Show answer
An AI product changes one part of an operating system, and that change may affect other products, metrics or workflows. The article gives the example of a content recommendation system that increases engagement by displaying fewer ads, potentially reducing advertising revenue. A local metric improves while a broader business outcome deteriorates. Teams should therefore identify the full set of affected metrics and dependencies before deployment. Evaluation should consider both intended benefits and possible trade-offs across the organization, rather than optimizing the AI product’s immediate target in isolation.

Study instruction:
- Map the business processes and KPIs affected by one AI recommendation and identify possible trade-offs.

Key point:
- Local model success can conflict with broader organizational objectives.
- Evaluate cross-functional effects and unintended metric trade-offs.

29. Q: What is the risk of using training data that does not represent actual users?
A: Show answer
When deployed users or cases differ from the training population, the model may perform poorly for groups or conditions it has not learned adequately. A model may extrapolate beyond its experience, but its predictions may become unreliable. The article uses self-driving cars to illustrate the difficulty of covering the large variety of road conditions and events. Organizations should compare training data with actual users, investigate performance by relevant group or context and update the data when gaps are found. Representative evaluation is necessary because aggregate accuracy can hide poor performance for a subset of users.

Study instruction:
- Compare the model’s training population with its intended production population and identify important gaps or underrepresented conditions.

Key point:
- Training data coverage must match the population and context of use.
- Aggregate performance can conceal failures for particular groups or scenarios.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
30. Q: What is a negative feedback loop in an AI product?
A: Show answer
A negative feedback loop occurs when the system’s outputs change user behavior or the environment in ways that gradually undermine the system’s intended result. The article gives a recommendation example: if an algorithm repeatedly shows content similar to what users have already consumed, users may initially engage but later find the recommendations repetitive and leave. The model’s own outputs shape the future data it receives, creating a self-reinforcing pattern. Evaluation should therefore monitor long-term behavior, diversity of outcomes and user satisfaction rather than only short-term engagement or immediate prediction accuracy.

Study instruction:
- Identify an AI recommendation or scoring system and map how its outputs could influence future input data and user behavior.

Key point:
- AI outputs can change the environment that supplies future data.
- Monitor long-term effects and feedback loops, not just immediate metrics.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
31. Q: What is model drift, and why is it a management issue?
A: Show answer
Model drift occurs when the relationship between input data and the outcomes a model predicts changes over time, or when the production environment differs from the conditions under which the model was trained. Customer preferences, market conditions, regulations, product availability and user behavior can change. A model that once performed well may gradually become inaccurate or produce harmful outputs. Drift is a management issue because it requires monitoring, investigation, retraining or workflow changes after launch. The organization should define performance thresholds and data-quality indicators that trigger review instead of assuming that a deployed model remains reliable indefinitely.

Study instruction:
- Choose a predictive use case and identify which real-world changes could cause data or concept drift and how they would be detected.

Key point:
- Performance can deteriorate as data and context change.
- Ongoing monitoring and retraining decisions are part of product ownership.

- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
32. Q: Why should AI projects collect user feedback during experimentation?
A: Show answer
Users can reveal problems that model metrics do not capture, including confusing outputs, missing context, unfair treatment or workflow friction. Early feedback can help teams identify why a model works for some people but not others. The article describes a LinkedIn case in which performance differed across user groups because training data did not represent all users; adding personalization improved the product. Feedback therefore helps diagnose causes and refine the product before broad rollout. It should be collected systematically and combined with quantitative results so that anecdotal comments do not dominate the evaluation.

Study instruction:
- Design a feedback collection process for an AI pilot that combines structured surveys, interviews, usage logs and error reports.

Key point:
- User feedback can expose gaps hidden by aggregate metrics.
- Feedback should lead to testable product improvements.

33. Q: How can experimentation reduce the risk of an AI rollout?
A: Show answer
Experimentation limits the number of users exposed to a new system and allows the organization to compare outcomes before scaling. If a product creates negative effects, the team can detect them early and restrict exposure. The article also notes that statistical methods can monitor experiments and stop them when harmful effects become significant. An effective experiment should define outcome measures, safety indicators, stopping rules and responsibilities before launch. This approach does not remove all risk, but it makes innovation more controlled and creates evidence for decisions about scale or redesign.

Study instruction:
- Write a pilot safety plan that identifies exposure limits, harm indicators, alert thresholds and who can stop the experiment.

Key point:
- Controlled rollout can limit harm while generating evidence.
- Stopping rules and safety monitoring should be designed before the experiment begins.

34. Q: When might a traditional A/B test be impractical for an AI project?
A: Show answer
A/B testing may be difficult when the user base is very small, as with a product designed for a handful of enterprise customers or a limited number of employees. It can also be impractical when the AI performs global optimization, where decisions for one user affect the best outcome for everyone else. Splitting the population may produce an inferior or unrealistic system because each group operates with only part of the available information. In such cases, teams need alternative experimental designs that respect the system’s interdependence and the limited size of the user population.

Study instruction:
- Assess whether a proposed AI pilot has enough users and whether treatment and control groups can operate independently.

Key point:
- Experiment design must fit the structure and scale of the system.
- Naive randomization can distort results in small or globally optimized environments.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
35. Q: What are staggered-exposure experiments, and when can they be useful?
A: Show answer
Staggered-exposure experiments introduce the AI product to users or groups at different times rather than relying on a large simultaneous treatment and control group. The organization can compare outcomes before and after exposure while accounting for timing and other factors. Bojinov suggests this approach as one way to address small user populations, especially when there are too few participants for a conventional A/B test. The design must still address bias from time trends, seasonality and other events that occur during the rollout. Analysis should be planned with appropriate statistical expertise.

Study instruction:
- Design a staggered rollout for a small enterprise user group and identify timing effects that could bias the comparison.

Key point:
- Staggered exposure can create evidence when the population is small.
- Time-related confounding must be measured and addressed.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
36. Q: What is a switchback experiment, and why may it suit global optimization systems?
A: Show answer
A switchback experiment alternates the entire system between operating with and without the AI intervention across defined time periods. It can be useful when decisions must be optimized globally, such as supply-chain planning, workforce scheduling or product mix optimization. Splitting users into separate groups may lead each group to a different and inferior resource allocation, so the experiment instead compares system-wide outcomes under alternating conditions. The design must consider carryover effects, time trends, operational safety and whether the system can switch reliably. It is an alternative for particular interdependent systems, not a universal replacement for A/B testing.

Study instruction:
- Choose a globally optimized process and outline a switchback schedule, outcome measures and controls for time and carryover effects.

Key point:
- Switchback testing preserves system-wide optimization during evaluation.
- Experiment design must account for time effects and operational feasibility.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
37. Q: Why is product adoption a separate stage from evaluation?
A: Show answer
A product may demonstrate value in an experiment yet fail to become part of everyday work. Users may not understand the tool, trust its outputs or know who is responsible when it makes a mistake. The product may also conflict with existing routines or threaten valued tasks and professional identity. Evaluation establishes whether the product can create value; adoption establishes whether people will use it appropriately and consistently. The article’s LinkedIn example shows that a tool that dramatically reduced analysis time attracted few users until the team addressed trust and communication problems. Adoption must therefore be designed and managed, not assumed after launch.

Study instruction:
- Identify three reasons an AI product might pass a technical pilot but still be rejected by its intended users.

Key point:
- A successful experiment does not guarantee real-world adoption.
- User trust, workflow fit and accountability determine whether value is realized.

38. Q: What are the three pillars of trust in an AI product?
A: Show answer
Bojinov identifies trust in the algorithm, the developer and the process. Trust in the algorithm concerns whether the product works, is sufficiently accurate and behaves as expected. Trust in the developer concerns whether users believe the people or organization behind the product understand their needs and have no hidden agenda. Trust in the process concerns whether the workflow gives users appropriate authority, protection and guidance when the AI is wrong. A failure in any one pillar can undermine adoption. The organization should therefore diagnose why users hesitate rather than assuming they simply need more training.

Study instruction:
- For an AI product, list the concerns users may have under each trust pillar and identify how each can be addressed.

Key point:
- Trust depends on model performance, developer credibility and process design.
- Adoption programmes must diagnose the actual source of distrust.

39. Q: How can organizations build trust in an AI algorithm?
A: Show answer
They should demonstrate that the model works for the intended task, explain its assumptions and show evidence from relevant real-world scenarios. Users may want to know whether the model is biased, which data was used and why the output makes sense for their work. The article recommends starting with efficacy and then addressing harder questions about transparency and explanation. Educational materials, representative examples, performance breakdowns and clear limitations can help users calibrate trust. The goal is not to persuade people to accept every output, but to help them understand when the tool is reliable and when additional review is required.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a user guide that explains the model’s purpose, data scope, performance evidence, limitations and examples of appropriate use.

Key point:
- Trust should be earned through relevant performance evidence and clear explanations.
- Users need to know both where the model works and where it may fail.

40. Q: How can trust in the developer be damaged?
A: Show answer
Trust in the developer can be damaged when intended users were excluded from the design process, believe the tool is intended to replace their roles or conclude that the development team does not understand their needs. Even a technically effective product may be rejected if employees see it as an imposed system designed without their input. The article’s LinkedIn example shows that users outside the initial development group assumed the tool was customized for only a few problems and would not work for them. Developers can address this by involving representative users, being transparent about the product’s purpose and demonstrating flexibility across different use cases.

Study instruction:
- Create a stakeholder engagement plan that gives intended users a role in requirements, testing and product decisions.

Key point:
- User involvement builds confidence that the product addresses real needs.

41. Q: Why must organizations define responsibility when an AI recommendation is wrong?
A: Show answer
Users need to know whether they are expected to follow the recommendation, challenge it or accept responsibility for the resulting decision. If authority and accountability are unclear, employees may avoid the product or follow it mechanically to protect themselves. The article recommends clear guidelines for resolving disagreements between AI and users when the system augments human judgment. When AI automates a process, stronger protections should identify and correct mistakes. A sound governance model defines who can override outputs, who approves high-risk actions, how decisions are documented and who owns remediation when harm occurs.

Study instruction:
- Draft an accountability matrix for an AI-assisted decision, including override authority, approval, documentation and incident ownership.

Key point:
- Decision rights and accountability must be clear before adoption.

42. Q: What is the purpose of a certification board or independent review group in an AI workflow?
A: Show answer
A review group can examine AI-generated analyses or recommendations before they are shared or used for consequential decisions. In the LinkedIn example, a certification board reviewed results and recommendations, making responsibility for mistakes discovered after certification rest with the review board rather than individual users. The broader principle is to establish a trusted process for quality assurance and accountability. A review board should have appropriate expertise, independence, clear criteria and authority to reject or request changes. It should not become a rubber stamp or a bottleneck that prevents routine work from progressing.

Study instruction:
- Design a review board charter with scope, membership, review criteria, escalation authority and documentation requirements.

- Independent review can strengthen trust and clarify responsibility.

43. Q: How should an organization respond when users believe an AI product is a black box?
A: Show answer
The organization should explain the system at a level appropriate to the user’s role. That may include the purpose of the model, major assumptions, the kinds of data used, examples of outputs, known limitations and the process for challenging a result. It may also need to show how the product fits the user’s workflow and who is responsible for decisions. The article describes a tool that was intentionally made simple but became opaque to potential users; educational materials describing its workings helped restore trust. Simplicity of interface should not mean opacity about purpose, limitations or accountability.

Study instruction:

44. Q: What should an organization do after an AI product has been adopted?
A: Show answer
Management continues after launch. The organization must provide engineering support, fix bugs, monitor performance, maintain data pipelines, respond to changing user needs and audit for unintended consequences, ethical issues and security flaws. The product may degrade as customer behavior, markets or data change. Management therefore requires assigned owners, monitoring thresholds, incident handling, retraining decisions and a continuous improvement cycle. A launch is not the end of the project; it is the beginning of an ongoing operational responsibility. The organization should also reassess whether the product still serves the intended purpose and whether its risks remain acceptable.

Study instruction:
- Write a post-launch operating plan with product owner, technical support, monitoring metrics, incident escalation and periodic audit dates.

45. Q: Why should AI models be monitored for changes in performance after deployment?
A: Show answer
Models operate in changing environments. Customer preferences, market conditions, policies, available products and user behavior can shift so that the training data no longer reflects current conditions. A model may still produce outputs while its accuracy and business value decline. Organizations should monitor prediction quality, input distributions, error rates and downstream outcomes, then investigate material changes. Monitoring helps identify when the model requires new data, retraining, recalibration or a change in how its outputs are used. Without monitoring, a once-effective product can quietly become unreliable.

Study instruction:
- Select monitoring indicators for one deployed model and define thresholds that trigger investigation or retraining review.

Key point:
- Model performance can degrade as the environment changes.
- Monitoring should detect both technical drift and deterioration in business outcomes.
