- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
46. Q: What is the difference between data drift and concept drift?
A: Show answer
Data drift refers to changes in the distribution of input data between development and production. Concept drift refers to changes in the relationship between inputs and the outcome the model is trying to predict. For example, the characteristics of customers may change, or the same customer characteristics may lead to different purchasing behavior because market conditions have shifted. Both can reduce model performance, but they may require different responses. Data monitoring can identify changing inputs, while outcome monitoring and periodic re-evaluation help detect changes in predictive relationships. The response may involve new data, retraining, revised features or a changed business process.

Study instruction:
- For one predictive model, identify examples of input-distribution change and changes in the relationship between inputs and outcomes.

Key point:
- Input data can change, and the world can change the meaning of that data.
- Monitoring should examine both data patterns and outcome performance.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
47. Q: Why should organizations retrain models on fresh data carefully rather than automatically?
A: Show answer
Fresh data can help a model reflect current conditions, but retraining is not always the correct response to a performance change. The new data may be noisy, biased or reflect a temporary shock. The organization should first investigate the cause of degradation, verify data quality and consider whether the model’s features, objective or operating context have changed. Retraining also requires validation to ensure that the new model does not create other errors or fairness problems. Because retraining can be expensive, monitoring should help identify when the benefit justifies the cost and when alternative corrective actions are more appropriate.

Study instruction:
- Build a retraining decision tree that includes drift evidence, data-quality checks, validation, cost and approval.

Key point:
- Retraining should follow diagnosis and validation, not be triggered blindly by new data.
- A new model must be tested before replacing the production version.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
48. Q: What is an AI audit, and why should it continue after deployment?
A: Show answer
An AI audit is a structured review of how a system performs and affects users, processes and stakeholders. It can examine intended and unintended consequences, ethical issues, security weaknesses, data practices, fairness and compliance with organizational requirements. The article argues that audits are essential because AI can change behavior and outcomes in ways that were not anticipated during development. A product may be technically effective but create indirect effects on access to opportunities, service quality or employee behavior. Audits should be risk-based, documented and repeated as the system or its environment changes.

Study instruction:
- Prepare an audit scope for a deployed AI system covering performance, fairness, privacy, security, user impact and governance.

Key point:
- Post-deployment audits detect effects that initial testing may miss.
- Audit frequency should reflect the system’s risk and rate of change.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
49. Q: What unintended effect does the LinkedIn People You May Know example illustrate?
A: Show answer
The article refers to research showing that a connection-recommendation algorithm could affect users’ career prospects because social networks influence access to information about job opportunities. A system designed to suggest professional connections can therefore have effects beyond user engagement or network size. It may change who receives information, who connects with whom and whose opportunities expand. The example illustrates why audits should consider second-order social and economic effects, not just the metric the model was designed to optimize. Beneficial effects can occur, but the same mechanism could also disadvantage some users.

Study instruction:
- Identify the intended metric for a recommendation system and map possible downstream effects on access, opportunity and user behavior.

Key point:
- AI products can influence outcomes beyond their immediate objective.
- Audits should examine indirect effects on users and connected systems.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
50. Q: How does the NIST AI Risk Management Framework support the lifecycle approach in the article?
A: Show answer
NIST’s AI Risk Management Framework organizes risk work around Govern, Map, Measure and Manage functions. Governance establishes accountability and policies; mapping clarifies context and potential impacts; measurement evaluates performance and risk; management prioritizes and responds to identified risks. These functions are ongoing and cross-cutting rather than a one-time approval. They complement Bojinov’s project stages by ensuring that risk identification, testing, monitoring and responsibility continue throughout development and operation. Organizations can use the framework to make lifecycle controls systematic without treating any single checklist as a substitute for judgment.

Study instruction:
- Map the five AI project stages to NIST’s Govern, Map, Measure and Manage functions and identify where each function applies.

Key point:
- NIST provides a continuous risk-management structure across the AI lifecycle.
- Governance should connect context, testing and management actions.

Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
51. Q: How can organizations use AI project gates to prevent poor projects from progressing?
A: Show answer
A project gate is a formal decision point at which evidence is reviewed before more resources or exposure are committed. Selection gates assess strategic value and feasibility; development gates check data, model performance and product integration; evaluation gates test real-world value and harm; adoption gates assess user readiness and trust; management gates confirm ongoing performance and risk controls. The gate should define minimum evidence, a decision owner and possible outcomes such as proceed, revise, pause or stop. Gates are most effective when they encourage honest learning rather than becoming bureaucratic signatures that teams complete automatically.

Study instruction:
- Create five stage gates for an AI project with required evidence, decision authority and stop-or-revise criteria.

Key point:
- Stage gates make lifecycle accountability explicit.
- A gate should test evidence, not merely confirm that a document exists.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
52. Q: What should leaders do when an AI project performs well technically but fails to gain user adoption?
A: Show answer
They should investigate which trust pillar or workflow issue is blocking use. Users may doubt the algorithm’s quality, the developer’s intentions or the process for handling mistakes. The product may also be poorly integrated, too difficult to use or disconnected from the tasks people actually perform. Leaders should interview nonusers and skeptical users, observe how work is done and test changes to explanation, training, role design and accountability. A technically successful product should not be forced into use without understanding the concern. Adoption failure is evidence that the product or its operating environment needs attention.

Study instruction:
- Create an adoption-recovery plan that diagnoses algorithm, developer and process trust separately.

Key point:
- Low adoption is often a signal about trust, product fit or process design.
- Talk to nonusers before assuming that more training is the only solution.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
53. Q: How can organizations distinguish an AI project failure from a valuable learning result?
A: Show answer
A project that is stopped after evidence shows that the problem is unsuitable, data is inadequate or risks are unacceptable may have prevented a larger loss. Learning is valuable when the organization documents what was tested, why the hypothesis failed and what reusable capability or insight was gained. Failure becomes wasteful when teams repeat the same mistake, conceal poor results or continue spending because of sunk costs. Leaders should define decision criteria before the pilot and reward honest reporting. The goal is not to make every project succeed, but to make the portfolio learn faster and allocate resources more effectively.

Study instruction:
- Write a post-project review template that records the original hypothesis, evidence, decision, lessons and reusable assets.

Key point:
- Stopping an unviable project can be a successful management decision.
- Document lessons so the organization does not repeat avoidable failures.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
54. Q: What should a practical AI project scorecard include?
A: Show answer
A useful scorecard should include strategic alignment, expected impact, feasibility, data readiness, technical performance, user adoption, operational outcomes and risk indicators. It should distinguish model metrics from business measures and include qualitative evidence such as user feedback and observed workflow changes. After deployment, it should track drift, incidents, fairness or quality concerns, support costs and the continuing value of the product. Each indicator needs a clear definition, baseline, owner and review frequency. A single accuracy score or project completion date cannot show whether the AI product is delivering sustained business value.

Study instruction:
- Build a scorecard with at least eight metrics and specify the data source, owner, baseline and decision it informs.

Key point:
- Measure technical quality, business impact, adoption and ongoing risk separately.
- Metrics need definitions, owners and decision thresholds.

Source links:
- HBR: Keep Your AI Projects on Track
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
55. Q: What are the most important lessons from “Keep Your AI Projects on Track”?
A: Show answer
The article’s central lesson is that an AI project succeeds only when it moves through a disciplined lifecycle. Leaders must select work that is strategically aligned and feasible, develop a reliable product, evaluate it in real conditions, earn user trust and manage it continuously. Bias, privacy, transparency, data quality and accountability must be considered from the beginning rather than added after problems emerge. Experimentation should test business outcomes and limit exposure to harm. After launch, teams need engineering support, performance monitoring and audits for unintended consequences. AI should be managed as a product and organizational capability, not as a one-off model-building exercise.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a one-page summary of the five stages and the most important control or decision at each stage.

Key point:
- A successful AI initiative requires disciplined lifecycle management.
- Strategic value, technical quality, user trust and ongoing governance must work together.

Process Management and AI: Designing, Improving, and Governing End-to-End Workflows
1. Q: What is process management, and why is it central to organizational performance?
A: Show answer
Process management is the disciplined practice of understanding how connected tasks, decisions, information, and resources combine to produce an outcome, then improving that system as a whole. It can apply to an individual's work, a departmental activity, a cross-functional workflow, or a process extending across organizational boundaries. Its value comes from looking beyond isolated tasks and examining the result delivered to a customer. Well-managed processes can reduce errors, delays, rework, unnecessary activity, and avoidable cost while improving reliability and service. The chapter emphasizes that process management is not simply documenting procedures; it is a continuing management approach that aligns people, data, technology, and decisions around performance.

Study instruction:
- Choose one recurring work process and map its inputs, activities, handoffs, outputs, customers, and performance problems.

Key point:
- Process management improves the connected system that produces an outcome, not just individual tasks.
- Customer value and measurable performance should guide improvement priorities.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
2. Q: How does artificial intelligence complement rather than replace process management?
A: Show answer
AI can improve particular activities within a process by predicting outcomes, classifying information, generating content, recommending actions, or automating routine decisions. Process management supplies the wider operating design in which those capabilities must work: the sequence of activities, the people responsible, the data exchanged, the controls applied, and the outcome measured. The chapter warns that AI commonly supports a task or subprocess rather than automatically transforming an entire end-to-end workflow. Organizations therefore need process ownership and coordination to connect separate AI applications. AI is most useful when it is introduced into a clearly understood process with reliable data, explicit decision rights, and a way to evaluate whether the resulting workflow performs better.

Study instruction:
- Identify one process and distinguish the activities that AI could assist from the decisions that still require accountable human ownership.

Key point:
- AI is an enabler inside a managed process, not a substitute for process design.
- End-to-end improvement requires coordination across multiple tasks and functions.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
3. Q: Why are process management and technology mutually reinforcing?
A: Show answer
A well-defined process gives technology a stable purpose, usable inputs, clear decision points, and measurable outcomes. This makes it easier to deploy an algorithm or automation tool and determine whether it creates value. In the other direction, technology can make process improvement faster and more scalable by capturing events, revealing bottlenecks, supporting analysis, and automating repeatable work. Integrated processes also encourage departments to use common data definitions and share information, which can improve the quantity and quality of data available for analytics and machine learning. The relationship is therefore reciprocal: process discipline improves technology implementation, while suitable technology strengthens the ability to understand, operate, and improve the process.

Study instruction:
- For a proposed technology investment, document the process problem, required data, workflow change, accountable owner, and success measure before selecting a tool.

Key point:
- Technology creates more value when the process it supports is understood and governed.
- Better-integrated processes can generate more consistent data for analytics and AI.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- Microsoft: What Is Process Mining?
4. Q: Why do isolated AI use cases rarely transform end-to-end performance by themselves?
A: Show answer
An end-to-end process contains multiple linked activities, handoffs, decisions, and exceptions. An AI tool may accelerate one activity while leaving upstream data errors, downstream queues, unclear approvals, or poor coordination untouched. A faster subprocess can even move the bottleneck elsewhere if the surrounding workflow is not redesigned. The chapter therefore argues that organizations must connect multiple relevant use cases and align them with a coherent process objective. Leaders should assess how each application changes the whole flow, not only the local task it automates. They should also check whether the technology creates new handoffs, review work, or exception queues that offset its apparent productivity gain.

Study instruction:
- Draw an end-to-end process map and mark where each proposed AI use case affects upstream inputs, downstream work, and customer outcomes.

Key point:
- Local automation does not guarantee end-to-end process improvement.
- Connected use cases must be evaluated against the complete workflow and its bottlenecks.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
5. Q: What productivity paradox does the chapter identify in organizational technology investment?
A: Show answer
The chapter highlights the gap between large investments in information technology and the productivity gains organizations can clearly demonstrate. It recalls the observation that computers can be visible throughout business while productivity statistics show limited improvement. A technology project may deliver new systems, data, or analytical capability without changing the underlying process, decision rights, incentives, or work practices that determine results. Organizations may also measure implementation milestones rather than operational outcomes and may lack credible return-on-investment evidence beyond basic productivity indicators. The practical lesson is to connect technology spending to a defined process problem and track whether cycle time, error rates, service, cost, capacity, or other meaningful outcomes actually improve.

Study instruction:
- Review a recent technology project and compare its implementation deliverables with measured changes in process performance and customer outcomes.

Key point:
- Technology expenditure is not itself evidence of productivity improvement.
- Benefits should be demonstrated through process-level outcomes and credible measurement.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
6. Q: How do departmental silos create inefficiency across an end-to-end process?
A: Show answer
A silo occurs when a department optimizes its own targets and systems without understanding how its work affects the next stage. The chapter illustrates this with sales entering incomplete or inaccurate order information and operations spending time correcting it under pressure. Operations may meet its own targets by cleaning the data, while the organization continues to incur avoidable rework because the root cause remains in sales. Each department can appear to perform adequately even though the total process is inefficient. Process management exposes these handoff failures, establishes shared accountability, and redirects improvement toward the source of errors rather than repeatedly compensating for them downstream.

Study instruction:
- Trace a recurring error to the department or handoff where it originates, and identify who bears the rework cost.

Key point:
- Local performance measures can hide end-to-end inefficiency.
- Cross-functional analysis should correct root causes instead of shifting work between departments.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- Microsoft: What Is Process Mining?
7. Q: How should an organization choose its first process-management initiative?
A: Show answer
An organization with limited process-management experience should usually begin with one or two processes that are important to performance and feasible to improve. The chapter recommends a focused starting point rather than attempting to redesign every workflow simultaneously. Candidate processes should have meaningful customer or operational consequences, visible pain points, accessible data, leaders willing to cooperate, and a realistic opportunity to demonstrate improvement. A manageable first initiative can build skills, reveal data and governance weaknesses, and create evidence that process thinking is useful. Selection should also consider whether the process crosses several departments, because that can produce significant benefits but requires stronger sponsorship and change management.

Study instruction:
- Score three candidate processes against strategic importance, customer impact, current problems, data availability, ownership, and implementation feasibility.

Key point:
- Start with a strategically important process that can be managed and measured.
- Early success should build capability for wider process improvement.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
8. Q: Why is order-to-cash a useful starting point for process improvement?
A: Show answer
Order-to-cash covers the connected activities from a customer's order through fulfillment, delivery, invoicing, and receipt of payment. It is a useful starting point because it links customer experience with operational execution and cash management. Delays or errors in order entry can affect inventory allocation, delivery promises, invoicing accuracy, receivables, and the timing of cash collection. It also crosses functions such as sales, operations, shipping, and finance, making it a practical setting for learning end-to-end ownership. The chapter uses order-to-cash to explain how process management can combine customer needs, process mapping, performance measures, technology, redesign, implementation, and monitoring.

Study instruction:
- Map the full order-to-cash sequence and identify the event that starts the process, the event that completes it, and the principal handoffs.

Key point:
- Order-to-cash connects customer service, operational delivery, billing, and cash.
- Its cross-functional nature makes it a useful test of end-to-end process management.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
9. Q: What is a process owner, and what responsibilities should the role carry?
A: Show answer
A process owner is the person accountable for coordinating and improving the performance of an end-to-end process across the departments that contribute to it. The role is not simply a senior sponsor or a document custodian. The owner should maintain a view of the full workflow, convene process managers, clarify priorities, resolve or escalate cross-functional barriers, review performance, and ensure that improvement actions are implemented. The chapter notes that the role may be new and that the owner may initially have limited formal authority. Consequently, influence, credibility, customer focus, and the ability to align people around organizational outcomes are important qualifications.

Study instruction:
- Draft a process-owner role description that specifies decision rights, performance accountability, escalation routes, and relationships with line managers.

Key point:
- The process owner is accountable for the whole process, not one department's tasks.
- Influence and cross-functional credibility are essential when formal authority is limited.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
10. Q: How should cross-functional process managers be selected?
A: Show answer
Process managers should come from the departments that perform or materially influence the process. For order-to-cash, the chapter identifies functions such as sales, operations, shipping, and finance. Representatives need enough operational knowledge to explain how work is actually performed, enough standing to speak for their areas, and enough organizational perspective to advocate for the end-to-end result. Selection should not be limited to seniority: practical knowledge of exceptions, data, customer needs, and handoffs is often critical. The team should have a clear mandate, time to participate, access to relevant information, and a route for escalating decisions that exceed its authority.

Study instruction:
- Create a stakeholder map showing each process stage, the department responsible, the representative required, and the decisions that person can make.

Key point:
- The team must represent the functions that jointly deliver the process.
- Members need both local expertise and an organization-wide perspective.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
11. Q: How should conflicts between a process owner and a line manager be handled?
A: Show answer
Conflicts arise because line managers often prioritize their department's targets and reporting relationships, while process owners focus on customer outcomes and the performance of the whole workflow. The chapter illustrates this with a salesperson encouraged to promise a short delivery time to meet a sales quota even though inventory constraints make a longer date more realistic. Organizations should clarify decision rights before such conflicts occur: which commitments are governed by process standards, which decisions remain with line management, and who resolves trade-offs. Senior leaders must reinforce that process performance is a shared organizational responsibility, not an optional request that can be ignored when local targets conflict.

Study instruction:
- Identify a likely conflict between a departmental KPI and an end-to-end customer commitment, then define an escalation and decision rule.

Key point:
- Unresolved authority conflicts can undermine process ownership.
- Leadership must align local incentives with the organization's end-to-end priorities.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
12. Q: Who are process customers, and how should their needs be identified?
A: Show answer
Process customers are the people or groups that receive value from the process or depend on its outputs. They may be external customers who purchase products or services, or internal customers who use the output to perform another activity. Process managers should ask who receives the result, what they need, which outcomes matter most, and how well the current process delivers them. The chapter stresses that customer identification can reveal gaps that are not visible from an internal departmental perspective. Customer needs should be supported by evidence such as service requests, complaints, delivery performance, retention patterns, interviews, and feedback rather than relying only on assumptions made by process teams.

Study instruction:
- List the primary and secondary customers of a process and record their required outcomes, service expectations, and evidence sources.

Key point:
- Customer needs define the value the process is meant to deliver.
- Internal assumptions should be tested against customer evidence.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
13. Q: How should internal and external customer priorities be reconciled?
A: Show answer
A process may serve several customers whose needs differ. External customers may value reliable delivery and product quality, while the organization needs accurate transactions and timely cash collection. Internal stakeholders such as marketing or sustainability teams may value dependable service data or reduced environmental impact. The chapter presents process management as a way to make these priorities explicit and align work with them. Teams should distinguish essential requirements from preferences, identify conflicts, assess trade-offs, and agree on measures that reflect the process's overall purpose. Where priorities cannot all be maximized, accountable leaders should make the trade-off transparent rather than allowing individual departments to decide independently.

Study instruction:
- Build a customer-priority matrix and identify where service, cost, cash, quality, and sustainability objectives may conflict.

Key point:
- Processes can have several legitimate customers and competing expectations.
- Explicit priorities prevent hidden trade-offs from emerging at departmental handoffs.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
14. Q: What should a current-state process map contain?
A: Show answer
A current-state map should show the major activities in sequence, the physical movement of goods or services, the creation and use of information, decision points, handoffs, delays, and the systems involved. It should describe how work is actually performed, including common variations and exceptions, rather than presenting only the official procedure. The chapter recommends a high-level flowchart as a starting point and highlights the value of describing interfaces between departments. A useful map should identify who performs each step, what information is required, what output is produced, and where responsibility changes. It becomes a shared factual basis for diagnosis and redesign.

Study instruction:
- Map one process using swimlanes and include activities, decisions, information flows, physical flows, systems, queues, and exceptions.

Key point:
- A current-state map must represent actual work, not only documented policy.
- Handoffs and information flows are as important as individual tasks.

- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- Microsoft: What Is Process Mining?
15. Q: Why are departmental interfaces and process âwhite spacesâ especially important?
A: Show answer
A white space is an area between departments where responsibility is unclear or where no single function sees the complete handoff. These gaps often contain waiting, repeated data entry, incomplete instructions, inconsistent standards, and unresolved exceptions. Because the work does not sit clearly within one department, it may be excluded from local performance reviews and remain invisible to managers. The chapter recommends examining interfaces early because delays and errors frequently originate there. Teams should assign responsibility for each handoff, define the information required, set service expectations, and establish a route for resolving incomplete or disputed work.

Study instruction:
- Select three handoffs in a process map and specify the sender, receiver, required data, acceptance criteria, and escalation path for each.

Key point:
- Unowned interfaces are common sources of delay and rework.
- Every important handoff needs clear inputs, outputs, and accountability.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
16. Q: What is process mining, and what information does it use?
A: Show answer
Process mining analyzes event data recorded by information systems to reconstruct and examine how a process actually operates. Typical evidence comes from application logs and audit trails in systems such as enterprise resource planning or customer relationship management platforms. The analysis can show the sequence of activities, the time between events, recurring process variants, bottlenecks, and deviations from an expected workflow. The chapter contrasts this capability with earlier approaches that relied heavily on manual mapping workshops. Process mining does not automatically explain every cause or determine the correct redesign; it supplies evidence that process managers can investigate alongside interviews, operational knowledge, and customer feedback.

Study instruction:
- Identify the systems that record events for a selected process and check whether records contain a usable case identifier, activity, timestamp, and relevant attributes.

Key point:
- Process mining uses system event data to reveal actual process execution.
- Data analysis should be combined with operational investigation before changes are approved.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- Microsoft: What Is Process Mining?
17. Q: How do process mining and task mining differ?
A: Show answer
Process mining examines event records across a broader workflow to reveal how process instances move through multiple activities and systems. Task mining generally focuses on detailed work performed within a smaller activity or subprocess, often to identify repetitive actions suitable for automation or simplification. The chapter describes process mining as useful for understanding end-to-end performance and identifying the major problems that redesign should address, while task mining can help improve smaller activities. The distinction matters because automating a desktop task will not necessarily resolve a wider process problem such as an unclear approval, a missing upstream field, or a delay between departments.

Study instruction:
- Classify three observed problems as end-to-end process issues or task-level issues and explain which evidence would help investigate each.

Key point:
- Process mining focuses on the broader workflow; task mining examines narrower work activities.
- Choose the analysis level that matches the problem being solved.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- Microsoft: What Is Process Mining?
18. Q: How can process mining help determine which problems should be redesigned first?
A: Show answer
Process mining can reveal where work waits, where cases follow unexpected paths, where rework occurs, and which process variants consume disproportionate time or resources. Managers can use these patterns to distinguish frequent problems from costly or high-risk exceptions. The chapter describes PepsiCo using process mining to identify major issues, including a high order-rejection rate after a new system was installed. The analysis helped the company focus on a measurable problem rather than redesigning based on anecdote alone. Teams should validate the data, investigate root causes, estimate customer and financial impact, and prioritize problems that combine material consequences with a realistic opportunity for improvement.

Study instruction:
- Rank observed bottlenecks by frequency, delay, rework, customer impact, risk, and controllability, then verify the top cause with process participants.

Key point:
- Process mining can turn vague complaints into measurable improvement priorities.
- The data identifies where to investigate; process owners decide what to change.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- Microsoft: What Is Process Mining?
19. Q: What role do process-performance measures and targets play in improvement?
A: Show answer
Measures describe how the current process performs, while targets state the level of performance the organization intends to achieve. They allow teams to compare the current state with the desired outcome, identify trade-offs, and determine whether a redesign has worked. The chapter emphasizes establishing measures before or during redesign so that teams can use baseline evidence to shape the future workflow. Measures should reflect the purpose of the process and the needs of its customers rather than simply counting departmental activity. Targets also need clear definitions, data owners, reporting frequency, and rules for interpreting exceptions so that people do not optimize one indicator at the expense of the whole process.

Study instruction:
- Define a baseline, target, data source, owner, review frequency, and balancing measure for each key process indicator.

Key point:
- Measures make process performance visible and comparable.
- Targets should reinforce the desired end-to-end outcome, not local activity alone.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
20. Q: Which measures are especially useful for an order-to-cash process?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies end-to-end cycle time, customer satisfaction, data accuracy, and process efficiency as important order-to-cash measures. Cycle time should cover the full period from order placement to receipt of payment, not only the time spent by one department. Customer satisfaction indicates whether delivery and service meet expectations. Data accuracy helps identify errors that cause rejection, correction, or disputes. Efficiency can include labor, rework, resource utilization, and cost. Organizations may add measures for order completeness, on-time delivery, invoice accuracy, dispute resolution, and receivables aging where these are relevant to their operating model. A balanced set prevents improvements in speed from hiding poorer quality or customer experience.

Study instruction:
- Design an order-to-cash scorecard with at least one measure for time, quality, customer outcome, and cost or resource use.

Key point:
- End-to-end cycle time should be paired with quality and customer measures.
- A balanced scorecard reduces the risk of optimizing one stage at another's expense.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
21. Q: How should realistic process-improvement targets be established?
A: Show answer
Targets should reflect the performance gap that matters, the customer's needs, available evidence, operational constraints, and the organization's capacity to implement change. The chapter contrasts earlier reengineering efforts that sometimes pursued arbitrary tenfold improvements with lean and Six Sigma approaches that often emphasized more incremental goals. Neither a dramatic nor a modest target is automatically appropriate. Teams should use baseline data, process analysis, customer expectations, resource constraints, and tested improvement opportunities to set an ambition that is meaningful and credible. Targets should also be reviewed as new information emerges, with any changes documented so that performance is not made to look better simply by moving the goalposts.

Study instruction:
- Calculate the baseline for a process measure and justify a proposed target using customer needs, root-cause evidence, and implementation capacity.

Key point:
- Targets should be evidence-based rather than selected for dramatic effect.
- Ambition must be balanced with feasibility and meaningful customer value.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
22. Q: How can cycle-time analysis distinguish actual work from waiting time?
A: Show answer
Total elapsed time includes both active work and periods when a case waits between activities. The chapter gives an example of a service process that took about 90 days, even though the actual work required roughly 10 days and the remaining 80 days were spent waiting. Better coordination removed 60 days of delay. This illustrates why managers should not assume that adding staff or automating a task is the main solution. They should examine queues, approval delays, missing information, scheduling constraints, and handoff rules. Reducing waiting time may improve customer experience and accelerate cash or service delivery without requiring a proportional increase in productive labor.

Study instruction:
- Break a process's elapsed time into active processing, queue time, approval time, and rework time, then identify the largest avoidable delay.

Key point:
- Elapsed cycle time can be dominated by waiting rather than work.
- Coordination and handoff improvements may produce large gains without adding capacity.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- Microsoft: What Is Process Mining?
23. Q: How should organizations select technology enablers for a process?
A: Show answer
Technology should be selected after the process problem, customer need, data requirements, and intended control environment are understood. The chapter identifies several possible enablers: robotic process automation for repetitive routine work, generative AI for language and content tasks, traditional machine learning for prediction and optimization, Internet-of-Things sensors for equipment data, blockchain for tracking certain supply-chain movements, and business analytics for decision support. These tools solve different problems and should not be treated as interchangeable. Selection should consider integration, data quality, security, cost, reliability, exception handling, employee skills, vendor dependence, and the ability to measure benefits. A technology portfolio should support a redesigned workflow rather than drive the process design by itself.

Study instruction:
- For one process problem, compare at least two technology options against task fit, data needs, control requirements, integration effort, and measurable benefit.

Key point:
- Match the technology to the specific process need and risk.
- Tool selection should follow process analysis, not replace it.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
24. Q: Where can generative AI support an order-to-cash process?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes potential uses such as drafting contracts, helping customers place more accurate orders, and communicating delivery changes. Generative AI may also summarize unstructured customer comments from calls, emails, or social media so that process teams can detect recurring service problems. These applications can reduce manual effort and help staff work with large volumes of language-based information. However, generated content should be checked for factual accuracy, policy compliance, tone, and completeness before it creates a contractual commitment or changes customer expectations. Organizations should define which messages may be sent automatically, which require approval, what data may be used, and how errors or customer disputes are escalated.

Study instruction:
- Select one customer communication use case and specify approved data, output checks, human review thresholds, and an error-correction route.

Key point:
- Generative AI can assist language-heavy activities and customer communication.
- Human review and clear authority are needed where outputs create obligations or affect customers.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
25. Q: What role can traditional machine learning play in process improvement?
A: Show answer
Traditional machine learning can identify patterns in historical and current data to support prediction, classification, and optimization. The chapter gives examples including pricing optimization, faster credit approvals, fraud prevention, and staffing estimates. In manufacturing and supply chains, models may forecast demand or production output so managers can plan capacity and inventory. The value depends on the quality and relevance of the training data, the stability of the operating environment, and whether the prediction is connected to a decision that someone can act on. Teams should monitor model performance after deployment because changing products, customers, regulations, or market conditions can make earlier patterns less reliable.

Study instruction:
- For a proposed prediction model, define the target outcome, input data, decision supported, error costs, responsible user, and monitoring plan.

Key point:
- Machine learning can improve forecasting and decision support when linked to an actionable process decision.
- Model performance and operating conditions must be monitored after deployment.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
26. Q: How can RPA, IoT, blockchain, and business analytics support different parts of a process?
A: Show answer
Robotic process automation is suited to repetitive, rule-based actions such as transferring data between systems or processing routine workflow steps. IoT sensors can provide timely information about equipment condition, location, temperature, or production activity, enabling maintenance or operational decisions. Blockchain may support shared traceability of selected transactions or goods movements where multiple parties need a common record. Business analytics can combine process and performance data to inform decisions throughout the workflow. None of these technologies automatically fixes unclear responsibilities, poor data definitions, or weak controls. Each should be assessed for its specific purpose, reliability, integration requirements, and contribution to the process outcome.

Study instruction:
- Match each technology to a real activity in a process and state the problem it addresses, the data it needs, and the control that remains necessary.

Key point:
- Different technologies address different process tasks and information needs.
- Their value depends on integration into a controlled workflow.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- Microsoft: What Is Process Mining?
27. Q: What should a cross-functional process-redesign team deliver?
A: Show answer
A redesign team should produce more than a new flowchart. The chapter says it must consider the workflow, required skills, technologies, organizational structure, and expectations of customers and partners. It should also specify how information moves, where decisions occur, who owns exceptions, and how performance will be measured. A useful design compares the current state with a future state and explains why each proposed change improves the customer outcome or removes a demonstrated problem. The team should document assumptions, dependencies, implementation risks, training needs, and control changes so that the design can be evaluated and implemented consistently across the functions involved.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a future-state design pack containing the workflow, roles, data requirements, technology components, controls, measures, training, and implementation dependencies.

Key point:
- Process redesign must address people, structure, technology, data, and customer expectations together.
- A flowchart alone is not a complete implementation design.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
28. Q: How can generative AI assist process design without replacing business expertise?
A: Show answer
Generative AI tools can accelerate process design by suggesting templates, drafting workflow descriptions, identifying possible omissions, and translating business experts' descriptions into structured models. The chapter describes Deutsche Telekom using Pega Blueprint to help redesign complex HR processes by giving business specialists a more accessible way to describe their work and by generating suggested templates. These capabilities can reduce communication friction between business and IT teams. They do not establish that a suggested process is suitable, compliant, or operationally safe. Process experts must validate the design against real work, policy, customer needs, system constraints, and control requirements before it is approved.

Study instruction:
- Review an AI-generated workflow proposal and record which steps, assumptions, roles, and controls require validation by process owners.

Key point:
- Generative AI can speed up design and improve communication between specialists.
- Accountable experts must validate every material process decision before implementation.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
29. Q: Why is change management essential when implementing an AI-enabled process?
A: Show answer
Process change affects responsibilities, skills, routines, information access, incentives, and sometimes the balance of authority between departments. The chapter emphasizes that implementation requires persuading stakeholders, retraining workers, integrating data, and coordinating many moving parts. Employees may resist because they fear job loss, distrust model recommendations, or believe the redesign ignores practical realities. Customers and partners may also need new instructions or service arrangements. Change management should therefore explain the reason for the change, involve affected users in design, provide role-specific training, communicate decision rights, and create feedback channels. It should also monitor unintended consequences rather than assuming that adoption will follow automatically after software deployment.

Study instruction:
- Create a change-impact assessment identifying affected roles, new skills, concerns, communications, training, and feedback arrangements.

Key point:
- Technology implementation is also an organizational and behavioral change.
- Participation, training, communication, and feedback help make redesigned processes workable.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
30. Q: How should an organization phase implementation of a redesigned process?
A: Show answer
Implementation should translate the approved design into coordinated changes to systems, data, roles, controls, training, and customer communication. The chapter notes that implementation may take months but should not drift into a multi-year effort without a clear reason. A practical plan can sequence foundational data and ownership work, system integration, controlled pilots, staff training, customer communication, and wider rollout. Before expanding, teams should test the workflow under normal and exception conditions and confirm that controls operate as intended. Each phase should have acceptance criteria, an accountable owner, rollback or contingency arrangements, and measures for deciding whether the process is ready to move forward.

Study instruction:
- Build an implementation plan with phases, owners, dependencies, testing criteria, training, customer communication, and go-live approval points.

Key point:
- Implementation must coordinate people, data, systems, and operating controls.
- Phased delivery with explicit acceptance criteria reduces rollout risk.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
31. Q: What does process control mean after a redesigned workflow is implemented?
A: Show answer
In the chapter, control is associated with predictability: confidence that the process will continue to perform within acceptable limits rather than repeatedly requiring firefighting. Control therefore involves clear operating standards, assigned responsibilities, reliable data, monitoring, exception handling, and corrective action. It does not mean preventing all variation or freezing the process permanently. Teams should understand normal variation, identify unusual or harmful deviations, and investigate whether the process or its environment has changed. Effective control also requires management to respond to findings. A dashboard that identifies problems without an owner or corrective action does not establish control; it only makes problems visible.

Study instruction:
- Define normal operating ranges, exception thresholds, owners, escalation routes, and corrective-action evidence for a redesigned process.

Key point:
- Control means sustained and predictable performance, not simply more reporting.
- Monitoring must connect exceptions to accountable investigation and action.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
32. Q: How should process mining support continuous monitoring after redesign?
A: Show answer
Process mining can compare actual event patterns with the intended process and reveal whether the redesigned workflow is being followed. It can help identify new bottlenecks, unexpected variants, repeated rework, delays, or deteriorating performance. Microsoft describes three broad uses: discovery of process models from event data, conformance checking against an intended model, and enhancement of a model using observed deviations. These capabilities can support continuous review, but they depend on accurate event data and sensible process definitions. Managers should combine analytical alerts with contextual investigation because a deviation may reflect a legitimate exception, a data defect, or a genuine control failure.

Study instruction:
- Define the expected process path and choose event-log indicators that would reveal deviations, bottlenecks, and recurring exceptions.

Key point:
- Ongoing process mining can test whether actual execution matches the intended design.
- Deviations need investigation before they are classified as failures.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- Microsoft: What Is Process Mining?
33. Q: Why is process variation important, and how can it be reduced?
A: Show answer
Variation means that similar process cases follow different paths, take different amounts of time, or produce inconsistent outcomes. Some variation is justified by customer needs, product differences, regulation, or genuine exceptions. Excessive or unexplained variation can create unpredictable delivery, higher cost, inconsistent service, and difficulty planning capacity. The chapter illustrates how a lower-cost but less reliable delivery carrier can introduce significant variation into final delivery performance. Organizations should measure variation, identify its sources, distinguish necessary flexibility from avoidable inconsistency, and address root causes through standards, supplier management, better data, or redesigned handoffs. The goal is dependable performance without eliminating justified exceptions.

Study instruction:
- Compare process outcomes across locations, teams, suppliers, or case types and classify the observed variation as necessary or avoidable.

Key point:
- Unexplained variation weakens predictability and customer service.
- Standardization should reduce harmful variation while preserving justified flexibility.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- Microsoft: What Is Process Mining?
34. Q: Why are common data standards and cross-department data sharing necessary for AI-enabled processes?
A: Show answer
End-to-end processes depend on information moving between systems and departments in a consistent form. If each department uses different definitions, identifiers, formats, or quality rules, downstream teams may have to reconcile or repair the information before they can act. The chapter's sales-to-operations example shows how poor upstream data can create substantial downstream rework. Shared standards clarify what fields mean, who maintains them, what validation applies, and how changes are communicated. Data sharing should also respect access controls, confidentiality, privacy, and legitimate business purposes. AI models are particularly dependent on data quality because errors or inconsistent definitions can distort predictions and recommendations.

Study instruction:
- Identify the critical data fields passed between two departments and document their definitions, validation rules, owners, and access restrictions.

Key point:
- Shared process data requires common definitions and clear stewardship.
- Data quality and access governance are prerequisites for dependable analytics and AI.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
35. Q: What risks arise when an organization automates a poorly defined process?
A: Show answer
Automating a poorly defined process can make errors happen faster, increase the volume of incorrect transactions, and obscure the source of a failure. A bot may reproduce an outdated rule, an AI model may recommend an action based on incomplete data, or an automated handoff may send unresolved exceptions to the wrong team. Automation can also create a false sense of control if users assume that system execution means a transaction was valid. Before automation, teams should clarify the process objective, inputs, decision rules, exceptions, approvals, evidence, and ownership. They should test normal and unusual cases, monitor error rates, and maintain a way to stop or reverse harmful actions.

Study instruction:
- Conduct a pre-automation risk review covering process clarity, data quality, exception handling, authorization, monitoring, and rollback capability.

Key point:
- Automation can scale a defective process as easily as a good one.
- Process and control design should precede automated execution.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
36. Q: How should AI risks be governed within an organization's process-management approach?
A: Show answer
AI risk governance should connect model decisions to the process in which they are used. Organizations need to identify the purpose of the AI system, affected people, operational context, potential harms, decision authority, data dependencies, and consequences of error. NIST's AI Risk Management Framework offers a voluntary structure for considering trustworthiness through the functions Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage across the AI lifecycle. In a process setting, this means assigning accountable owners, documenting intended use, evaluating performance and impacts, monitoring deployment, and responding to problems. Governance should be proportionate to the decision's significance and should not be treated as a one-time approval before deployment.

Study instruction:
- Apply the NIST AI RMF functions to one process use case and record governance owners, context, evaluation measures, and risk responses.

Key point:
- AI risk governance must continue through design, deployment, use, and evaluation.
- Process owners and AI specialists need clearly assigned responsibilities.

Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
37. Q: What human oversight and escalation controls should remain in an AI-enabled process?
A: Show answer
Human oversight should be designed around the consequences of an incorrect or uncertain output. Low-risk routine tasks may be automated with sampling and exception monitoring, while high-impact decisions may require prior review by an authorized person. The process should specify confidence or exception thresholds, who can approve or override a recommendation, what evidence must be retained, and how a disputed result is escalated. Staff should be able to report unexpected model behavior without being penalized for questioning automation. Oversight must be meaningful: reviewers need sufficient information, competence, time, and authority to challenge outputs rather than merely clicking approval on a system-generated recommendation.

Study instruction:
- For one AI-supported decision, define review thresholds, override authority, required evidence, escalation contacts, and post-decision monitoring.

Key point:
- Human review should match the risk and impact of the automated decision.
- Oversight is ineffective without information, competence, time, and authority to intervene.

Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
38. Q: How should organizations address privacy, fairness, transparency, and security in AI-supported processes?
A: Show answer
Organizations should identify what personal or sensitive data the process uses, why it is needed, who may access it, how long it is retained, and whether it is shared with vendors or other systems. They should test whether model outcomes differ unfairly across affected groups, whether recommendations can be explained sufficiently for the decision context, and whether systems are protected against unauthorized access or manipulation. NIST identifies trustworthiness characteristics including validity, reliability, safety, security, resilience, accountability, transparency, explainability, privacy enhancement, and fairness with harmful bias managed. These concerns should be incorporated into process design, procurement, testing, monitoring, and incident response rather than delegated solely to a technical team.

Study instruction:
- Complete a data and impact review for an AI use case, identifying sensitive fields, access controls, fairness tests, transparency needs, and incident procedures.

Key point:
- Trustworthiness requires attention to data, outcomes, security, and accountability.
- Privacy and fairness controls should be built into the process lifecycle.

Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
39. Q: How should the return on investment of an AI-enabled process initiative be measured?
A: Show answer
ROI should compare the full cost of designing, implementing, operating, and governing the solution with the value actually achieved. Relevant benefits may include shorter cycle times, fewer errors, reduced rework, improved service, higher capacity, lower write-offs, better asset utilization, or faster cash collection. Costs may include software, integration, data preparation, model evaluation, security, training, process redesign, oversight, and ongoing maintenance. The chapter cautions that many organizations lack ROI evidence beyond productivity metrics. A credible evaluation therefore needs a baseline, a defined measurement period, attribution assumptions, and balancing indicators to ensure that faster work has not reduced quality or increased downstream risk.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a before-and-after business case that includes implementation and recurring costs, quantified benefits, nonfinancial outcomes, and balancing measures.

Key point:
- ROI should reflect the total process and lifecycle cost, not only the automated task.
- Benefits require a baseline and evidence that the change caused the improvement.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
40. Q: What does the Mars Wrigley example illustrate about combining AI with process management?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Mars Wrigley combining a digital twin of a production line with machine learning to predict output and reduce overfilling and waste. It also describes decision-intelligence visualizations and recommendations for preventive maintenance, as well as AI-supported supply-chain decisions involving supply and demand, invoice processing, and truck utilization. The example illustrates that value came from combining several capabilities across production and supply-chain activities rather than deploying a single isolated model. The reported increase in truck utilization and improvements in order fulfillment and customer ratings are examples cited by the article, not universal performance guarantees. The broader lesson is to connect models to operational decisions and measurable process outcomes.

Study instruction:
- Map the technologies in the Mars Wrigley example to the operational decisions they support and identify the performance measure each could influence.

Key point:
- AI value emerges when predictions and recommendations are embedded in real operating decisions.
- A portfolio of connected use cases can improve several stages of a process.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
41. Q: What does the PepsiCo example show about using process mining to improve operational performance?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that PepsiCo began process-mining work in accounts payable and later expanded the approach to additional processes, including end-to-end order-to-cash. The article describes annual reductions in labor hours and write-offs, along with automated customer messages about overdue receivables. It also explains how process mining helped identify a high order-rejection rate after a new SAP implementation; the reported rate fell from 30% to 4% after the company used AI-supported analysis to focus on the problem. The example demonstrates the value of diagnosing process behavior from event data, prioritizing material issues, and linking findings to targeted corrective action.

Study instruction:
- Analyze a process rejection or exception rate and outline how event data could identify the causes and verify whether corrective action worked.

Key point:
- Process mining can identify measurable operational problems that are difficult to see within one department.
- Improvement claims should be linked to defined baselines and observed outcomes.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- Microsoft: What Is Process Mining?
42. Q: What does the Deutsche Telekom example show about AI-assisted process redesign?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Deutsche Telekom facing a complex landscape of roughly 800 HR processes across more than 20 countries. Traditional redesign work had been slow partly because business specialists and IT teams struggled to communicate requirements. A generative-AI-supported design tool helped business experts describe processes in familiar language, suggested elements that might have been overlooked, and produced templates for review. The article reports that 250 processes had been streamlined and that the company planned to address the remainder. The example illustrates how AI can reduce design friction and accelerate documentation, while business expertise remains necessary to validate the proposed process and adapt it to local requirements.

Study instruction:
- Identify where business-to-IT communication slows a redesign and specify how AI-generated process descriptions should be validated by process experts.

Key point:
- AI can accelerate process modeling and improve communication between business and technology teams.
- Generated designs require expert review and local operational validation.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
43. Q: How should leaders apply the chapter's seven-step approach to process management and AI?
A: Show answer
The sequence is to establish process ownership, identify process customers, map the current process, define performance measures and targets, consider technology enablers, redesign the process, and implement and monitor it. The steps are connected rather than independent checklist items. Customer needs inform the measures; the current-state map reveals the problems; the measures help prioritize redesign; technology is selected to support the future workflow; and implementation creates a new operating model that must be monitored. Leaders should revisit earlier steps when evidence changes. For example, monitoring may reveal a new customer need or an unexpected process variant that requires a revised design or additional control.

Study instruction:
- Apply all seven steps to one real process and identify the evidence, decision, owner, and deliverable required at each stage.

Key point:
- The seven steps create a connected path from accountability and customer needs to continuous monitoring.
- Process management is iterative and must respond to new evidence and changing conditions.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
44. Q: How can organizations avoid repeating the failures associated with earlier process-reengineering movements?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that process reengineering lost favor after projects in the early 1990s were associated with failure and mindless layoffs. Organizations can avoid repeating these patterns by treating process improvement as a disciplined, evidence-based management practice rather than a slogan for rapid restructuring. They should understand the actual work, involve employees who perform it, focus on customer outcomes, validate data, set realistic targets, and assess the effects of changes on skills and responsibilities. Workforce reduction should not be treated as the default source of benefit. Leaders should distinguish genuine elimination of waste from transferring workload, weakening controls, or removing capabilities needed to serve customers reliably.

Study instruction:
- Review a proposed redesign and identify whether each benefit comes from removing waste, improving coordination, changing technology, or simply shifting work or risk elsewhere.

Key point:
- Process redesign should improve performance rather than pursue change for its own sake.
- Employee involvement and evidence-based decisions reduce the risk of harmful implementation.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
45. Q: What should senior leaders do to put process management at the center of an AI strategy?
A: Show answer
Senior leaders should treat process as the mechanism through which the organization delivers value and should connect AI investments to clearly owned workflows. They should appoint credible process owners, align departmental incentives with end-to-end outcomes, make customer needs explicit, invest in reliable shared data, and require business and technology teams to redesign work together. Leaders should also fund training, establish appropriate AI risk governance, and insist on measures that show whether service, quality, cost, reliability, and productivity have improved. The chapter's central message is not that every process needs AI, but that organizations serious about AI should understand and manage the processes in which it is used.

Study instruction:
- Draft an executive agenda for one priority process covering ownership, customer outcomes, data, AI use cases, risk governance, investment, and performance review.

Key point:
- Process strategy should guide AI investment and operational change.
- Leaders must align accountability, people, data, technology, and measurable outcomes.

Source links:
- Davenport and Redman: How to Marry Process Management and AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
