Table 7
Heavy Machinery Meets AI: Fusion Products, Services, Systems, and Solutions
1. Q: What is a fusion strategy in an industrial business?
A: Show answer
A fusion strategy combines the physical-product strengths of industrial manufacturers with the data, software, and AI capabilities associated with digital businesses. Rather than simply adding connectivity to an existing machine, the company reimagines how the product, its service, and the customer's wider operating system could work when data and intelligence are designed in from the beginning. The objective is to use product-in-use information to generate insights, improve performance, and solve customer problems. The strategy therefore changes product design, service delivery, partnerships, organizational coordination, and value capture. It treats physical assets and digital capabilities as complementary sources of customer value.

Study instruction:
- "Choose one physical product and describe how sensors, software, data, AI, services, and partners could change the customer's outcome rather than merely add a connected feature."

Key point:
- Fusion strategy integrates physical products with digital intelligence to create customer value.
- The goal is business-model and outcome innovation, not connectivity for its own sake.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
- NIST: Digital Twins
2. Q: Why do industrial manufacturers need to move beyond traditional product-centered competition?
A: Show answer
Traditional industrial competition often emphasizes product features, production efficiency, distribution, and the initial sale. Yet the customer's experience and costs continue after purchase, when the machine is used, maintained, combined with other equipment, and adapted to changing conditions. Product-in-use data can reveal performance problems, operating patterns, and opportunities for improvement that are invisible at the point of sale. Companies that use this information can offer more personalized services, improve equipment over time, and differentiate through customer outcomes. The competitive arena can also expand beyond familiar manufacturers to include software firms, data providers, and ecosystem partners.

Study instruction:
- "Map the customer journey after a product sale and identify where usage data could improve uptime, cost, quality, safety, or productivity."

Key point:
- The value opportunity continues throughout the product's operating life.
- Competition increasingly includes digital capabilities and customer outcomes, not only hardware specifications.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
3. Q: How does fusion strategy differ from the Internet of Things?
A: Show answer
The Internet of Things connects physical objects through sensors, software, and networks so that they can be monitored and data can be collected. Fusion strategy goes further by rethinking how the physical product and its associated services should be designed when continuous data and advanced digital capabilities are integral to the offering. IoT may improve monitoring or automate a defined process; fusion strategy uses information to generate new customer value, change the product or service model, and coordinate multiple parts of the business. The distinction is strategic rather than merely technical: fusion strategy requires cross-functional decisions about customers, data, products, services, partners, and economics.

Study instruction:
- "Compare one connected-product project with a proposed fusion strategy and identify what changes in design, customer value, data use, and organizational ownership."

Key point:
- Connectivity enables data collection; fusion strategy redesigns how the business creates value from it.
- Fusion strategy requires cross-functional leadership rather than an isolated technology project.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
4. Q: What are the four types of fusion strategies described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The four types are fusion products, fusion services, fusion systems, and fusion solutions. Fusion products are digitally enabled physical offerings designed to collect and use product-in-use data. Fusion services use data and AI to provide timely, tailored recommendations or support. Fusion systems integrate multiple products and machines, often from different suppliers, so that the overall system performs better. Fusion solutions combine products, services, and systems around a customer's broader performance problem. The categories describe different ways of creating value and can be explored in stages, although a company may pursue more than one as its capabilities and customer needs develop.

Study instruction:
- "Take one industrial customer problem and sketch a product, service, system, and solution response, identifying the data and partners each would require."

Key point:
- The framework separates four related but distinct ways to create customer value.
- A company should choose its starting point based on customer need, data, capability, and reach.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
5. Q: What makes a product a fusion product rather than an ordinary connected device?
A: Show answer
A fusion product is conceived to use information about how it performs in real settings as a central part of its value proposition. Sensors and connectivity are not merely added for remote monitoring; they help the product adapt, improve, provide personalized recommendations, or participate in a larger service. The product may be designed from the start around software, data, and the physical mechanism working together. Its performance can improve through updates or learning from a wider installed base, subject to safety and validation controls. The key test is whether digital capability changes the product's usefulness and the customer's outcome, not simply whether it can transmit data.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a connected machine and identify which functions would be materially different if its digital capabilities were designed into the product from the beginning."

Key point:
- A fusion product integrates digital intelligence into its core design and use.
- A sensor connection alone does not establish a fusion strategy.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
- NIST: Digital Twins
6. Q: What is a fusion service, and how does product-in-use data improve it?
A: Show answer
A fusion service uses information generated during product operation to deliver timely, tailored support or recommendations. Instead of relying only on scheduled inspections or a technician's visit after a failure, the provider can analyze operating conditions, usage patterns, and performance signals to identify issues or suggest improvements. In the chapter's aircraft-engine example, analysis of routes, altitude, speed, weather, and load can inform fuel-efficiency recommendations. The service can scale beyond labor-intensive visits when insights are delivered digitally, but it still needs accurate data, customer trust, clear responsibilities, and a method for verifying the benefit.

Study instruction:
- "Choose a service currently delivered through periodic visits and identify which recommendations could be generated from reliable product-in-use data."

Key point:
- Fusion services turn operational data into tailored customer support.
- Value depends on actionable recommendations and verified customer benefit, not data volume alone.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
7. Q: What is a fusion system?
A: Show answer
A fusion system connects and coordinates multiple products, machines, or subsystems so that the whole operating environment performs better. It differs from simply monitoring each device separately because it considers interactions, dependencies, and system-level outcomes. A customer may operate equipment from several specialist suppliers, and the value may come from coordinating their data and behavior rather than optimizing each machine in isolation. The system provider must therefore address interoperability, shared data models, integration, ongoing updates, and responsibility for overall performance. The chapter's building-management example illustrates how information from many facility systems can support anomaly detection and more coordinated maintenance.

Study instruction:
- "Map the machines and software in one customer environment and identify the system-level outcome that would improve if they shared reliable information."

Key point:
- A fusion system optimizes connected assets as an integrated whole.
- Interoperability and continuing system management are central capabilities.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
- NIST: Digital Twins for Advanced Manufacturing
8. Q: What is a fusion solution, and why does it represent a broader value proposition?
A: Show answer
A fusion solution combines products, services, and systems to improve a customer's overall performance. Rather than selling separate components and leaving the customer to integrate them, the provider uses data, AI, and partner capabilities to address a recurring customer problem in a way that can be adapted across similar customers. The chapter's agricultural example links equipment, satellite imagery, agronomic information, and forecasting to help farmers make decisions about yields, costs, and returns. A solution requires deep customer understanding, trusted relationships, ecosystem coordination, and a way to connect revenue to the value created.

Study instruction:
- "Describe a customer's end-to-end problem and design a solution that combines physical equipment, analytics, service, and partner capabilities."

Key point:
- Fusion solutions organize multiple capabilities around a customer's performance outcome.
- The provider must earn trust and understand the customer's business, not only its equipment.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
9. Q: How do the four fusion strategies differ in scope and complexity?
A: Show answer
Fusion products focus on digitally enabled individual offerings; fusion services use data to improve support and recommendations; fusion systems integrate multiple related assets; and fusion solutions combine these capabilities around a broader customer objective. Complexity generally increases as the scope expands because the company must coordinate more data sources, partners, workflows, and performance responsibilities. The article suggests that many firms may begin with products and build toward services, systems, and solutions as they gain experience. That is a useful path, not a universal rule: the best starting point depends on the customer problem, data reach, technical capability, and ecosystem access.

Study instruction:
- "Place three potential projects on a four-stage fusion map and explain their scope, dependencies, and organizational complexity."

Key point:
- The four types describe expanding levels of integration and customer outcome.
- Progression should follow capability and customer value rather than a fixed maturity checklist.

- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
10. Q: What do data reach and data richness mean in the fusion strategy framework?
A: Show answer
Data reach concerns how broadly the company can observe product use across customers, machines, locations, and operating contexts. Data richness concerns how much useful detail the company can capture about that use, including the variety, frequency, and context of the information. A product may generate detailed data from a small number of installations, or a large installed base may produce broad but relatively shallow information. These dimensions help determine which fusion strategies are feasible. Broad reach can reveal patterns across customers, while rich data can support more precise diagnosis and recommendations. Both require appropriate rights, quality, security, and customer trust.

Study instruction:
- "Assess one product's data reach and richness by listing installations, sensors, data types, frequency, context, and gaps."

Key point:
- Reach describes coverage; richness describes the depth and usefulness of product-use information.
- More data is valuable only when it is relevant, reliable, permitted, and actionable.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
- NIST: Digital Twins
11. Q: Why is product-in-use data strategically different from point-of-sale data?
A: Show answer
Point-of-sale data can show who purchased a product, when, and at what price, but it provides limited evidence about how the product performs in real conditions. Product-in-use data can reveal operating patterns, environmental conditions, failures, customer behavior, and the outcomes produced after the sale. It enables the manufacturer to improve design, tailor services, anticipate maintenance needs, and understand how products interact with a customer's wider system. The shift changes the relationship from a one-time transaction toward an ongoing feedback loop. Companies must still respect customer permissions, data quality, privacy, and commercial confidentiality.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the decisions supported by sales records with those supported by continuous operating data for one product."

Key point:
- Product-in-use data reveals performance and customer outcomes after the sale.
- Continuous feedback can support improvement, services, and longer-term customer relationships.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
12. Q: How can structured and unstructured data contribute to industrial AI?
A: Show answer
Structured data includes information organized in defined fields, such as operating hours, temperatures, pressures, serial numbers, and maintenance records. Unstructured data includes images, video, sound, text, and other information that does not fit neatly into a fixed table. Industrial AI can combine these sources to detect defects, recognize abnormal sounds, interpret visual conditions, or identify patterns that would be difficult to capture through conventional records alone. The value depends on context and labeling: a model must understand what a signal represents and how it relates to the machine or process. Data governance, representative coverage, and validation are necessary before decisions are automated.

Study instruction:
- "List the structured and unstructured data available for one machine and propose a specific decision each type could improve."

Key point:
- Different data types reveal different aspects of machine behavior.
- Combining data requires context, quality controls, and validation.

- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
13. Q: Why can real-time analysis create more value than collecting data for later reporting?
A: Show answer
Real-time or near-real-time analysis can identify a developing issue while there is still an opportunity to intervene, adjust a process, or advise a customer. A delayed report may describe what happened but arrive too late to prevent downtime, waste, or a poor operating decision. The chapter's examples use data from machinery and operating environments to generate immediate recommendations or detect anomalies. Not every decision needs real-time processing: latency requirements should be matched to the operational risk, connectivity, cost, and safety context. Critical actions may require local control or human confirmation rather than depending on a remote model response.

Study instruction:
- "For one use case, estimate the time between signal, analysis, recommendation, and action, then identify the value of reducing that delay."

Key point:
- Timely insight matters when it changes an operational decision before harm or waste occurs.
- Processing speed should match the use case and its safety requirements.

- NIST: Digital Twins
14. Q: What is a digital twin, and how does it support a fusion strategy?
A: Show answer
A digital twin is a digital representation of a physical asset, process, or system that is connected to relevant data and models so it can support monitoring, analysis, prediction, or optimization. It is more than a static 3D drawing: its usefulness depends on how well it represents the real system and how its information is maintained and validated. In fusion strategies, digital twins can help manufacturers understand product behavior, test design alternatives, simulate operating conditions, and coordinate system-level changes. A twin should be designed for a defined decision or outcome, with appropriate data quality, model validation, lifecycle management, and security.

Study instruction:
- "Define one digital-twin use case and specify the physical system, data inputs, model outputs, decisions supported, and validation evidence."

Key point:
- A digital twin connects a digital model to a real asset or process for useful decisions.
- Its credibility depends on validation, maintenance, and clear purpose.

Source links:
- NIST: Digital Twins
- NIST: Digital Twins for Advanced Manufacturing
15. Q: What are product, process, and performance twins?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes three related digital-twin views in its automotive example. A product twin represents the product as designed. A process twin represents the process or production system that created it. A performance twin represents the product in use. Together they connect design intent, manufacturing execution, and real-world operation. Comparing these views can help identify whether a problem originates in design, production, or the conditions of use. The concept is useful because improvements in one lifecycle stage may affect another. The company must maintain consistent identifiers and data relationships so that the twins describe the same asset and its history.

Study instruction:
- "For a manufactured product, sketch the design, production, and in-use twins and show what data must link them."

Key point:
- The three views connect design, manufacturing, and product performance.
- Lifecycle linkage helps locate causes and improve future versions.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
- NIST: Digital Twins
16. Q: How should a company validate a digital twin before using it for operational decisions?
A: Show answer
Validation should test whether the twin represents the physical system accurately enough for its intended use. The company should define the model's purpose, required accuracy, operating range, data inputs, assumptions, and acceptable uncertainty. It can compare model outputs with measured behavior, test boundary conditions, and examine performance under unusual conditions. Validation should be repeated when the physical asset, model, data pipeline, or operating environment changes materially. NIST's manufacturing work emphasizes requirements, data management, model development, validation, and quantified uncertainty. A twin used for high-consequence decisions needs stronger evidence and controls than one used for exploratory visualization.

Study instruction:
- "Write a validation plan for one twin with reference measurements, test conditions, accuracy thresholds, uncertainty analysis, and change-control requirements."

Key point:
- A digital twin must be validated for its specific decision context.
- Model credibility requires evidence and ongoing maintenance, not visual realism alone.

Source links:
- NIST: Digital Twins for Advanced Manufacturing
- NIST: Digital Twins
17. Q: How can AI improve industrial product design?
A: Show answer
AI can analyze large datasets from product use, manufacturing, testing, and customer environments to identify patterns and design opportunities. Generative methods can help explore design alternatives or produce virtual representations for testing, while predictive models can estimate performance under different conditions. The chapter argues that digital and physical capabilities should be combined so that data informs design rather than remaining separate from engineering. AI does not replace engineering judgment, safety analysis, or physical testing. A design must still meet performance, reliability, maintainability, regulatory, and cost requirements, and the model's assumptions should be tested before a generated design is accepted.

Study instruction:
- "Select a design problem and identify where AI could generate or rank alternatives, what engineering constraints must be enforced, and how prototypes will be validated."

Key point:
- AI can expand design exploration and connect real-world use to future product improvements.
- Engineering validation remains essential before AI-generated designs are deployed.

18. Q: How can predictive maintenance create value in industrial equipment?
A: Show answer
Predictive maintenance uses operating data and models to estimate when equipment may need attention or is likely to fail. It can help schedule maintenance before a breakdown, reduce unnecessary inspections, improve spare-parts planning, and increase availability. The model may combine vibration, temperature, pressure, acoustic signals, usage patterns, and service history. Its value depends on reliable sensors, representative failure data, appropriate thresholds, and integration with maintenance workflows. False alarms can waste resources, while missed failures can create safety and production risks. A company should measure avoided downtime and maintenance cost alongside model accuracy and operational consequences.

Study instruction:
- "Choose one machine failure mode and define the sensor signals, prediction horizon, maintenance action, false-alarm cost, and success metric."

Key point:
- Predictive maintenance is valuable when predictions lead to timely, effective action.
- Model performance must be evaluated against operational cost and safety, not accuracy alone.

19. Q: How can fusion services change the economics of industrial after-sales support?
A: Show answer
Traditional after-sales support can rely heavily on technicians, scheduled visits, and standard procedures. Fusion services use product-use data to identify needs and provide customized recommendations remotely or at scale. This can reduce unnecessary visits and allow specialists to focus on complex interventions. Providers may charge for individual services, bundle support packages, or link payment to measurable outcomes. The economic model must account for data infrastructure, model development, customer support, liability, and the cost of delivering recommendations. A service is commercially sustainable when customers recognize the benefit and the provider can reliably deliver it at a cost below the value created.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the cost and customer value of a traditional maintenance service with a data-enabled service for one equipment category."

Key point:
- Data can make after-sales support more timely and scalable.
- The service model must balance customer benefit, provider cost, and performance responsibility.

20. Q: What business models can companies use to monetize fusion products and services?
A: Show answer
Possible models include charging a premium for a product that performs better, selling digital services separately, bundling services with equipment, charging subscription or usage fees, offering software licenses, charging integration fees, or sharing gains from improved customer performance. The right model depends on how measurable the value is, who controls the relevant data, how much risk the provider assumes, and whether the customer prefers predictable or variable payments. The company should avoid charging for data access without demonstrating useful outcomes. Pricing experiments can help identify which parts of the offering customers value and whether the provider can deliver them profitably at scale.

Study instruction:
- "Design two alternative pricing models for a data-enabled offering and compare customer incentives, provider costs, risk allocation, and revenue predictability."

Key point:
- Fusion strategy creates new ways to price products, services, integration, and outcomes.
- Monetization should reflect demonstrable customer value and delivery economics.

- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
21. Q: What is a performance contract, and when might it suit an industrial fusion offering?
A: Show answer
A performance contract links the provider's obligation or payment to an agreed level of equipment performance, availability, efficiency, or another measurable outcome. It can align incentives because the provider has a reason to monitor the asset, anticipate problems, and maintain performance rather than simply sell a machine. The arrangement requires a precise metric, measurement method, baseline, operating assumptions, exclusions, service responsibilities, and dispute process. It may be attractive when the provider has strong control over maintenance and access to reliable operating data. It is risky when customer behavior, third-party systems, or external conditions materially affect the outcome but are not addressed in the contract.

Study instruction:
- "Draft a performance-contract outline defining the outcome metric, baseline, data access, responsibilities, exclusions, remedies, and verification process."

Key point:
- Outcome-based contracts connect payment or obligation to customer performance.
- Clear measurement and risk allocation are essential to avoid disputes.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
22. Q: How do gain-sharing agreements differ from ordinary subscription pricing?
A: Show answer
A subscription typically charges a recurring fee for access to a service or capability. A gain-sharing agreement links some payment to the improvement or economic benefit generated for the customer. Gain-sharing can align incentives when the provider's analytics or services measurably increase productivity, reduce fuel use, or lower downtime. It also creates measurement challenges: the parties need an agreed baseline, a method for isolating the provider's contribution, treatment of external factors, and rules for sharing savings or gains. The model may be attractive when customer outcomes can be observed and jointly verified, but it is less suitable when attribution is highly uncertain.

Study instruction:
- "For one service, compare a fixed subscription with a gain-sharing model and define how the baseline and attributable benefit would be measured."

Key point:
- Gain sharing links revenue to measured customer improvement.
- Attribution rules and reliable baselines determine whether the model is workable.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
23. Q: Why are partnerships central to fusion strategy?
A: Show answer
Industrial fusion offerings often require capabilities that no single company owns: machinery, sensors, cloud infrastructure, AI models, software, connectivity, specialist data, and customer-domain knowledge. Partnerships allow firms to combine these capabilities and reach a broader customer problem. The chapter describes ecosystems involving equipment makers, digital providers, and specialist partners. Partnership is not simply outsourcing; it changes how the company creates, delivers, and captures value. The manufacturer must decide which capabilities are core, which can be sourced, how data and intellectual property are governed, and how the customer experiences one coherent solution despite multiple contributors.

Study instruction:
- "Map the capabilities required for a fusion offering and identify which should be built internally, partnered for, or purchased."

Key point:
- Ecosystems combine complementary capabilities to solve customer problems.
- Partnerships require clear governance of data, IP, responsibilities, and customer experience.

- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
24. Q: What does coopetition mean in a fusion ecosystem?
A: Show answer
Coopetition occurs when companies that compete in some areas cooperate in others because their combined capabilities create value neither could deliver alone. In a fusion system, machines from multiple suppliers may need to exchange information and coordinate performance even while their manufacturers compete for equipment sales. Cooperation can improve interoperability and customer outcomes, but it creates questions about data access, intellectual property, security, liability, and commercial boundaries. Firms should define the purpose of collaboration, permitted data uses, technical interfaces, decision rights, and exit arrangements. Trust and governance are essential because the partners may remain competitors in adjacent markets.

Study instruction:
- "Identify one potential ecosystem partnership with a competitor and list the shared objective, data boundaries, IP protections, and competition risks."

Key point:
- Competitors may need to collaborate where customer systems cross company boundaries.
- Coopetition requires carefully defined technical and commercial safeguards.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
25. Q: Why should a fusion strategy begin with the customer's problem rather than with a preferred technology?
A: Show answer
Starting with technology can produce impressive demonstrations that do not solve an important operational problem. A customer-centered approach begins by understanding the job to be done, the current cost or performance gap, the operating context, and the outcome the customer values. The company can then decide whether sensors, AI, digital twins, analytics, or integration are useful. The chapter emphasizes that fusion solutions should improve customer performance and be designed around real needs. This reduces the risk of building a sophisticated system that is difficult to adopt or monetize. Customer discovery should continue after launch because use conditions and priorities can change.

Study instruction:
- "Interview a customer about a recurring operational problem before proposing a digital solution; quantify the baseline and desired outcome."

Key point:
- Technology is a means; customer performance is the objective.
- Problem discovery reduces the risk of building features without meaningful demand.

- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
26. Q: How does a fusion strategy change the role of the industrial manufacturer?
A: Show answer
The manufacturer moves from being primarily a maker and seller of physical equipment toward being a provider or orchestrator of connected products, services, systems, and customer outcomes. It must understand product use, develop digital capabilities, manage data, work with partners, and support customers throughout the operating lifecycle. This does not make manufacturing expertise less important; physical engineering remains a core asset. Instead, the manufacturer combines that expertise with software, analytics, and ecosystem management. The organization must decide which capabilities it needs to own, which it can access through partners, and how it will maintain responsibility for the customer experience.

Study instruction:
- "Describe how the company's identity, capabilities, revenue model, and customer relationships would change if it adopted a fusion strategy."

Key point:
- The manufacturer's role expands from equipment delivery to ongoing customer value creation.
- Physical engineering remains important but must connect with digital and ecosystem capabilities.

27. Q: Why does fusion strategy require cross-functional leadership?
A: Show answer
Fusion strategy cuts across product design, engineering, operations, software, data, sales, service, legal, cybersecurity, and finance. A functionally isolated IoT project may collect data without changing the product or customer proposition. A fusion strategy needs senior leaders to agree on which customer problems matter, what data can be used, how products and services fit together, and how value and responsibility are shared. Cross-functional coordination also helps avoid fragmented architecture, conflicting incentives, and duplicated investment. Leadership must create common priorities and decision rights while allowing specialists to contribute their expertise.

Study instruction:

Key point:
- Fusion strategy is an enterprise strategy, not an IT department project.
- Shared priorities and clear governance are required across functions.

28. Q: How can digital capabilities alter the competitive landscape for traditional industrial companies?
A: Show answer
Digital capabilities can allow software firms, data platforms, startups, and technology providers to enter markets previously dominated by manufacturers. These firms may compete through analytics, customer relationships, software updates, or integrated services rather than by making the physical machine. Industrial companies may also face competition from ecosystems rather than individual product vendors. The response is not necessarily to build every digital capability internally. It is to understand where digital differentiation matters, protect strategic customer relationships, form useful partnerships, and make the physical product part of a broader value proposition. Competitive analysis should include both traditional rivals and new entrants with different business models.

29. Q: Why might data become a more important source of scale and differentiation than physical assets alone?
A: Show answer
Physical assets remain essential in industrial markets, but data can allow a company to improve products across a large installed base, personalize services, anticipate failures, and develop new offerings without reproducing every physical capability. Insights may become more valuable as more machines operate in diverse settings, provided the data are relevant, lawful to use, and technically comparable. The chapter argues that companies can gain advantage from connected information assets and customer outcomes. Data does not automatically create a moat: rivals may obtain similar data, customers may restrict access, and models may be replicated. The advantage depends on learning speed, domain expertise, integration, trust, and execution.

30. Q: What data architecture is needed to support fusion products, services, and systems?
A: Show answer
A fusion architecture must connect sensors, machines, edge devices, cloud services, applications, analytics, customer systems, and partner platforms. It needs consistent identifiers, metadata, time synchronization, data quality rules, interfaces, access controls, and lifecycle management. Different products may use different protocols and vendors, so interoperability is important. The architecture should support the required latency and reliability for each use case, rather than move every signal to one central platform by default. A modular design can allow new machines and partners to be added without rebuilding the entire system. Architecture choices should be driven by customer outcomes and operational constraints.

Study instruction:
- "Draw the data flow for one fusion use case from sensor to decision and identify interfaces, latency needs, security controls, and system owners."

Key point:
- Fusion architecture connects physical assets, software, data, and customer workflows.
- Interoperability and modularity reduce integration friction as ecosystems expand.

31. Q: Why is interoperability important when industrial systems contain equipment from different suppliers?
A: Show answer
Customers often operate machines and systems from multiple manufacturers. If each product uses incompatible data formats, interfaces, or control assumptions, the customer must build and maintain costly connections. Interoperability allows information and services to move across components while preserving safety, security, and meaning. It supports system-level optimization and reduces the risk that one vendor's equipment becomes an isolated data silo. Interoperability does not require every system to be identical; it requires agreed interfaces, semantics, testing, and governance. Companies should consider standards and integration requirements early rather than treating them as late-stage technical fixes.

Study instruction:
- "List the systems that must exchange data in a customer environment and identify the standards, APIs, identifiers, and tests needed for reliable interoperability."

Key point:
- Fusion systems depend on components communicating and working together.
- Interoperability should be designed and tested, not assumed.

32. Q: What cybersecurity risks arise when heavy machinery becomes connected and AI-enabled?
A: Show answer
Connected machinery can expose operational technology, sensor networks, cloud services, software updates, remote maintenance channels, and customer data to cyber threats. A compromised system could disrupt production, manipulate sensor readings, interfere with equipment behavior, expose confidential information, or create physical safety consequences. Risk management should cover device identity, secure configuration, access control, network segmentation, patching, monitoring, incident response, supplier security, and safe recovery. Digital twins also introduce questions about the integrity and confidentiality of models and their data. Controls must reflect the operational environment because a security update or shutdown may affect production and safety.

Study instruction:
- "Threat-model one connected machine from sensor to cloud and identify attack paths, safety impacts, controls, and recovery procedures."

Key point:
- Cybersecurity is part of the safety and reliability of connected industrial systems.
- Security must cover devices, data, models, networks, partners, and operations.

33. Q: How should an industrial company govern AI risk in fusion products and services?
A: Show answer
The company should identify the AI system's purpose, affected people, operating context, decision authority, failure modes, and potential consequences. It should establish governance, map risks, measure performance, and manage issues throughout design, deployment, use, and monitoring. NIST's AI Risk Management Framework organizes this work through Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage functions and emphasizes trustworthiness characteristics such as validity, reliability, safety, security, transparency, explainability, privacy, and fairness. The framework is voluntary, so organizations should combine it with applicable law, sector rules, safety standards, and contractual duties. Human oversight should be designed for consequential decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Apply Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage to one industrial AI use case and document risks, controls, evidence, and accountable owners."

Key point:
- AI risk management must continue across the system lifecycle.

34. Q: Why are safety and human oversight especially important when AI controls or advises heavy machinery?
A: Show answer
Industrial decisions can affect workers, customers, equipment, crops, infrastructure, and the environment. A model that misclassifies an object, misreads a sensor, or recommends an unsafe action may cause more than a poor prediction score; it may create physical harm or costly downtime. The system should define which decisions can be automated, which require human confirmation, and which conditions trigger a safe stop or fallback. Testing should cover normal and edge conditions, sensor failures, unusual environments, and model updates. Operators need understandable alerts, training, and a way to override or escalate. Oversight should match the consequence and reversibility of the decision.

Study instruction:
- "For one automated machine function, define safe operating limits, human intervention points, fallback behavior, and validation tests."

Key point:
- Industrial AI must be evaluated against safety and operational consequences.
- Human oversight and fail-safe design should be explicit, not assumed.

35. Q: How should a company protect customer data used in fusion services?
A: Show answer
The company should establish what data are collected, why they are needed, who owns or controls them, who can access them, how long they are retained, and whether they can be used to train models or support other customers. Contracts and technical controls should define permitted uses, confidentiality, security, portability, deletion, and incident responsibilities. Data minimization and purpose limitation can reduce exposure, while aggregation or de-identification may help where appropriate but do not remove every risk. Customers need understandable explanations and meaningful control consistent with the service and applicable law. Trust can be lost if data are repurposed beyond reasonable expectations.

Study instruction:
- "Draft a data-governance map for one fusion service covering collection, purpose, access, retention, model training, sharing, and deletion."

Key point:
- Customer data rights and permitted uses must be clear before scaling a service.
- Trust depends on transparent purpose, security, and enforceable governance.

36. Q: What intellectual-property issues arise when industrial manufacturers partner with digital firms?
A: Show answer
Partnerships may involve pre-existing designs, software, algorithms, training data, customer information, jointly developed models, and insights generated through use. The parties should distinguish background IP from new work, specify ownership or licensing of jointly created outputs, and define whether improvements can be reused for other customers. They should also address confidentiality, open-source components, model training rights, data portability, and restrictions on reverse engineering. If responsibilities are unclear, the partnership may create disputes or lock the customer into one provider. Commercial agreements should be matched by technical controls and governance so that rights are respected in practice.

Study instruction:
- "Create an IP and data-rights checklist for a proposed fusion partnership, separating background assets, new developments, customer data, and model outputs."

Key point:
- Data and software rights can determine who captures value from an ecosystem.

37. Q: Why must digital twins be maintained across the lifecycle of an industrial asset?
A: Show answer
A digital twin can become inaccurate as the physical asset changes, software is updated, components are replaced, or operating conditions evolve. Lifecycle management keeps the model aligned with the real system and preserves traceability between design, manufacturing, operation, maintenance, and retirement. It includes version control, data lineage, calibration, model updates, validation, access management, and documented changes. A stale twin may give misleading predictions even if it was accurate when first created. NIST's manufacturing work emphasizes requirements, data management, model validation, and maintenance as parts of trustworthy digital-twin implementation.

Study instruction:
- "Define a lifecycle process for one digital twin, including update triggers, validation responsibilities, versioning, and retirement criteria."

Key point:
- A digital twin is a maintained operational model, not a one-time digital copy.
- Lifecycle controls protect model relevance and credibility.

38. Q: How can an industrial company move from a pilot fusion product to a scalable offering?
A: Show answer
The company should first validate the customer problem and measurable benefit in a controlled pilot. It should test sensor reliability, data quality, model performance, connectivity, user workflow, service cost, security, and integration with existing systems. Before scaling, management should identify repeatable architecture, support processes, partner roles, training needs, commercial pricing, and operational controls. The pilot should include a clear decision gate: scale, redesign, narrow the use case, or stop. A demonstration that works in one environment may fail elsewhere because machines, conditions, and customer processes differ. Scaling requires evidence across representative contexts.

Study instruction:
- "Design a pilot-to-scale gate with customer outcome measures, technical thresholds, cost-to-serve, security review, and a defined go/no-go decision."

Key point:
- A successful demonstration is not yet a scalable business.
- Scale only after testing technical, operational, commercial, and customer assumptions.

39. Q: What metrics should a company use to evaluate a fusion strategy?
A: Show answer
Metrics should connect technical performance to customer and business outcomes. Technical measures may include data availability, model accuracy in context, latency, uptime, interoperability, and incident rates. Customer measures may include downtime avoided, yield, throughput, quality, energy use, maintenance cost, or service response time. Business measures may include adoption, renewal, revenue per asset, cost to serve, margin, partner contribution, and customer retention. The company should establish baselines and distinguish correlation from attributable impact. A dashboard that focuses only on software usage or model accuracy may miss whether the offering actually improves the customer's operation or produces sustainable economics.

Study instruction:
- "Build a balanced scorecard for one fusion offering with technical, customer, commercial, and risk indicators."

Key point:
- Fusion performance must be measured in customer outcomes as well as technology metrics.
- Baselines and attribution rules make results credible.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
40. Q: How should an industrial company choose which product or process to digitize first?
A: Show answer
The best starting point is a use case where a meaningful customer or operational problem intersects with accessible data, feasible technology, and a credible route to value. The company should consider how often the problem occurs, its cost or risk, whether the relevant signals can be captured, whether an intervention can improve the result, and whether the benefit can be measured. It should also assess safety, integration, data rights, and customer willingness to adopt. Starting with the most fashionable technology or the most complex asset can create an expensive pilot with unclear value. Prioritization should balance strategic importance with the ability to learn and deliver.

Study instruction:
- "Score three candidate use cases on customer pain, data readiness, intervention potential, measurable value, risk, and scale potential."

Key point:
- Choose use cases by customer value and feasibility, not by technology novelty.
- A focused starting point can create evidence for broader transformation.

41. Q: What organizational capabilities must an industrial company develop for fusion strategy?
A: Show answer
Capabilities include domain engineering, software development, data engineering, AI and analytics, cybersecurity, product management, service design, customer success, ecosystem management, and commercial contracting. The company also needs leaders who can coordinate these disciplines around a shared customer outcome. It may develop some capabilities internally and partner for others, but it must retain enough expertise to evaluate quality, manage risk, and protect strategic knowledge. Workforce development is important because engineers, technicians, sales teams, and service personnel need to work with digital tools and data-driven processes. Capability building should be tied to specific use cases rather than a generic digital-skills campaign.

Study instruction:
- "Compare current capabilities with those needed for one fusion offering and create a build, hire, partner, or acquire plan."

Key point:
- Fusion strategy requires a blend of physical, digital, commercial, and ecosystem skills.

42. Q: Why does the CEO need to lead digital transformation in an industrial company?
A: Show answer
Fusion strategy changes priorities, investment, operating models, partnerships, and the company's relationship with customers. If digital work is delegated entirely to a technology function, it may remain disconnected from product strategy, capital allocation, and business-unit incentives. The CEO and senior team must set a shared direction, resolve cross-functional trade-offs, fund the necessary capabilities, and ensure that business leaders own customer outcomes. This does not mean the CEO makes technical decisions. It means executive leadership treats digital integration as a strategic responsibility and creates the conditions for functions to work together.

Study instruction:
- "Identify three decisions that require CEO or executive-team sponsorship because they cross functional boundaries or change the business model."

Key point:
- Digital transformation requires enterprise-level direction and resource alignment.
- Technology leadership is necessary but cannot replace business leadership.

43. Q: How can an industrial company avoid treating AI as an isolated technology experiment?
A: Show answer
The company should connect each AI initiative to a defined business problem, an accountable business owner, a measurable outcome, and a plan for operational integration. It should involve the people who will use or be affected by the system, including engineers, operators, service teams, customers, and risk specialists. Data pipelines, model monitoring, security, training, maintenance, and fallback procedures should be designed before deployment. A collection of disconnected proofs of concept may generate learning but rarely produces a coherent strategy. Portfolio governance should prioritize use cases, share reusable capabilities, retire unsuccessful experiments, and scale those with demonstrated value.

Study instruction:
- "Review the AI pilot portfolio and classify each project as scale, redesign, integrate, pause, or stop based on evidence and strategic fit."

Key point:
- AI experiments become strategy when they connect to operations and customer outcomes.
- Portfolio governance prevents isolated pilots from consuming resources without impact.

44. Q: What role does the customer ecosystem play in a fusion solution?
A: Show answer
A customer ecosystem includes the equipment, software, data providers, service partners, suppliers, operators, and other organizations that contribute to the customer's outcome. A fusion solution must coordinate relevant parts of this ecosystem rather than optimize one product in isolation. The provider needs to understand how information and work move across participants, where decisions are made, and which dependencies could block delivery. Ecosystem design should clarify responsibilities, interfaces, data permissions, service levels, and how value is shared. A strong ecosystem makes the solution more complete, but it also increases governance and integration demands.

Study instruction:
- "Draw the customer's ecosystem for one operational problem and identify the critical participants, handoffs, data exchanges, and accountability gaps."

Key point:
- Customer outcomes often depend on a network of organizations and systems.
- Ecosystem value requires coordination, interoperability, and clear accountability.

45. Q: How can a company build customer trust in data-driven industrial services?
A: Show answer
Trust requires reliable performance, transparent data practices, understandable recommendations, responsive support, and clear accountability when something goes wrong. Customers should know what information is collected, how it is used, who can access it, and whether it is shared or used to improve models. The provider should explain the limits of predictions and avoid presenting uncertain outputs as guarantees. Contracts should define responsibilities and remedies, while operational teams should be able to question or override recommendations where appropriate. Trust grows when the service consistently improves outcomes and the provider behaves as a responsible steward of customer information.

Study instruction:
- "Create a customer trust checklist covering performance evidence, data rights, transparency, support, liability, and complaint handling."

Key point:
- Trust is a condition for access to product-use data and long-term service relationships.
- Reliable outcomes and responsible data practices must reinforce each other.

51. Q: How should a company decide whether to sell a fusion offering as a product, service, system, or solution?
A: Show answer
The choice depends on the scope of the customer problem, the data available, the company's capabilities, and the degree of integration required. If the value is concentrated in one redesigned machine, a fusion product may be appropriate. If ongoing recommendations improve operation, a service may fit. If several assets must work together, a system is more relevant. If the customer's objective requires combining multiple products, services, and partners, a solution may create greater value. The company should not force every opportunity into the most complex model; it should select the smallest offering that solves the problem and can be delivered reliably.

Study instruction:
- "Use the four fusion categories to classify a customer opportunity and explain why the selected model is proportionate to the problem."

Key point:
- The fusion model should match the customer's outcome and integration needs.
- Complexity should be justified by additional value, not by ambition alone.

52. Q: What are common failure modes when industrial companies implement fusion strategies?
A: Show answer
Common failure modes include starting with technology rather than a customer problem, treating AI as a siloed IT project, collecting data without a decision use, underestimating integration work, relying on poor-quality sensors, neglecting cybersecurity, failing to define data rights, and scaling a pilot before it has been validated in representative conditions. Companies may also struggle when business units have conflicting incentives or when the revenue model does not reflect the value delivered. These risks can be reduced through a focused use case, cross-functional ownership, strong data governance, customer involvement, staged testing, and explicit commercial assumptions.

Study instruction:
- "Review a current digital initiative against customer value, data quality, integration, security, ownership, scalability, and monetization."

Key point:
- Fusion initiatives fail when technical activity is disconnected from operational and commercial reality.
- Governance and staged validation reduce avoidable scale-up risk.

- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)

53. Q: How can an industrial company protect against vendor lock-in in a fusion ecosystem?
A: Show answer
Vendor lock-in can occur when data, interfaces, models, or critical services become difficult to move to another provider. The company should define data portability, interface standards, export formats, service continuity, model ownership or licensing, and transition assistance in contracts. It should prefer modular architecture and documented APIs where practical, while recognizing that some specialized capabilities may create legitimate dependencies. The company should also assess concentration risk and maintain contingency plans for critical providers. The goal is not to avoid all long-term partnerships; it is to ensure that the customer can continue operating and evolving the system if a partner relationship changes.

Study instruction:
- "Assess one critical digital supplier for concentration, data portability, interface openness, exit costs, and service-continuity risk."

Key point:
- Ecosystem dependence should be governed through architecture and contract design.
- Portability and continuity reduce the risk of a partner becoming an operational bottleneck.

- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)

54. Q: How should a company measure the customer value created by a fusion solution?
A: Show answer
Customer value should be defined in terms of the outcome the solution is meant to improve, such as yield, throughput, uptime, safety, energy use, maintenance cost, quality, or predictability. The provider should establish a baseline, identify relevant operating conditions, and agree how changes will be measured. It should separate the solution's contribution from external factors such as weather, demand, operator behavior, or changes in input quality. Where possible, compare performance with a suitable control or historical baseline. The customer should be able to understand and verify the result, especially when pricing or contract payments depend on it.

Study instruction:
- "For one solution, define the outcome metric, baseline, attribution method, data source, review period, and customer sign-off process."

Key point:
- Customer value must be defined and measured before it can be monetized.
- Attribution rules are essential when outcomes depend on many external factors.

55. Q: Why should industrial firms think in terms of ecosystems rather than isolated company-to-company partnerships?
A: Show answer
An ecosystem involves a network of participants whose products, services, data, and capabilities combine to create customer value. A single bilateral partnership may solve one integration problem, but a broader ecosystem can support multiple products, customers, developers, and complementary services. This requires common interfaces, governance, standards, trust, and a clear model for value sharing. The manufacturer may need to act as an orchestrator that ensures the customer receives a coherent solution while partners contribute specialized capabilities. Ecosystems can expand reach and innovation, but they also create coordination, security, competition, and accountability challenges.

Study instruction:
- "Map the ecosystem around one customer solution and identify the orchestrator, participants, standards, incentives, and governance gaps."

Key point:
- An ecosystem coordinates multiple contributors around a shared customer outcome.
- Scale depends on trust, interoperability, incentives, and governance.

56. Q: How can industrial companies develop a practical fusion-strategy roadmap?
A: Show answer
A practical roadmap starts with customer discovery and an assessment of the company's data reach and richness. It then selects a focused use case, defines the target outcome, checks data rights and technical feasibility, and builds a cross-functional team. The company should develop a minimum viable offering, validate it in real operating conditions, and measure both customer and commercial results. After the pilot, it can standardize architecture, establish support and security controls, negotiate partner arrangements, and scale to additional customers or use cases. The roadmap should include stop or redesign decisions so that weak pilots do not continue simply because they are technologically interesting.

Study instruction:
- "Build a phased roadmap from discovery through pilot, validation, commercialization, and scale, with explicit decision gates and accountable owners."

Key point:
- A fusion roadmap turns a broad ambition into staged evidence and decisions.
- Customer value, data rights, risk, and economics should be tested before scale.

57. Q: What skills will industrial employees need as products and services become more digital?
A: Show answer
Employees will need a combination of domain expertise and digital fluency. Engineers may work with data and models; technicians may interpret predictive alerts; operators may interact with automated systems; service teams may deliver analytics-based recommendations; and sales staff may discuss customer outcomes and recurring services. Employees also need awareness of data security, AI limitations, and when to escalate unusual behavior. The objective is not to turn every worker into a data scientist. It is to help people use digital tools appropriately in their roles and collaborate across disciplines. Training should be practical and linked to actual workflows.

58. Q: How should a company manage workforce resistance to a fusion strategy?
A: Show answer
Resistance may arise from uncertainty about job security, concern about surveillance, lack of confidence with new tools, fear of losing expertise, or previous technology projects that failed to deliver. Leaders should explain the customer and business problem, involve employees in design, clarify how data will be used, provide training, and create safe ways to raise concerns. The company should identify which tasks will change and how roles may evolve rather than promise that nothing will be different. Adoption should be measured through workflow quality and customer outcomes, not only logins or training attendance. Employees are more likely to support change when they can see its purpose and influence its implementation.

59. Q: What is the role of human-machine collaboration in fusion strategy?
A: Show answer
Human-machine collaboration combines machine capabilities such as sensing, computation, pattern recognition, and speed with human judgment, domain knowledge, creativity, and responsibility. The chapter argues that industrial firms should stop treating human and machine intelligence as separate. In practice, the division of work should depend on the task: machines may detect patterns or generate recommendations, while people interpret context, handle exceptions, approve high-impact actions, and improve the system. The design should make uncertainty visible and provide operators with appropriate control. Effective collaboration increases capability without assuming that automation is always preferable.

Study instruction:
- "For one workflow, divide tasks between people and AI, identify handoffs and exceptions, and specify where human approval is mandatory."

Key point:
- The strongest designs combine complementary human and machine strengths.
- Human oversight should be proportionate to risk and decision consequence.

60. Q: How can industrial AI recommendations be made explainable to operators and customers?
A: Show answer
An explanation should provide the information needed to understand and act on a recommendation, such as the relevant signals, operating context, confidence or uncertainty, likely consequence, and recommended next step. The appropriate level of detail depends on the user and the decision. A technician may need sensor trends and diagnostic evidence, while an executive may need the operational and financial implications. Explanations should not imply that a complex model is fully understood merely because a short rationale is displayed. Users need training to interpret model limits and a route to challenge or escalate recommendations that conflict with direct observations or safety procedures.

Study instruction:
- "Design an explanation screen for one AI recommendation and identify what evidence, uncertainty, context, and action guidance the user needs."

Key point:
- Explainability should support appropriate human action, not create false confidence.
- Different users need different levels of technical and operational explanation.

Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
61. Q: How should a company govern model updates in connected industrial products?
A: Show answer
Model updates can change system behavior and may affect safety, reliability, performance, and customer expectations. The company should define version control, test environments, approval authority, validation criteria, deployment windows, rollback procedures, and post-update monitoring. It should identify whether an update changes the model's intended use or operating limits and whether customers or regulators must be notified. Updates should be tested against representative data and edge cases, with clear records of what changed and why. A remote update should never bypass required safety checks or leave operators without a safe fallback if the new version behaves unexpectedly.

Study instruction:
- "Create a model-update release process covering testing, approval, customer notification, deployment, monitoring, and rollback."

Key point:
- AI model updates are controlled product changes, not routine software housekeeping.
- Safety, traceability, and rollback capability must be built into deployment.

Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework

62. Q: What should an industrial company consider when using generative AI with engineering or operational data?
A: Show answer
Generative AI can help synthesize documents, interpret unstructured information, explore design alternatives, and assist with technical analysis. However, outputs may be incomplete, inaccurate, or inconsistent with physical constraints. Companies should protect confidential engineering data, control access, validate generated designs and instructions, and prevent unreviewed outputs from directly controlling high-risk equipment. The system should be evaluated on the actual task, with representative tests and clear human review responsibilities. Generative AI should complement specialist judgment and established engineering controls, not bypass them. Data provenance and intellectual-property rules should be addressed before sensitive information is submitted to external services.

Study instruction:
- "Choose one engineering use case and define permitted data, model access, output validation, human approval, and prohibited actions."

Key point:
- Generative AI can assist industrial work but does not remove the need for engineering verification.
- Data confidentiality, IP, and safety controls must precede deployment.

63. Q: How can industrial companies use data from machines operating in different environments?
A: Show answer
Machines used across farms, factories, mines, buildings, or transport systems encounter different weather, loads, operators, maintenance histories, and operating practices. Data from varied environments can help a company understand which patterns are general and which are context-specific. Models should account for these differences rather than assume that performance in one setting will transfer automatically to another. The company should check sensor calibration, data completeness, environmental coverage, and potential bias in the installed base. Local validation and monitoring are important when recommendations are applied to new regions, customer types, or equipment configurations.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the environmental factors that could change model performance for one machine and design tests across representative operating conditions."

Key point:
- Broader operating data can improve learning but also introduces context variation.
- Models should be validated in the environments where they will be used.

64. Q: Why does a fusion strategy require a different approach to customer relationships?
A: Show answer
The relationship shifts from a transaction focused on selling and maintaining equipment toward an ongoing partnership concerned with customer performance. The provider may need access to operating data, regular interaction with users, and shared agreement about service outcomes. Customers may also become more dependent on the provider's software, analytics, and ecosystem. This increases the importance of transparency, reliability, responsiveness, data governance, and fair contract terms. Sales teams must understand the customer's operating model and value drivers, while service teams must communicate insights in a way that supports action. Trust becomes a strategic asset because it enables continuous learning and improvement.

Study instruction:

Key point:
- Fusion offerings create ongoing relationships centered on customer outcomes.

65. Q: What is the difference between selling equipment and selling an outcome?
A: Show answer
Selling equipment transfers a physical product to the customer, usually with defined specifications and warranty obligations. Selling an outcome means the provider takes responsibility for, or shares risk in, a result such as uptime, fuel efficiency, throughput, yield, or system availability. Outcome-based offerings can align incentives because the provider benefits when the customer's performance improves. They also require a clear baseline, measurement method, operating assumptions, and allocation of risks outside the provider's control. The company must have sufficient data and operational influence to support the commitment. Outcome selling is not simply a new label for a product sale; it changes service delivery and accountability.

Study instruction:
- "Choose one equipment category and determine whether the provider could credibly contract for an outcome rather than a hardware specification."

Key point:
- Outcome selling shifts attention from product attributes to customer performance.
- The provider needs measurement, control, and risk-allocation capabilities to make the promise credible.

Source links:
- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
66. Q: How should a company choose partners for a fusion strategy?
A: Show answer
Partners should contribute capabilities that are difficult or inefficient to build internally, such as specialist software, sensors, cloud infrastructure, analytics, domain data, or access to customer ecosystems. Selection should consider technical competence, interoperability, security, financial stability, customer reputation, data practices, and willingness to share value. The company should also evaluate strategic dependence and whether the partner's incentives align with customer outcomes. A partner that provides an attractive technology but restricts data portability or customer access may create long-term risk. Due diligence should include technical testing, contractual rights, operational support, and exit arrangements.

Study instruction:
- "Score three potential partners against capability, interoperability, security, data rights, customer fit, economics, and dependency risk."

Key point:
- Partner choice affects both the capability and control of the fusion offering.
- Technical fit must be assessed alongside incentives, rights, and continuity.

- Vijay Govindarajan and Venkat Venkatraman, “Heavy Machinery Meets AI,” Harvard Business Review (2024)
